Induced Fubini-Study metrics on strictly pseudoconvex CR manifolds and zeros of random CR functions Thanks: Hendrik Herrmann and George Marinescu are partially supported by the ANR-DFG project QuaSiDy (Project ID 490843120). George Marinescu is partially supported by the DFG Priority Program 2265 ‘Random Geometric Systems’ (Project-ID 422743078). Chin-Yu Hsiao is partially supported by Taiwan Ministry of Science and Technology projects 108-2115-M-001-012-MY5, 109-2923-M-001-010-MY4. Wei-Chuan Shen is supported by the SFB/TRR 191 ”Symplectic Structures in Geometry, Algebra and Dynamics”, funded by the DFG (Projektnummer 281071066 – TRR 191).
Hendrik Herrmann, Chin-Yu Hsiao, George Marinescu, Wei-Chuan Shen

Abstract
. Let X X be a compact strictly pseudoconvex embeddable Cauchy-Riemann manifold and let T P T_{P} be the Toeplitz operator on X X associated with a first-order pseudodifferential operator P P . In our previous work we established the asymptotic expansion for k k large of the kernel of the operators χ ⁡ ( k − 1 ​ T P ) \chi(k^{-1}T_{P}) , where χ \chi is a smooth cut-off function supported in the positive real line. By using these asymptotics, we show in this paper that X X can be projectively embedded by maps with components of the form χ ⁡ ( k − 1 ​ λ ) ​ f λ \chi(k^{-1}\lambda)f_{\lambda} , where λ \lambda is an eigenvalue of T P T_{P} and f λ f_{\lambda} is a corresponding eigenfunction. We establish the asymptotics of the pull-back of the Fubini-Study metric by these maps and we obtain the distribution of the zero divisors of random Cauchy-Riemann functions. We then establish a version of the Lelong-Poincaré formula for domains with boundary and obtain the distribution of the zero divisors of random holomorphic functions on strictly pseudoconvex domains.



1.
Introduction and Main Results

1.1.

In our previous work
[
26
]
we introduced a semi-classical approach
to the spectral theory of Toeplitz operators of Boutet de Monvel
and Guillemin
[
7
]
and gave several applications to
the CR geometry of strictly pseudoconvex Cauchy-Riemann manifolds.
In this paper we give further applications of the semi-classical
approach: projective embedding, asymptotics of the induced
Fubini-Study metrics and distribution of zeros of
random Cauchy–Riemann and holomorphic functions.

A famous result due to Kodaira
[
36
]
states that
a compact complex manifold carrying a
positive holomorphic line bundle
L
is holomorphically embedded
into the complex projective space by the Kodaira maps
\Phi_{k}

given by the high tensor powers
L^{k}
. A theorem of Tian
[
49
]

shows that the Kodaira maps
\Phi_{k}
are asymptotically isometric
with respect to the Fubini-Study metric and the Kähler forms
given by the curvatures of
L^{k}
as
k\to+\infty
.
These results yield the asymptotic distribution
of zero sets for random holomorphic sections

[
13
,
15
,
16
,
17
,
43
,
4
,
47
]
,
see also
[
3
]
for a survey.

We will establish analogues of these results in the
setting of Cauchy–Riemann manifolds (CR manifolds for short).
A classical result due to Boutet de Monvel
[
10
]

states that any compact strictly pseudoconvex CR manifold
of dimension greater or equal than five can be CR embedded
into the complex euclidean space.
In dimension three there are non-embeddable
compact strictly pseudoconvex CR manifolds
(see e. g. Burns
[
11
]
, where the boundary
of the non-fillable example of strictly pseudoconcave manifold
by Grauert
[
20
]
, Andreotti-Siu
[
1
]
and Rossi
[
46
]

is shown to be non-embeddable). Lempert
[
40
]

showed the embeddabillity in any dimension
by assuming the existence of a CR Reeb vector field.
The embeddability of Sasakian manifolds was established in
[
42
]
.
Using the semiclassical form introduced in
[
26
]

of the spectral theory of Toeplitz operators
[
7
]
,
we obtained in
[
26
]
embedding results for
CR manifolds and we related their CR Reeb dynamics
to those of spheres in the complex euclidean space.

In this work we study embeddings of compact strictly pseudoconvex CR
manifolds into the complex projective space linking the Fubini-Study
metric with CR geometric data of the underlying manifold.
Then, in analogy to the complex case, we obtain results on
the asymptotic distributions of zero sets for random CR functions
which turns out to be directly related to the study of zero sets for
random holomorphic functions on strictly pseudoconvex domains.
In the following, we explain the precise set-up and formulate the main results.

We consider an orientable
compact strictly pseudoconvex Cauchy–Riemann (CR for short) manifold
(X,T^{1,0}X)

of dimension
2n+1
,
n\geq 1
, with volume form
dV

such that the Kohn Laplacian on
X
has closed range in

L^{2}(X)
, where
L^{2}(X)
denotes the space of square integrable functions on
X
.
Denote by
\Pi\colon L^{2}(X)\to L^{2}(X)
the Szegő-projector and by
H_{b}^{0}(X)=\Pi L^{2}(X)
the space of
L^{2}
CR functions. Choose a contact form
\xi
on
X
, that is,
\xi\in\Omega^{1}(X)
is a smooth real non-vanishing one form such that
\ker\xi=\Re(T^{1,0}X)
. We can and we will choose
\xi
in a way that the Levi form associated to
\xi
is positive definite (see Definition
2.3
). Let
P\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{cl}}(X)
be a first order formally self-adjoint classical pseudodifferential operator which is transversally elliptic meaning that the principal symbol
\sigma_{P}
of
P
restricted to the symplectic cone

\Sigma:=\{(x,t\xi(x)):x\in X,~t>0\}\subset T^{*}X
is everywhere positive. One can then define the so called characteristic contact form
\alpha_{P}
with respect to
P
which is given by

(1.1.1)

\displaystyle\alpha_{P}:=\frac{\xi}{\sigma_{P}(\xi)}.

It turns out that
\alpha_{P}
does not depend on the choice of
\xi
.
We consider the Toeplitz operator
T_{P}:=\Pi P\Pi:\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(X)\to\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(X)

associated to
P
. One has that
T_{P}
has a self-adjoint
L^{2}
-extension.
More precisely,
T_{P}:\Dom T_{P}\subset L^{2}(X)\to L^{2}(X)

is self-adjoint, with
\Dom T_{P}=\{u\in L^{2}(X):\Pi P\Pi u\in L^{2}(X)\}
, where
\Pi P\Pi u
is defined in the sense of distributions.
Since
[
7
]
*Proposition 2.14 applies here,
the spectrum of
T_{P}:\operatorname{Dom}T_{P}\to L^{2}(X)
consists only of
a discrete set of real eigenvalues, which is bounded from below and has only
+\infty

as accumulation point. Furthermore, given
\lambda\neq 0
we find that
\ker(T_{P}-\lambda\operatorname{Id})
is a finite dimensional subspace of
L^{2}(X)
contained in
H_{b}^{0}(X)\cap\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(X)
. So let
0<\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}\leq\ldots
be the positive eigenvalues of
T_{P}
counting multiplicity and let
\{f_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subset H_{b}^{0}(X)
be a respective orthonormal system of eigenvectors. In particular, we have
T_{P}f_{j}=\lambda_{j}f_{j}
,
(f_{j},f_{j})=1
and
(f_{j},f_{\ell})=0
for all
j,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,
j\neq\ell
. We consider a function
\chi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
with

\text{supp}\chi\subset(0,+\infty)
and define for
k>0
the function

(1.1.2)

\chi_{k}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C}\,,\quad\chi_{k}(t)=\chi\left(k^{-1}t\right).

Then
\chi_{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
,
\text{supp}\chi_{k}=k\text{supp}\chi\subset(0,+\infty)
.
In
[
26
]
the functional calculus
\chi_{k}(T_{P})
was studied and it was shown that its Schwartz kernel given by

(1.1.3)

\displaystyle\chi_{k}(T_{P})(x,y)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\chi_{k}(\lambda_{j})f_{j}(x)\overline{f_{j}(y)}

has an asymptotic expansion for
k\to+\infty
(see Theorem
2.10
for more details). Moreover, first applications were obtained with respect to weighted spectral measures and almost spherical embeddings. In this paper we study further applications concerning projective embeddings and equidistribution results for CR-currents and zeros of random holomorphic functions. We assume
\operatorname{supp}(\chi)\subset(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})
for some
0<\delta_{1}<\delta_{2}
,
\chi\not\equiv 0
and put
N_{k}=\#\{j\mid\lambda_{j}\leq\delta_{2}k\}
. We consider the map

(1.1.4)

\displaystyle F_{k}\colon X\to\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}},\,\,\,F_{k}(x)=(\chi_{k}(\lambda_{1})f_{1},\ldots,\chi_{k}(\lambda_{N_{k}})f_{N_{k}}).

Furthermore, given a non-negative function
\eta\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}_{+},[0,\infty))
,
\eta\not\equiv 0
,
\mathbb{R}_{+}:=(0,\infty)
, and
j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
we define

(1.1.5)

\displaystyle\tau_{j}(\eta)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}t^{n+j}\eta(t)dt.

We note that
t\mapsto\tau_{0}(\eta)^{-1}t^{n}\eta(t)
defines a probability distribution on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that its momenta are given by
\tau_{j}(\eta)/\tau_{0}(\eta)
for
j\in\mathbb{N}
. In particular, we put

(1.1.6)

\displaystyle\operatorname{mv}(\eta):=\frac{\tau_{1}(\eta)}{\tau_{0}(\eta)},\,\,\,\,\operatorname{var}(\eta):=\frac{\tau_{2}(\eta)}{\tau_{0}(\eta)}-(\operatorname{mv}(\eta))^{2}

to denote its mean value and variance respectively.
We now state our first main result.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
(X,T^{1,0}X)
be an orientable
compact strictly pseudoconvex Cauchy–Riemann manifold
of dimension
2n+1
,
n\geq 1
,
such that the Kohn Laplacian on
X
has closed range in

L^{2}(X)
. Let
\xi
be a contact form on
X
such that its associated Levi form

\mathcal{L}
is positive definite. Consider a formally self-adjoint first order pseudodifferential operator

P\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{cl}}(X)
with

\sigma_{P}(\xi)>0
on
X
and denote by
T_{P}
its Toelplitz operator. Let
0<\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}\leq\ldots
be the positive eigenvalues of
T_{P}
counting multiplicity and let
\{f_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subset H_{b}^{0}(X)\cap\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(X)
be a respective orthonormal system of eigenvectors.
Given
k>0
,
0<\delta_{1}<\delta_{2}
and
\chi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}((\delta_{1},\delta_{2}))
,
\chi\not\equiv 0
, consider the the map

F_{k}\colon X\to\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}},\,\,\,F_{k}(x)=(\chi_{k}(\lambda_{1})f_{1},\ldots,\chi_{k}(\lambda_{N_{k}})f_{N_{k}}),

where
N_{k}=\#\{j\mid\lambda_{j}\leq\delta_{2}k\}
and
\chi_{k}(\lambda):=\chi\left(k^{-1}\lambda\right)
.
There exists
k_{0}>0
such that the map

[F_{k}]\colon X\to\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{N_{k}-1},\,\,\,\,[F_{k}](x):=[F_{k}(x)],

is a well defined CR embedding for all
k\geq k_{0}
. Furthermore,
[F_{k}]^{*}ds^{2}_{FS}
has an asymptotic expansion for
k\to+\infty
in
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}
-topology. Here
ds^{2}_{FS}
denotes the Hermitian line element with respect to the Fubini-Study metric. More precisely, there exist smooth sesquilinear forms
r_{j}\colon\mathbb{C}TX\times\mathbb{C}TX\to\mathbb{C}
,
j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, such that for any
M,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
there is a constant
C_{M,\ell}>0
with

(1.1.7)

\displaystyle\left\|[F_{k}]^{*}ds^{2}_{FS}-\sum_{j=0}^{M}k^{2-j}r_{j}\right\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\ell}(X)}\leq C_{M,\ell}k^{-M+1},\,\,\,\text{ for all }k\geq k_{0}.

In addition, we have
r_{0}=\operatorname{var}(|\chi|^{2})\alpha_{P}\otimes\alpha_{P}
and
r_{1}(Z,W)=-i\operatorname{mv}(|\chi|^{2})d\alpha_{P}(Z,\overline{W})
for all
Z,W\in T_{x}^{1,0}X
,
x\in X
. Here
\alpha_{P}
denotes the characteristic contact form with respect to
P
as in (
1.1.1
) and
\operatorname{mv}(|\chi|^{2})
,
\operatorname{var}(|\chi|^{2})
are given by (
1.1.6
).

Let
g_{FS}:=\Re(ds^{2}_{FS})
and
\omega_{FS}:=-\Im(ds^{2}_{FS})
denote the Riemannian metric and the Kähler form on
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{N}
induced by the Fubini-Study metric. The asymptotic expansion of
[F_{k}]^{*}ds^{2}_{FS}
in Theorem
1.1
then leads to results concerning the pullback of
g_{FS}
and
\omega_{FS}
on
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{N_{k}}
under
[F_{k}]
. See for example Lemma
3.6
together with Lemma
3.3
for a refined statement on the pullback of
g_{FS}
under
[F_{k}]
.

Remark 1.2
.

We note that by Boutet de Monvel’s proof
[
9
,
10
]
and Kohn’s argument
[
38
]
, for any compact strictly pseudoconvex
CR manifold
X
, the closed range condition is equivalent to the CR embeddability of
X
into the complex euclidean space. We also recall that the closed range condition holds for any compact strictly pseudoconvex CR manifold when
n\geq 2
(cf.
[
10
]
), or when
n=1
and
X
admits a transversal CR
\mathbb{R}
-action (cf.
[
40
,
42
]
).

Remark 1.3
.

We note that the statement that the map
[F_{k}]
in Theorem
1.1
is an embedding when
k>0
is large enough cannot be directly obtained from the almost spherical embedding result in
[
26
]
. Furthermore, with respect to the results on equidistribution below it was necessary to understand how fast
[F_{k}]
separates points when
k
becomes large. So the method in the proof of Theorem
1.1
differs from the strategy used in
[
26
]
to obtain the almost spherical embedding result there. See Remark
3.9
for further explanations.

An important class of strictly pseudoconvex CR manifolds are Sasakian manifolds. A strictly pseudoconvex CR manifold
(X,T^{1,0}X)
together with a real transversal CR vector field
\mathcal{T}
is called a Sasakian manifold. Recall that a real vector field is transversal if it is transversal to
\Re(T^{1,0}X)
and CR if its flow preserves
T^{1,0}X
. From Theorem
1.1
we can deduce the following embedding result for Sasakian manifolds.

Corollary 1.4
.

Let
(X,T^{1,0}X)
be a compact Sasakian manifold with transversal CR vector field
\mathcal{T}
and consider the transversal CR
\mathbb{R}
-action on
X
induced by the flow of
\mathcal{T}
. Let
dV
be an
\mathbb{R}
-invariant volume form on
X
and choose a contact form
\xi
for
(X,T^{1,0}X)
such that its associated Levi form
\mathcal{L}
is positive definite. Assume that the contraction of
\xi
with
\mathcal{T}
is positive and put
P:=-i\mathcal{T}
.
Then
(X,T^{1,0}X)
,
P
,
dV
satisfy the assumptions
in Theorem
1.1
.
Hence, given
\chi\in\mathscr{C}_{c}^{\infty}((\delta_{1},\delta_{2}))
,
\chi\not\equiv 0
,
for some
0<\delta_{1}<\delta_{2}
there exists
k_{0}>0
such that
the associated map
[F_{k}]:X\to\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{N_{k}-1}

in Theorem
1.1

defines a CR embedding of
X
into
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{N_{k}-1}

for all
k\geq k_{0}
satisfying the asymptotics
(
1.1.7
).
Furthermore, the map
[F_{k}]
is equivariant with respect to the
CR
\mathbb{R}
-action on
X
and the weighted holomorphic

\mathbb{R}
-action on
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{N_{k}-1}
given by

\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{N_{k}-1}\ni(t,[z_{1},\ldots,z_{N_{k}}])\mapsto[e^{i\lambda_{1}t}z_{1},\ldots,e^{i\lambda_{N_{k}}t}z_{N_{k}}]\in\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{N_{k}-1}.

with
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots
as in Theorem
1.1
.

Remark 1.5
.

Equivariant projective CR embeddings for Sasakian manifolds and related objects were studied in
[
25
]
and
[
30
]
before. It turns out (see
[
25
, Remark 1.6]
) that the existence of an equivariant projective CR embedding can be directly deduced from equivariant euclidean CR embedding results as in
[
40
,
42
,
45
,
50
]
. Hence, the non-trivial statement in Corollary
1.4
is that the projectivization
[F_{k}]
of the map
F_{k}:X\to\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}}
becomes an equivariant CR embedding for
k
large enough and that
[F_{k}]^{*}ds_{\operatorname{FS}}
admits an asymptotic expansion for
k\to\infty
.
Furthermore, we would like to point out that the related results in
[
25
]
and
[
30
]
rely on the study of CR sections of positive CR line bundles. Using Theorem
1.1
we obtain the equivariant CR embedding without considering positive CR line bundles.

The projective embeddability of
X
as in
Theorem
1.1

is related to the study of equilibrium measures in CR geometry
and asymptotic distribution of zero sets for random holomorphic functions.
Let
f\in H_{b}^{0}(X)\cap\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(X)
be a smooth CR function
such that zero is a regular value of the map
f\colon X\to\mathbb{C}
.
It turns out that under the assumptions on
f
we
have that
df/f
is integrable on
X
and the zero divisor of
f

is given by
\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right]=(2\pi i)^{-1}d(df/f)
in the sense of distributions,
that is,

(1.1.8)

\displaystyle\left(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right],\psi\right)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{X}\frac{df}{f}\wedge d\psi

for all
\psi\in\Omega^{2n-1}(X)
(cf.
[
24
]
,
see also Theorem
4.21
and
Remark
4.24
).
We consider
\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right]
as distribution valued random variable.
For each
k>0
we consider the space

A_{k}\subset H_{b}^{0}(X)\cap\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(X)

given by the linear span of the elements

f_{1},\ldots,f_{N_{k}}
with
f_{j},N_{k}
as in Theorem
1.1
.
Given
\chi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
as in Theorem
1.1

we consider the probability measure
\mu_{k}
on

A_{k}
induced by the standard complex Gaussian measure

\mu^{G}
on
\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}}
and the map

(1.1.9)

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}}\ni a\mapsto\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}a_{j}\chi(k^{-1}\lambda_{j})f_{j}\in A_{k}.

In other words, we consider random CR functions of the form

f=a_{1}\chi(k^{-1}\lambda_{1})f_{1}+\ldots+a_{N_{k}}\chi(k^{-1}\lambda_{N_{k}})f_{N_{k}},

where
a_{j}
are i.i.d standard complex Gaussian random variables, and study
\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right]
, seen as a distribution valued random variable, especially in the case when
k
becomes large. Therefore, given
k>0
we consider the expectation value
\mathbb{E}_{k}(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right])
of
\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right]
, which is - in case of existence - defined by

(1.1.10)

\displaystyle\left(\mathbb{E}_{k}\left(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right]\right),\psi\right):=\int_{A_{k}}\left(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right],\psi\right)d\mu_{k}(f),\,\,\,\,\psi\in\Omega^{2n-1}(X).

Theorem 1.6
.

Under the same assumptions as in Theorem
1.1
and with the notations above there exists
k_{0}>0
such that for each
k\geq k_{0}
we have that
\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right]
is a well-defined current for all
f
outside a subset in
A_{k}
of
\mu_{k}
-measure zero and the expectation value
\mathbb{E}_{k}(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right])
given by (
1.1.10
) exists
with
\mathbb{E}_{k}\left(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right]\right)=d\beta_{k}
where
\beta_{k}
is the smooth one form given by

\beta_{k}(x)=\frac{d_{x}\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,y)|_{x=y}}{2\pi i\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,x)}

for all
x\in X
and
k\geq k_{0}
. Here
\eta_{k}(T_{P})
is given by (
1.1.3
) with
\eta=|\chi|^{2}
and
\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right]
denotes the zero divisor of
f
. Furthermore, the probability space
(A_{k},\mu_{k})
is given by (
1.1.9
).

From the results in
[
26
]
(see Lemma
5.7
in this paper) we obtain as a direct consequence that

k^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{k}\left(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right]\right)=\frac{\text{mv}(|\chi|^{2})}{2\pi}d\alpha_{P}+O(k^{-1})\,\,\text{ in }\mathscr{C}^{\infty}\text{-topology}

which leads to a convergence result for the normalized expected measures
k^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{k}\left(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right]\right)
weakly in the sense of distributions when
k
goes to
+\infty
. It turns out that this behavior holds in an even stronger sense. Therefore, consider
k\in\mathbb{N}
and put

A_{\infty}=\Pi_{k=1}^{\infty}A_{k}
,
d\mu_{\infty}=\Pi_{k=1}^{\infty}d\mu_{k}
. We have the following.

Theorem 1.7
.

Under the same assumptions as in Theorem
1.1
, with the notations above and
k_{0}>0
as in Theorem
1.6
we have for
\mu_{\infty}
-almost every
f=(f_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in A_{\infty}
that
(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f_{k}\right)\right],\psi)
is well defined for all
\psi\in\Omega^{2n-1}(X)
and
k\geq k_{0}
. Furthermore, given
\psi\in\Omega^{2n-1}(X)
, we have for
\mu_{\infty}
-almost every
f=(f_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in A_{\infty}
that

(1.1.11)

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\left(k^{-1}\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f_{k}\right)\right],\psi\right)=\frac{\operatorname{mv}(|\chi|^{2})}{2\pi}\int_{X}d\alpha_{P}\wedge\psi.

Remark 1.8
.

One can further prove the following: The probability that for
f_{k}\in A_{k}
the term
\left(k^{-1}\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f_{k}\right)\right],\psi\right)
differs from the right-hand side of (
1.1.11
) more than
1/\sqrt{k}
is an
O(1/\sqrt{k})
uniformly in
\psi\in\Omega^{2n-1}(X)
with
\|\psi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{1}(X,\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}^{2n-1}T^{*}X)}\leq 1
. See Corollary
5.15
for more details.

Example 1.9
.

Let
L\to M
be a positive holomorphic line bundle with hermitian metric
h
over a complex compact manifold
M
of complex dimension
n\geq 1
. It turns out that the circle bundle
X:=\{v\in L^{*}\mid|v|_{h^{*}}=1\}
, where
L^{*}
denotes the dual of
L
and
h^{*}
the Hermitian metric induced by
h
, is a compact strictly pseudoconvex CR manifold satisfying the assumptions in Theorem
1.1
. Furthermore, the fiberwise
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action on
L^{*}
induces a transversal CR
S^{1}
-action on
X
with infinitesimal generator denoted by
\mathcal{T}
. It follows that
\mathcal{T}
defines a Reeb vector field on
X
with respect to some contact form
\xi
which is related to the Chern connection for
(L,h)\to M
. In particular, we have that the pullback of curvature of
h
is given to
X
is given by
d\xi
(up to some constant factor). For any
m\in\mathbb{Z}
put

H^{0}_{b,m}(X):=\{f\in H^{0}_{b}(X)\cap\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(X)\mid\mathcal{T}f=imf\}.

Choosing the Reeb volume form
\xi\wedge(d\xi)^{n}
on
X
and

P=-i\mathcal{T}
we find that the corresponding Toeplitz operator
T_{P}

satisfies the assumptions in Theorem
1.7
with

\sigma_{P}(\xi)=1
, that is,
\alpha_{P}=\xi
. Since
\mathcal{T}
is a CR vector field we obtain

T_{P}=-i\mathcal{T}
on
H^{0}_{b}(X)\cap\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(X)
.
Then, from Fourier decomposition, we find that the set of positive eigenvalues of

T_{P}
is given by
\{m\in\mathbb{N}\mid H^{0}_{b,m}(X)\neq\{0\}\}
with corresponding eigenspaces given by
H^{0}_{b,m}(X)
.
Choosing
\chi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}((0,1))
,

\chi\not\equiv 0
, Theorem
1.7
leads
to a statement on the zero sets for random CR functions
f_{k}
of the form

f_{k}=\sum_{m=1}^{k}\chi_{k}(m)u_{m}

when
k
becomes large. Here,
u_{m}\in H^{0}_{b,m}(X)

are standard Gaussian random functions in
H^{0}_{b,m}(X)

where the Gaussian measure is induced by the
L^{2}
-inner product for functions.

Let
H^{0}(M,L^{m})
denote the space of holomorphic section
on
M
with values in the
m
-th tensor power of
L
.
Then
H^{0}_{b,m}(X)
can be identified with
H^{0}(M,L^{m})

for any
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. The equidistribution results

[
13
,
15
,
16
,
17
,
43
,
4
,
47
]

for the zero sets
of holomorphic sections in
H^{0}(M,L^{m})
,
see also
[
3
]
for a survey, lead to equidistribution results for
CR functions in
H^{0}_{b,m}(X)
when
m
becomes large.
In particular, it turns out that the zero sets of functions in

H^{0}_{b,m}(X)
distribute in accordance with

d\xi=d\alpha_{P}
up to some constant factor when
m
goes to
+\infty
.
Hence, there is a relation between
Theorem
1.7
and
classical results on the asymptotic distribution for zero sets
of holomorphic sections in this specific set-up.

Replacing
M
by a compact complex cyclic orbifold in the set-up above, equidistribution results for CR functions in the direct sum
\bigoplus_{j=1}^{d}H^{0}_{b,m_{j}}(X)
for some fixed
d
depending on the orbifold structure, when
m_{j}
,
1\leq j\leq d
, become large in a way related to the orbifold singularities of
M
, were obtained by Hsiao-Shao
[
33
]
. Also here, the zero sets of those functions distribute asymptotically in accordance with
d\xi=d\alpha_{P}
up to some factor.

Standard examples for CR manifolds are boundaries of smoothly bounded domains in complex manifolds. Therefore, Theorem
1.7
should be considered in the context of domains with boundary.

Let
Y
be an
(n+1)
-dimensional complex Hermitian manifold,
n\geq 1
. Consider a relatively compact strictly pseudoconvex domain
D\subset\subset Y
with smooth boundary
X:=bD
. Putting
T^{1,0}X:=\mathbb{C}TX\cap T^{1,0}Y
, where
T^{1,0}Y
denotes the complex structure on
Y
, we find that
(X,T^{1,0}X)
is a compact strictly pseudoconvex CR manifold which is CR embeddable into the complex euclidean space (see
[
31
]
).
It follows that
X
satisfies the assumptions of Theorem
1.1
(see Remark
1.2
).
From results due to Grauert
[
19
]
and Kohn–Rossi
[
37
]
, we have that
X
is connected and that any smooth CR function
f
on
X
extends holomorphically to
D
. In particular, there exists a uniquely determined function
\mathcal{F}\in\mathcal{O}(D)\cap\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\overline{D})
such that
f=\mathcal{F}
on
X
. Hence we have a linear mapping
L\colon H_{b}^{0}(X)\cap\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(X)\to\mathcal{O}(D)\cap\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\overline{D})
given by
L(f)=\mathcal{F}
. Assume that
P,T_{P},\xi
are given as in Theorem
1.1
.
Let
0<\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}\leq\ldots
denote the the positive eigenvalues of
T_{P}
and denote by
\{f_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subset H_{b}^{0}(X)\cap\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(X)
a respective orthonormal system of eigenfunctions.
Given a function
\eta\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}\left(\mathbb{R}_{+},[0,\infty)\right)

we have that the integral kernel of
\eta_{k}(T_{P})
can be written as
\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,y)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\eta_{k}(\lambda_{j})f_{j}(x)\overline{f_{j}(y)}
with
\eta_{k}(t)=\eta(k^{-1}t)
for
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
k>0
. We define

(1.1.12)

\displaystyle B^{\eta}_{k}\colon\overline{D}\to\mathbb{R},\,\,\,\,\,B^{\eta}_{k}(z)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\eta_{k}(\lambda_{j})L(f_{j})(z)\overline{L(f_{j})(z)}.

Then
B^{\eta}_{k}
is smooth and for any
c>0
we have that
k^{-1}\partial\overline{\partial}\log(c+B^{\eta}_{k})
defines a non negative
(1,1)
-form on
D
which is smooth up to the boundary. Denote by
\Omega^{n,n}(\overline{D}):=\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\overline{D},\Lambda^{n,n}\mathbb{C}T^{*}Y)
the space of
(n,n)
-forms on
D
which are smooth up to the boundary. We have the following result for
B^{\eta}_{k}
.

Theorem 1.10
.

Let
Y
be an
(n+1)
-dimensional connected complex Hermitian manifold,
n\geq 1
. Consider a relatively compact strictly pseudoconvex domain
D\subset\subset Y
with smooth boundary
X:=bD
. Let
\xi
be a contact form for
(X,T^{1,0}X)
where
T^{1,0}X=\mathbb{C}TX\cap T^{1,0}Y
such that its induced Levi form is positive definite. Consider a Toeplitz operator
T_{P}=\Pi P\Pi
on
X
as in the set-up of Theorem
1.1
. Choose
\eta\in\mathscr{C}_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+},[0,\infty))
,
\eta\not\equiv 0
. With the notations above, for all
\psi\in\Omega^{n,n}(\overline{D})
, we have

(1.1.13)

\lim_{k\to\infty}\int_{D}k^{-1}i\partial\overline{\partial}\log(1+B^{\eta}_{k})\wedge\psi=\operatorname{mv}(\eta)\int_{bD}\alpha_{P}\wedge\iota^{*}\psi,

Here,
\iota\colon bD\to\overline{D}
denotes the inclusion map and
\alpha_{P}
,
\operatorname{mv}(\eta)
are given by (
1.1.1
) and (
1.1.6
). More precisely, there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\left|\int_{D}k^{-1}i\partial\overline{\partial}\log(1+B^{\eta}_{k})\wedge\psi-\operatorname{mv}(\eta)\int_{bD}\alpha_{P}\wedge\iota^{*}\psi\right|\leq Ck^{-1}(\log(k)+1)\|\psi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{2}(\overline{D},\Lambda^{n,n}\mathbb{C}T^{*}Y)}

for all
k\geq 1
and
\psi\in\Omega^{n,n}(\overline{D})
.

Theorem
1.10
is interesting in the context of zero sets for random holomorphic functions as follows. Let
\chi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
,
\chi\not\equiv 0
, be a smooth function with
\text{supp}(\chi)\subset(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})
for some
0<\delta_{1}<\delta_{2}
. Put
N_{k}=\#\{j\in\mathbb{N}\colon\lambda_{j}\leq\delta_{2}k\}
.
Given i.i.d. standard complex Gaussian random variables
a_{0},a_{1},\ldots,a_{N_{k}}
, we consider random holomorphic functions on
D
of the form

(1.1.14)

u_{k}=a_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}a_{j}\chi(k^{-1}\lambda_{j})L(f_{j}).

We are especially interested in the behavior of the zeros of
u_{k}
when
k
becomes large. We say that
u_{k}
is regular if for any
p\in bD
with
u_{k}(p)=0
we have
(\partial u_{k})_{p}\neq 0
(cf. Definition
4.1
). For regular
u_{k}
we have that the zero divisor is given by
\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(u_{k}\right)\right]=(\pi)^{-1}i\partial\overline{\partial}\log|u_{k}|
in distributions that is

\displaystyle\left(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(u_{k}\right)\right],\psi\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{\pi}\left(-\int_{bD}\iota^{*}(\frac{1}{2u_{k}}\partial u_{k}\wedge\psi)-\int_{bD}\iota^{*}(\log(|u_{k}|)\wedge\overline{\partial}\psi)+\int_{D}\log(|u_{k}|)\wedge\partial\overline{\partial}\psi\right)

for all
\psi\in\Omega^{n,n}(\overline{D})
. Given
\psi
with compact support in
D

this formula becomes the well known Lelong-Poincaré formula (see
[
39
]
).
For the general case we refer to Theorem
4.14
.
In order to state our result we need to introduce some probability spaces first.
For
k>0
put
A_{k}=\text{span}\left(\{1\}\cup\{L(f_{j})\mid\lambda_{j}\leq\delta_{2}k\}\right)\subset\mathcal{O}(D)\cap\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\overline{D})
.
On
A_{k}
we consider the probability measure
\mu_{k}
induced
by the standard complex Gaussian measure
\mu^{G}
on
\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}
and the map

(1.1.15)

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}\ni a\mapsto a_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}a_{j}\chi_{k}(\lambda_{j})L(f_{j})\in A_{k}.

We use the same notation
(A_{k},\mu_{k})
for the probability spaces as before
since the construction just differs by the term
a_{0}
which is introduced to avoid
simultaneous vanishing of the
u_{k}
’s away from the boundary
bD
.
Similar as in the CR set-up above, we study
\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right]

as a distribution valued random variable on
A_{k}
especially when
k
becomes large.
Given
k>0
we consider the expectation value
\mathbb{E}_{k}(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right])
of
\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right]
,
which is - in case of existence - defined by

(1.1.16)

\displaystyle\left(\mathbb{E}_{k}\left(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right]\right),\psi\right):=\int_{A_{k}}\left(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right],\psi\right)d\mu_{k}(f),\,\,\,\,\psi\in\Omega^{n,n}(\overline{D}).

Recall that
B_{k}^{|\chi|^{2}}(z)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}|\chi_{k}(\lambda_{j})|^{2}|L(f_{j})(z)|^{2}
,
z\in\overline{D}
.

Theorem 1.11
.

Under the same assumptions as in Theorem
1.10

and with the notations above we have
for each
k>0
that
\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right]
is a well-defined current for all
f
outside a subset in

A_{k}
of
\mu_{k}
-measure zero and the expectation value
\mathbb{E}_{k}\left(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right]\right)

defined by (
1.1.16
) exists with

\mathbb{E}_{k}\left(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right]\right)=\frac{i}{2\pi}\partial\overline{\partial}\log(1+B^{|\chi|^{2}}_{k}).

Here,
\chi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
,
\chi\not\equiv 0
,
and the respective probability space
(A_{k},\mu_{k})
is given by (
1.1.15
).

As a direct consequence of Theorem
1.10
and
Theorem
1.11
we obtain

\lim_{k\to\infty}\left(k^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{k}\left(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right]\right),\psi\right)=\frac{\operatorname{mv}(|\chi|^{2})}{2\pi}\int_{bD}\alpha_{P}\wedge\iota^{*}\psi

for all
\psi\in\Omega^{n,n}(\overline{D})
. This shows that in average the most of the zeros
of the random holomorphic functions
u_{k}
in (
1.1.14
) concentrate near
the boundary when
k
becomes large.
See also
[
17
, Example 2.10]
for the concentration of zeros
of random holomorphic
(n,0)
-forms near the boundary.

This phenomenon even holds in a stronger sense as we will explain now.
Consider
k\in\mathbb{N}
and put
A_{\infty}=\Pi_{k=1}^{\infty}A_{k}
,

d\mu_{\infty}=\Pi_{k=1}^{\infty}d\mu_{k}
. We have the following.

Theorem 1.12
.

Under the same assumptions as in
Theorem
1.10

and with the notations above we have for
\mu_{\infty}
-almost every

u=(u_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in A_{\infty}
that
(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(u_{k}\right)\right],\psi)

is well defined for all
\psi\in\Omega^{n,n}(\overline{D})
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Furthermore, given
\psi\in\Omega^{n,n}(\overline{D})
, we have for
\mu_{\infty}
-almost every
u=(u_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in A_{\infty}
that

(1.1.17)

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\left(k^{-1}\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(u_{k}\right)\right],\psi\right)=\frac{\operatorname{mv}(|\chi|^{2})}{2\pi}\int_{bD}\alpha_{P}\wedge\iota^{*}\psi.

Roughly speaking, it follows from Theorem
1.12

that the zero set of a random holomorphic function
u_{k}
in (
1.1.14
)
accumulate at the boundary as
k\to+\infty
. Since
\int_{bD}\alpha_{P}\wedge\iota^{*}\psi=0
for all
\psi\in\Omega^{n,n}(\overline{D})
with

\alpha_{P}\wedge\iota^{*}\psi=0
we conclude in addition that the zero set of
u_{k}

converges tangentially to the boundary when
k
becomes large. However, it turns out (see Lemma
5.2
) that the intersection of the zero set of
u_{k}
and the boundary is transversal almost surely for
k>0
.
For other results on equidistribution for strictly pseudoconvex domains
see
[
33
]
. We note that a similar statement
as in Remark
1.8
also holds with respect to
Theorem
1.12
.
See Theorem
7.11
for more details.

Example 1.13
.

Let
D=\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\mid|z|<1\}
be the unit ball in
\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
,
n\geq 1
.
Then
X:=bD
is the unit sphere and hence a compact strictly pseudoconvex CR manifold.
It follows that
D
satisfies the assumptions in Theorem
1.12
.
We find that
\xi:=\iota^{*}\omega_{0}
defines a contact form on
X
where

\omega_{0}=\frac{1}{2i}\sum_{j=1}^{n+1}\overline{z}_{j}dz_{j}-z_{j}d\overline{z}_{j},\,\,\,z\in\mathbb{C}^{n+1}

and
\iota\colon X\to\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
denotes the inclusion map. Then the vector field

z\mapsto i\sum_{j=1}^{n+1}z_{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{j}}-\overline{z}_{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{z}_{j}},\,\,\,z\in\mathbb{C}^{n+1}

restricts to the respective Reeb vector field on
X
denoted by
\mathcal{T}
.
Putting
P:=-i\mathcal{T}
and choosing the Reeb volume form
\xi\wedge(d\xi)^{n}

on
X
we find that
T_{P}=\Pi P\Pi
satisfies the assumptions in Theorem
1.1

with
\sigma_{P}(\xi)=1
, that is,
\alpha_{P}=\xi
. It turns out that the set of eigenvalues
T_{P}
is given by
\mathbb{N}_{0}
. More precisely, for any
d\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
we have that the space of CR eigenfunctions of
T_{P}
for the eigenvalue
d
is given by restrictions of homogeneous polynomials of degree
d
to
X
. Choosing a cut-off function
\chi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}((0,1))
,
\chi\not\equiv 0
, Theorem
1.12
leads to a result on the the asymptotic distribution on
\overline{D}
for zero sets of weighted sums (weighted with respect to
\chi
) of (orthogonal) homogeneous Gaussian random polynomials with degree less or equal than
d
when
d
becomes large.

The asymptotic distribution on
\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
,
n\geq 1
, of zero sets for orthogonal Gaussian random polynomials with degree less or equal than
d
when
d
becomes large was studied by Bloom-Shiffman
[
5
]
. From their results (see also
[
2
]
) it follows that those zero sets distribute asymptotically in accordance to some equilibrium current
s_{\text{eq}}
under fairly general assumptions. With respect to the set-up considered here it is well-known (see
[
5
]
) that
s_{\text{eq}}
is given by
\frac{i}{\pi}\partial\overline{\partial}V
with
V(z)=\max\{\log|z|,0\}
,
z\in\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
, in the sense of distributions.
In context of Theorem
1.12

this situation can be expressed by choosing the cut-off function
\chi

to be the characteristic function of the interval
[0,1]\subset\mathbb{R}
.
Theorem
1.12
cannot be applied in this situation since
\chi
is not smooth.
However, a direct calculation shows that

\operatorname{mv}(|\chi|^{2})=\frac{n+1}{n+2}
for this choice of
\chi
.
Then, considering test forms
\psi\in\Omega_{c}^{n,n}(D)
we have
(s_{\text{eq}},\psi)=0
and
also the right-hand side of (
1.1.17
) in
Theorem
1.12
would vanish.

For further examples where Theorem
1.11
or
Theorem
1.12
apply, see Examples
6.11
and

6.12
. We refer the reader to
[
32
]
for other results concerning
the semi-classical spectral asymptotics of Toeplitz operators on strictly pseudoconvex domains.

The proofs of the results above are obtained by studying an object
\mathcal{C}_{f}

which we will explain now. Let
(X,T^{1,0}X)
be a compact strictly pseudoconvex CR manifold
satisfying the assumptions in Theorem
1.1
and
consider a smooth CR function
f\in H_{b}^{0}(X)\cap\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(X)
such that
df_{p}\neq 0

holds for all
p\in X
with
f(p)=0
. The assumptions on
f
and
X
entail
that
df/f
is well defined in the sense of currents.
More precisely, we have that
df/f
is integrable on
X
and hence the linear map

(1.1.18)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{f}\colon\Omega^{2n}(X)\to\mathbb{C},\,\,\,\,\mathcal{C}_{f}(\psi):=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{X}\frac{df}{f}\wedge\psi

is well defined (see Theorem
4.19
). In this work the study of
\mathcal{C}_{f}

is important for two reasons. On the one hand it is directly linked to the description of zero sets for
CR functions via
d\mathcal{C}_{f}=\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right]
in distributions sense for any smooth CR function
f

such that zero is a regular value of the map
f\colon X\to\mathbb{C}

(see Theorem
4.21
).
On the other hand
\mathcal{C}_{f}
appears as a boundary term in the Lelong-Poincaré formula
for domains with boundary (see Definition
4.10
and
Theorem
4.14
).

This work is organized as follows. In Section
3

we prove the projective embedding result for CR manifolds as stated in
Theorem
1.1
. Section
4

contains a proof of the Lelong-Poincaré formula for domains with boundary.
The tools developed in Section
3
and
Section
4
will be used to study
\mathcal{C}_{f}
given
by (
1.1.18
) seen as a distribution valued random variable in
Section
5
. The study of
\mathcal{C}_{f}
will then
lead to a proof of the equidistribution results for CR manifolds as stated in
Theorem
1.6
and Theorem
1.7
.
After some remarks on complex manifolds with boundary in Section
6
, we prove
Theorem
1.10
,
1.11
and
1.12
.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Notations

We use the following notations throughout this article.
\mathbb{Z}
is the set of integers,
\mathbb{N}=\{1,2,3,\ldots\}
is the set of natural numbers,
\mathbb{N}_{0}=\mathbb{N}\bigcup\{0\}
and
\mathbb{R}
is the set of
real numbers. Also,
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}:x\geq 0\}
,
\mathbb{R}^{*}:=\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
,
\mathbb{R}_{+}:=\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\setminus\{0\}
.
We write
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n})\in\mathbb{N}^{n}_{0}

if
\alpha_{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

j=1,\ldots,n
. For
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
we write

\begin{split}&x^{\alpha}=x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\ldots x^{\alpha_{n}}_{n},\\
&\partial_{x_{j}}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\,,\quad\partial^{\alpha}_{x}=\partial^{\alpha_{1}}_{x_{1}}\ldots\partial^{\alpha_{n}}_{x_{n}}=\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|}}{\partial x^{\alpha}}\,.\end{split}

Let
z=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
,
z_{j}=x_{2j-1}+ix_{2j}
,
j=1,\ldots,n
,
be coordinates of
\mathbb{C}^{n}
. We write

\begin{split}&z^{\alpha}=z_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\ldots z^{\alpha_{n}}_{n}\,,\quad\overline{z}^{\alpha}=\overline{z}_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\ldots\overline{z}^{\alpha_{n}}_{n}\,,\\
&\partial_{z_{j}}=\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{j}}=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{2j-1}}-i\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{2j}}\Big)\,,\quad\partial_{\overline{z}_{j}}=\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{z}_{j}}=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{2j-1}}+i\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{2j}}\Big),\\
&\partial^{\alpha}_{z}=\partial^{\alpha_{1}}_{z_{1}}\ldots\partial^{\alpha_{n}}_{z_{n}}=\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|}}{\partial z^{\alpha}}\,,\quad\partial^{\alpha}_{\overline{z}}=\partial^{\alpha_{1}}_{\overline{z}_{1}}\ldots\partial^{\alpha_{n}}_{\overline{z}_{n}}=\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|}}{\partial\overline{z}^{\alpha}}\,.\end{split}

For
j,s\in\mathbb{Z}
, set
\delta_{js}=1
if
j=s
,

\delta_{js}=0
if
j\neq s
.

Let
U
be an open set in
\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}}
and let
V
be an open set in
\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}}
. Let
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(V)
and
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(U)
be the space of smooth functions with compact support in
V
and the space of smooth functions on
U
, respectively;
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(U)
and
\mathscr{E}^{\prime}(V)
be the space of distributions on
U
and the space of distributions with compact support in
V
, respectively.

Let
F:\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(V)\to\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(U)
be a continuous operator and let
F(x,y)\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(U\times V)
be the distribution kernel of
F
. In this work, we will identify
F
with
F(x,y)
.
We say that

F
is a smoothing operator
if
F(x,y)\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(U\times V)
.
For two continuous linear operators
A,B:\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(V)\to\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(U)
, we write
A\equiv B
(on
U\times V
) or
A(x,y)\equiv B(x,y)
(on
U\times V
) if
A-B
is a smoothing operator, where
A(x,y),B(x,y)\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(U\times V)
are the distribution kernels of
A
and
B
, respectively.

If
U=V
, for a smooth function
f(x,y)\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(U\times U)
, we write
f(x,y)=O\left(|x-y|^{\infty}\right)
if for all multi-indices
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n_{1}}
,
\left(\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{y}^{\beta}f\right)(x,x)=0
, for all
x\in U
.

Let us introduce some notion in microlocal analysis used in this paper. Let
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
be an open set. For any
m\in\mathbb{R}
,
S^{m}_{1,0}(D\times D\times\mathbb{R}_{+})

is the space of all
s(x,y,t)\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(D\times D\times\mathbb{R}_{+})

such that for all compact sets
K\Subset D\times D
, all
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{2n+1}_{0}
and
\gamma\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
there is a constant
C_{K,\alpha,\beta,\gamma}>0
satisfying the estimate

(2.1.1)

\left|\partial^{\alpha}_{x}\partial^{\beta}_{y}\partial^{\gamma}_{t}a(x,y,t)\right|\leq C_{K,\alpha,\beta,\gamma}(1+|t|)^{m-|\gamma|},\ \ \mbox{for all $(x,y,t)\in K\times\mathbb{R}_{+}$, $|t|\geq 1$}.

We put

S^{-\infty}(D\times D\times\mathbb{R}_{+}):=\bigcap_{m\in\mathbb{R}}S^{m}_{1,0}(D\times D\times\mathbb{R}_{+}).

Let
s_{j}\in S^{m_{j}}_{1,0}(D\times D\times\mathbb{R}_{+})
,

j=0,1,2,\cdots
with
m_{j}\rightarrow-\infty
as
j\rightarrow+\infty
.
By the argument of Borel construction, there always exists
s\in S^{m_{0}}_{1,0}(D\times D\times\mathbb{R}_{+})

unique modulo
S^{-\infty}
such that

(2.1.2)

s-\sum^{\ell-1}_{j=0}s_{j}\in S^{m_{\ell}}_{1,0}(D\times D\times\mathbb{R}_{+})

for all
\ell=1,2,\cdots
. If
s
and
s_{j}
have the properties above, we write

(2.1.3)

s(x,y,t)\sim\sum^{+\infty}_{j=0}s_{j}(x,y,t)~\text{in}~S^{m_{0}}_{1,0}(D\times D\times\mathbb{R}_{+}).

Also, we use the notation

(2.1.4)

s(x,y,t)\in S^{m}_{{\rm cl\,}}(D\times D\times\mathbb{R}_{+})

if
s(x,y,t)\in S^{m}_{1,0}(D\times D\times\mathbb{R}_{+})
and we can find
s_{j}(x,y)\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(D\times D)
,
j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, such that

(2.1.5)

s(x,y,t)\sim\sum^{+\infty}_{j=0}s_{j}(x,y)t^{m-j}\text{ in }S^{m}_{1,0}(D\times D\times\mathbb{R}_{+}).

Let
M,M_{1}
be smooth paracompact manifolds. We can define
S^{m}_{1,0}(M\times M_{1}\times\mathbb{R}_{+})
,

S^{m}_{{\rm cl\,}}(M\times M_{1}\times\mathbb{R}_{+})
and asymptotic sums in the standard way. Given a smooth vector bundle
E\to M
over
E
we denote the space of smooth sections on
M
with values in
E
by
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(M,E)
. The space of those sections with compact support is denoted by
\mathscr{C}_{c}^{\infty}(M,E)
. For any
q\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
we denote the space of smooth
q
-forms on
M
by
\Omega^{q}(M)
and denote space of those forms with compact support by
\Omega_{c}^{q}(M)
. Recall the definition of the exterior differential
d\colon\Omega^{q}(M)\to\Omega^{q+1}(M)
.

For
m\in\mathbb{R}
, let
L^{m}_{{\rm cl\,}}(D)
denote the space of classical pseudodifferential operators of order
m
on
D
. For
P\in L^{m}_{{\rm cl\,}}(D)
, let

\sigma_{P}
denote the principal symbol of
P
.

We recall some notations of semi-classical analysis.
Let
U
be an open set in
\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}}
and let
V

be an open set in
\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}}
.

Definition 2.1
.

We say a
k
-dependent continuous linear operator

F_{k}:\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(V)\to\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(U)
is
k
-negligible
if for all
k
large enough,
F_{k}
is a smoothing operator,
and for any compact set
K
in
U\times V
, for all multi-index

\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n_{1}}
,
\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n_{2}}
and
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
there exists a constant
C_{K,\alpha,\beta,N}>0
such that

\left|\partial^{\alpha}_{x}\partial^{\beta}_{y}F_{k}(x,y)\right|\leq C_{K,\alpha,\beta,N}k^{-N},

for all
x,y\in K
and
k
large enough.
We write
F_{k}(x,y)=O(k^{-\infty})
(on
U\times V
) or
F_{k}=O(k^{-\infty})
(on
U\times V
) if
F_{k}
is
k
-negligible.

Next, we recall semi-classical symbol spaces. Let
W
be an open set in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, we define the space

(2.1.6)

S(1;W):=\{a\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(W):\sup_{x\in W}|\partial^{\alpha}_{x}a(x)|<\infty~\text{for all}~\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{N}\}.

Consider the space
S^{0}_{\operatorname{loc}}(1;W)
containing all smooth functions
a(x,k)
with
real parameter
k
such that for all multi-index
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{N}
, any
cut-off function
\chi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(W)
, we have

(2.1.7)

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{R}\\
k\geq 1\end{subarray}}\sup_{x\in W}|\partial^{\alpha}_{x}(\chi(x)a(x,k))|<\infty.

For general
m\in\mathbb{R}
, we can also consider

(2.1.8)

S^{m}_{\operatorname{loc}}(1;W):=\{a(x,k):k^{-m}a(x,k)\in\ S^{0}_{\operatorname{loc}}(1;W)\}.

In other words,
S^{m}_{\operatorname{loc}}(1;W)
takes all the smooth function
a(x,k)
with parameter
k\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying the following estimate. For any compact set
K\Subset W
, any multi-index
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}_{0}
, there is a constant
C_{K,\alpha}>0
independent of
k
such that

(2.1.9)

|\partial^{\alpha}_{x}(a(x,k))|\leq C_{K,\alpha}k^{m},~\text{for all}~x\in K,~k\geq 1.

For a sequence of
a_{j}\in S^{m_{j}}_{\operatorname{loc}}(1;W)
with
m_{j}
decreasing,
m_{j}\to-\infty
, and
a\in S^{m_{0}}_{\operatorname{loc}}(1;W)
. We say

(2.1.10)

a(x,k)\sim\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}a_{j}(x,k)~\text{in}~S^{m_{0}}_{\operatorname{loc}}(1;W)

if for all
l\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

(2.1.11)

a-\sum_{j=0}^{l-1}a_{j}\in S^{m_{l}}_{\operatorname{loc}}(1;W).

In fact, for all sequence
a_{j}
above, there always exists an element
a
as the asymptotic sum, which is unique up to the elements in
S^{-\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(1;W):=\cap_{m}S^{m}_{\operatorname{loc}}(1;W)
.

The detail of discussion here about microlocal analysis or semi-classical analysis can be found in
[
14
,
21
]
for example. All the notations introduced above can be generalized to the case on paracompact manifolds.

Given a complex manifold
Y
we denote its complex structure by
T^{1,0}Y
. For any
p,q\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
we denote the space of smooth
(p,q)
-forms by
\Omega^{p,q}(Y):=\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(Y,\Lambda^{p,q}\mathbb{C}T^{*}Y)
. We further denote the space of those forms which af compact support by
\Omega^{p,q}_{c}(Y)
. Here,
\Lambda^{p,q}\mathbb{C}T^{*}Y
is the bundle of alternating multilinear forms on
\mathbb{C}TY
of type
(p,q)
. Recall that on
Y
we have the decomposition for the exterior differential
d=\partial+\overline{\partial}
with

\partial\colon\Omega^{p,q}(Y)\to\Omega^{p+1,q}(Y),\,\,\,\overline{\partial}\colon\Omega^{p,q}(Y)\to\Omega^{p,q+1}(Y).

2.2.
Microlocal analysis of the Szegő kernel

Let
X
be a smooth (
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}
) orientable manifold of real dimension
2n+1,~n\geq 1
. We say
X
is a (codimension one) Cauchy–Riemann (CR for short) manifold with CR structure
T^{1,0}X
if

T^{1,0}X\subset\mathbb{C}TX
is a subbundle such that

(1)

(codimension one)
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}T^{1,0}_{p}X=n
for any
p\in X
.

(2)

(non-degenerate)
T^{1,0}_{p}X\cap T^{0,1}_{p}X=\{0\}
for any
p\in X
, where
T^{0,1}_{p}X:=\overline{T^{1,0}_{p}X}
.

(3)

(integrable) For
V_{1},V_{2}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(X,T^{1,0}X)
, then
[V_{1},V_{2}]\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(X,T^{1,0}X)
, where

[\cdot,\cdot]
stands for the Lie bracket between vector fields.

Fix a smooth Hermitian metric
\langle\cdot|\cdot\rangle
on the complexified tangent bundle
\mathbb{C}TX
such that
T^{1,0}X
is orthogonal to
T^{0,1}X
. Then locally there is a real vector field
\mathcal{T}
with
|\mathcal{T}|^{2}:=\langle\mathcal{T}|\mathcal{T}\rangle=1
which is pointwise orthogonal to
T^{1,0}X\oplus T^{0,1}X
. Notice that
\mathcal{T}
is unique up to the choice of sign. Denote
T^{*1,0}X
and
T^{*0,1}X
the dual bundles in
\mathbb{C}T^{*}X
annihilating
\mathbb{C}\mathcal{T}\bigoplus T^{0,1}X
and
\mathbb{C}\mathcal{T}\bigoplus T^{1,0}X
, respectively. Define the bundles of
(0,q)
forms by
T^{*0,q}X:=\wedge^{q}T^{*0,1}X
. The Hermitian metric on
\mathbb{C}TX
induces, by duality, a Hermitian metric on
\mathbb{C}T^{*}X
, and also on
T^{*0,q}X
for all
q\in\{0,\ldots,n\}
. We shall also denote all these induced metrics by
\langle\cdot|\cdot\rangle
. For any open set
D\subset X
, denote
\Omega^{0,q}(D)
be the space of smooth sections of
T^{*0,q}X
over
D
and let
\Omega^{0,q}_{c}(D)
be the subspace of
\Omega^{0,q}(D)
whose elements have compact support in
D
.

Locally, there also exists an orthonormal frame

\{\omega_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
of
T^{*1.0}X
.
Notice that the real

(2n)
-form
\omega:=i^{n}\omega_{1}\wedge\overline{\omega}_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\omega_{n}\wedge\overline{\omega}_{n}
is
independent of the choice of orthonormal frame, so it is globally defined.
Locally, there exists a real
1
-form

\xi
of
|\xi|^{2}:=\langle\xi|\xi\rangle=1

which is orthogonal to
T^{*1,0}X\oplus T^{*0,1}X
.
The
\xi
is unique up to the choice of sign.
Since
X
is orientable, there is a nowhere vanishing
(2n+1)
-form

\Theta
on
X
. Thus,
\xi
can be specified uniquely by
requiring that
\omega\wedge\xi=f\Theta
,
where
f
is a positive smooth function.

Definition 2.2
.

The so chosen
\xi
is globally defined, and we call it the characteristic form on
X
.

The Levi distribution
HX
of the CR manifold
X
is the real part of

T^{1,0}X\oplus T^{0,1}X
,
i.e., the unique subbundle
HX
of
TX
such that

(2.2.1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}HX=T^{1,0}X\oplus T^{0,1}X.

Let
J:HX\to HX
be the complex structure given by

J(u+\overline{u})=iu-i\overline{u}
, for every
u\in T^{1,0}X
.
If we extend
J
complex linearly to
\mathbb{C}HX
we have

T^{1,0}X\,=\,\left\{V\in\mathbb{C}HX\,:\,\,JV\,=\,iV\right\}
.
Thus the CR structure
T^{1,0}X
is determined by
the Levi distribution and
J
.
The annihilator
(HX)^{\perp}\subset T^{*}X
is given by

(HX)^{\perp}=\mathbb{R}\xi
, so
we have

\langle\,\xi(x),u\,\rangle=0
,
for any
u\in H_{x}X,\>x\in X
.
The restriction of
d\xi

on
HX
is a
(1,1)
-form.

Definition 2.3
.

The Levi form
\mathcal{L}_{x}=\mathcal{L}^{\xi}_{x}
of
X
at
x\in X

associated to
\xi
is the symmetric bilinear map

(2.2.2)

\mathcal{L}_{x}:H_{x}X\times H_{x}X\to\mathbb{R},\quad\mathcal{L}_{x}(u,v)=\frac{d\xi}{2}(u,Jv),\quad\text{ for }u,v\in H_{x}X.

It induces a Hermitian form

(2.2.3)

\mathcal{L}_{x}:T^{1,0}_{x}X\times T^{1,0}_{x}X\to\mathbb{C},\quad\mathcal{L}_{x}(U,V)=\frac{d\xi}{2i}(U,\overline{V}),\quad\text{ for }U,V\in T^{1,0}_{x}X.

Definition 2.4
.

A CR manifold
X
is said to be strictly pseudoconvex if
there exists a characteristic
1
-form
\xi

such that for every
x\in X
the Levi form

\mathcal{L}^{\xi}_{x}
is positive definite. In this case, such
1
-form
\xi
is also called a contact form because
\xi\wedge(d\xi)^{n}\neq 0
, where
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}X=2n+1
.

From now on, we assume that
(X,T^{1,0}X)
is a compact strictly pseudoconvex CR manifold of dimension
2n+1

and we fix a characteristic
1
-form
\xi
on
X
such that
\mathcal{L}^{\xi}_{x}
is positive definite at every
x\in X
.

Definition 2.5
.

The Reeb vector field
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}(\xi)

associated with the contact form
\xi
is
the vector field uniquely defined by the equations

i_{\mathcal{T}}\xi=1
,
i_{\mathcal{T}}d\xi=0
,
cf.
[
18
]
*Lemma/Definition 1.1.9.

We take
\langle\cdot|\cdot\rangle
so that
\langle\mathcal{T}|\mathcal{T}\rangle=1
,
\langle\xi|\xi\rangle=1
on
X
.
With respect to the given Hermitian metric
\langle\cdot|\cdot\rangle
, we consider the orthogonal projection

(2.2.4)

\pi^{(0,q)}:\Lambda^{q}\mathbb{C}T^{*}X\to T^{*0,q}X.

The tangential Cauchy–Riemann operator is defined to be

(2.2.5)

\overline{\partial}_{b}:=\pi^{(0,q+1)}\circ d:\Omega^{0,q}(X)\to\Omega^{0,q+1}(X).

By Cartan’s formula, we can check that

(2.2.6)

\overline{\partial}_{b}^{2}=0.

In this paper, we work with two volume forms on
X
.

(
i
)

A given smooth positive
(2n+1)
-form
dV(x)
.

(
ii
)

The
(2n+1)
-form
dV_{\xi}:=\frac{2^{-n}}{n!}\xi\wedge\left(d\xi\right)^{n}
given by the contact form
\xi
.

Take the
L^{2}
-inner product
(\cdot|\cdot)
on
\Omega^{0,q}(X)
induced by
dV
and
\langle\cdot|\cdot\rangle
via

(2.2.7)

(f|g):=\int_{X}\langle f|g\rangle dV,~f,g\in\Omega^{0,q}(X).

We denote by
L^{2}_{(0,q)}(X)
the completion of
\Omega^{0,q}(X)
with respect to
(\cdot|\cdot)
, and we write
L^{2}(X):=L^{2}_{(0,0)}(X)
. Furthermore, for
q=0
we will also use the notation
(\cdot,\cdot):=(\cdot|\cdot)
to denote the
L^{2}
-inner product.
Let
\|\cdot\|
denote the corresponding
L^{2}
-norm on
L^{2}_{(0,q)}(X)
.
Let
\overline{\partial}_{b}^{*}
be the formal adjoint of
\overline{\partial}_{b}
with respect to
(\cdot|\cdot)
, and the Kohn Laplacian is defined by

(2.2.8)

\Box^{(0)}_{b}:=\overline{\partial}_{b}^{*}\overline{\partial}_{b}:\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(X)\to\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(X),

which is not an elliptic operator because the principal symbol
\sigma_{\Box^{(0)}_{b}}(x,\eta)\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(T^{*}X)
has a non-empty characteristic set

\{(x,\eta)\in T^{*}X:\eta=\lambda\xi(x),~\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{*}\}
. For later use, we denote the symplectic cone

(2.2.9)

\Sigma:=\{(x,\eta)\in T^{*}X:\eta=\lambda\xi(x),~\lambda>0\}.

In fact,
\Box^{(0)}_{b}
can even not be hypoelliptic, that is,
\Box^{(0)}_{b}u\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(X)
cannot guarantee
u\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(X)
. We extend
\overline{\partial}_{b}
to the
L^{2}
-space by

(2.2.10)

\overline{\partial}_{b}:\Dom\overline{\partial}_{b}\subset L^{2}(X)\to L^{2}_{(0,1)}(X),\quad\Dom\overline{\partial}_{b}:=\{u\in L^{2}(X):\overline{\partial}_{b}u\in L^{2}_{(0,1)}(X)\}.

Let
\overline{\partial}^{*}_{b,H}:\Dom\overline{\partial}^{*}_{b,H}\subset L^{2}_{(0,1)}(X)\to L^{2}(X)
be the
L^{2}
adjoint of
\overline{\partial}_{b}
. Let

\Box^{(0)}_{b}:\Dom\Box^{(0)}_{b}\subset L^{2}(X)\to L^{2}(X)
, where

\Dom\Box^{(0)}_{b}=\{u\in L^{2}(X):u\in\Dom\overline{\partial}_{b},\overline{\partial}_{b}u\in\Dom\overline{\partial}^{*}_{b,H}\}

and
\Box^{(0)}_{b}u=\overline{\partial}^{*}_{b,H}\,\overline{\partial}_{b}u
,
u\in\Dom\Box^{(0)}_{b}
.

Definition 2.6
.

The space of
L^{2}
CR functions is given by

(2.2.11)

H_{b}^{0}(X):=\Ker\Box^{(0)}_{b}:=\left\{u\in\Dom\Box^{(0)}_{b}:\Box^{(0)}_{b}u=0~\text{in the sense of distributions}\right\}.

Definition 2.7
.

The orthogonal projection

(2.2.12)

\Pi:L^{2}(X)\to\ker\Box^{(0)}_{b}

is called the Szegő projection on
X
, and we call
its Schwartz kernel
\Pi(x,y)\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(X\times X)
the Szegő kernel on
X
.

In our case, we can understand the singularities of
\Pi
by microlocal analysis as in
[
8
]
, see also
[
34
,
31
]
.

Theorem 2.8

(
[
34
]
*Part I Theorem 1.2)
.

Let
(X,T^{1,0}X)
be an orientable
compact strictly pseudoconvex Cauchy–Riemann manifold with volume form
dV
such that the Kohn Laplacian on
X
has closed range in
L^{2}(X)
. For any coordinate chart
(D,x)
in
X
, we have

(2.2.13)

\Pi(x,y)\equiv\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{it\varphi(x,y)}s^{\varphi}(x,y,t)dt,

where

(2.2.14)

\begin{split}&\varphi(x,y)\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(D\times D),\\
&\mbox{${\rm Im\,}\varphi(x,y)\geq 0$},\\
&\mbox{$\varphi(x,y)=0$ if and only if $y=x$},\\
&\mbox{$d_{x}\varphi(x,x)=-d_{y}\varphi(x,x)=\xi(x)$},\end{split}

and

(2.2.15)

\begin{split}&s^{\varphi}(x,y,t)\in S^{n}_{1,0}(D\times D\times\mathbb{R}_{+}),\\
&s^{\varphi}(x,y,t)\sim\sum^{+\infty}_{j=0}s^{\varphi}_{j}(x,y)t^{n-j}~\text{in}~S^{n}_{1,0}(D\times D\times\mathbb{R}_{+}),\\
&s^{\varphi}_{j}(x,y)\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(D\times D),~j=0,1,2,\ldots,\\
&s^{\varphi}_{0}(x,x)=\frac{1}{2\pi^{n+1}}\frac{dV_{\xi}}{dV}(x)\neq 0.\end{split}

In the local picture, near any point
p\in X
, let
\{W_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
be an orthonormal frame of

T^{1,0}X
in a neighborhood of
p
such that
\mathcal{L}_{p}(W_{j},\overline{W}_{s})=\delta_{js}\mu_{j}
,
for some
\mu_{j}>0
and
j,s\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
Define

(2.2.16)

\det\mathcal{L}_{p}:=\Pi_{j=1}^{n}\mu_{j}(p).

Then

(2.2.17)

dV_{\xi}(x)=\det\mathcal{L}_{x}\sqrt{\det(g)}\,dx.

where
g=(g_{jk})_{j,k=1}^{2n+1}
,

g_{jk}=\langle\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}|\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{k}}\rangle
.

We have the following local description of the tangential Hessian of the phase function calculated in
[
34
]
*Part I Chapter 8. Such local picture is essential for calculating the leading coefficient of the Szegő kernel expansion
[
8
,
34
,
31
]
. Also, it turns out that it is crucial in our proof of the CR embedding theorem (Theorem
1.1
).

Theorem 2.9

(
[
34
]
*Part I Theorem 1.4)
.

For a given point
p\in X
, let
\{W_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
be an orthonormal frame of
T^{1,0}X
in a neighborhood of
p
such that
\mathcal{L}_{p}(W_{j},\overline{W}_{s})=\delta_{js}\mu_{j}
, for some
\mu_{j}>0
and
j,s\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
. Take local coordinates near
p
such that

(2.2.18)

x(p)=0,\quad\xi(p)=dx_{2n+1},\quad\mathcal{T}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{2n+1}},

and

(2.2.19)

\begin{split}W_{j}=\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{j}}+i\mu_{j}\overline{z}_{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{2n+1}}-d_{j}x_{2n+1}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{2n+1}}+\sum_{l=1}^{2n}e_{jl}(x)\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{l}},\end{split}

where
d_{j}\in\mathbb{C}
,
e_{jl}=O(|x|)
,
e_{jl}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}
,
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
,
l\in\{1,\ldots,2n\}
. Then, for
\varphi
in (
2.2.14
), we have

(2.2.20)

\begin{split}\varphi(x,y)&=x_{2n+1}-y_{2n+1}+i\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mu_{j}|z_{j}-w_{j}|^{2}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}i\mu_{j}(\overline{z}_{j}w_{j}-z_{j}\overline{w}_{j})\\
&+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(d_{j}(z_{j}x_{2n+1}-w_{j}y_{2n+1})+\overline{d}_{j}(\overline{z}_{j}x_{2n+1}-\overline{w}_{j}y_{2n+1})\right)\\
&+(x_{2n+1}-y_{2n+1})f(x,y)+O\left(|(x,y)|^{3}\right),\end{split}

where
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(D\times D)
satisfies

f(x,y)=\overline{f}(y,x)
and
f(0,0)=0
.

We notice that under such coordinates, when the neighborhood is small enough, we can find some constant
C>0
such that

(2.2.21)

\Im\varphi(x,y)\geq C|z-w|^{2}.

See
[
34
]
*Part I Proposition 7.16 for a proof of (
2.2.21
).

2.3.
Semi-classical spectral asymptotics for Toeplitz operators

We recall the main result of
[
26
]
which is based on
Theorem
2.8
.
The following theorem is essential for the proofs of the results in this work.

Theorem 2.10

(
[
26
]
*Theorem 1.1)
.

Let
(X,T^{1,0}X)
be an orientable
compact strictly pseudoconvex Cauchy–Riemann manifold
of dimension
2n+1
,
n\geq 1
, with volume form
dV

such that the Kohn Laplacian on
X
has closed range in

L^{2}(X)
. Let
\xi
be a contact form on
X
such that the Levi form

\mathcal{L}=\frac{1}{2}d\xi(\cdot,J\cdot)
is positive definite.
Let
(D,x)
be any coordinates patch and let

\varphi:D\times D\to\mathbb{C}
be the phase function satisfying
(
2.2.13
) and (
2.2.14
).
Then for any formally self-adjoint first order pseudodifferential operator

P\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{cl}}(X)
with

\sigma_{P}(\xi)>0
on
X
,
and for any
\chi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}((0,+\infty))
,
\chi\not\equiv 0
,
the Schwartz kernel of
\chi_{k}(T_{P})
,

\chi_{k}(\lambda):=\chi\left(k^{-1}\lambda\right)

k>0
,
can be represented for
k
large by

(2.3.1)

\mbox{$\chi_{k}(T_{P})(x,y)=\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{ikt\varphi(x,y)}{A}(x,y,t,k)dt+O\left(k^{-\infty}\right)$ on $D\times D$},

where
{A}(x,y,t,k)\in S^{n+1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(1;D\times D\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+})
,

(2.3.2)

\begin{split}&{A}(x,y,t,k)\sim\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}{A}_{j}(x,y,t)k^{n+1-j}~\mathrm{in}~S^{n+1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(1;D\times D\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}),\\
&A_{j}(x,y,t)\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(D\times D\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}),~j=0,1,2,\ldots,\\
&{A}_{0}(x,x,t)=\frac{1}{2\pi^{n+1}}\frac{dV_{\xi}}{dV}(x)\,\chi(\sigma_{P}(\xi_{x})t)t^{n}\not\equiv 0,\end{split}

and for some compact interval
I\Subset\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

(2.3.3)

\begin{split}\operatorname{supp}_{t}A(x,y,t,k),~\operatorname{supp}_{t}A_{j}(x,y,t)\subset I,\ \ j=0,1,2,\ldots.\end{split}

Moreover, for any
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(X)

such that
\operatorname{supp}(\tau_{1})\cap\operatorname{supp}(\tau_{2})=\emptyset
,
we have

(2.3.4)

\tau_{1}\chi_{k}(T_{P})\tau_{2}=O\left(k^{-\infty}\right).

From Theorem
2.10
we can deduce the following useful lemma as in
[
26
]
.

Lemma 2.11

(cf.
[
26
]
*Lemma 4.12)
.

In the situation of Theorem
2.10

we have in
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}
-topology as
k\to+\infty
,

(2.3.5)

d_{x}\chi_{k}(T_{P})(x,y)|_{y=x}=i\xi(x)\frac{k^{n+2}}{2\pi^{n+1}}\frac{dV_{\xi}}{dV}(x)\sigma_{P}(\xi_{x})^{-n-2}\int_{0}^{+\infty}t^{n+1}\chi(t)dt+O(k^{n+1}),

and

(2.3.6)

\left(d_{x}\otimes d_{y}\right)\chi_{k}(T_{P})(x,y)|_{y=x}=\xi(x)\otimes\xi(x)\frac{k^{n+3}}{2\pi^{n+1}}\frac{dV_{\xi}}{dV}(x)\sigma_{P}(\xi_{x})^{-n-3}\int_{0}^{+\infty}t^{n+2}\chi(t)dt+O(k^{n+2}).

3.
Projective Embeddings via
T_{P}

Let
(X,T^{1,0}X)
and
T_{P}
be as in Theorem
1.1
. In this section we are going to prove Theorem
1.1
. More precisely, for each
k>0
we will construct a CR map
F_{k}\colon X\to\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}
related to
T_{P}
and show that
X\ni x\mapsto[F_{k}(x)]\in\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{N_{k}}
defines a CR embedding of
X
into the complex projective space when
k
is sufficiently large (see Theorem
3.5
). As a consequence of this result we obtain Theorem
1.1
.

First, we will define two objects
h^{F},\mathcal{H}^{F}
(see Definition
3.1
) which determine (see Lemma
3.4
) whether the projectivization of a map
F\colon M\to\mathbb{C}^{N+1}\setminus{\{0\}}
, defined on any compact smooth real manifold
M
is an embedding. Then we will construct maps
F_{k}
,
k>0
, defined on
X
and such that
h^{F_{k}},\mathcal{H}^{F_{k}}
are directly linked to the functional calculus of
T_{P}
. Using Theorem
2.10
we can analyze the behavior of
h^{F_{k}}
and
\mathcal{H}^{F_{k}}
when
k
becomes large (see Corollary
3.7
and Lemma
3.8
). The embedding result follows. At the end of this section we will prove two integral estimates (see Lemma
3.16
and Lemma
3.15
) related to
h^{F_{k}}
which we will need for the proof of the results in Section
5
and Section
7
.

3.1.
Projective Embeddability

Definition 3.1
.

Let
M
be a real manifold and
F\colon M\to\mathbb{C}^{N+1}\setminus\{0\}
a smooth map. We define the symmetric bilinear form
\mathcal{H}^{F}=\{\mathcal{H}^{F}_{x}\}_{x\in M}
on
TM
by
\mathcal{H}^{F}_{x}\colon T_{x}M\times T_{x}M\to\mathbb{R}
,

\mathcal{H}^{F}_{x}(X,Y)=\frac{1}{|F(x)|^{4}}\text{Re}\left(\langle XF(x),F(x)\rangle\overline{\langle YF(x),F(x)\rangle}-\langle XF(x),YF(x)\rangle|F(x)|^{2}\right).

Furthermore, we denote by
h^{F}\colon M\times M\to\mathbb{R}
the function given by

h^{F}(x,y)=\frac{|\langle F(x),F(y)\rangle|^{2}}{|F(x)|^{2}|F(y)|^{2}}.

We have that
h^{F}
and
\mathcal{H}^{F}
are related in the following way.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
M
be a real manifold and
F\colon M\to\mathbb{C}^{N+1}\setminus\{0\}
a smooth map. Given
y\in M
define
g^{F}_{y}\colon M\to[0,1]
,
g^{F}_{y}(x)=h^{F}(x,y)
.
Then
g^{F}_{y}
is well defined and has a local maximum at
x=y
with
g^{F}_{y}(y)=1
. Furthermore, the Hessian of
g_{y}
at
x=y
is given by
\text{Hess}_{y}(g^{F}_{y})=\mathcal{H}^{F}_{y}
.

Proof.

First recall that given a smooth function
f\colon M\to\mathbb{R}
which has a critical point at
x\in M
we have that the Hessian
\text{Hess}_{x}(f)\colon T_{x}M\times T_{x}M\to\mathbb{R}
of
f
at
x
is defined by
\text{Hess}_{x}(f)(X,Y)=(\tilde{X}(\tilde{Y}(f))(x)
where
\tilde{X}
and
\tilde{Y}
are smooth vector fields on
M
with
\tilde{X}_{x}=X
and
\tilde{Y}_{x}=Y
. Since
(df)_{x}=0
it turns out that
\text{Hess}_{x}(f)(X,Y)
is independent of the choice of
\tilde{X}
and
\tilde{Y}
. Furthermore, since
df([\tilde{X},\tilde{Y}]_{x})=0
it follows that
\text{Hess}_{x}(f)
is symmetric.

We have that
g_{y}
is smooth. From the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality we find that
g_{y}
is well defined and
g_{y}(y)=1
. It follows that
g_{y}
has a local maximum at
x=y
and hence
(dg_{y})_{y}=0
. Let
X\in\Gamma(M,TM)
be a smooth real vector field.
Putting
\tilde{F}=F/|F|
we find for any
v\in\mathbb{C}^{N+1}
that

X|\langle\tilde{F},v\rangle|^{2}=\langle(X\tilde{F}),v\rangle\overline{\langle\tilde{F},v\rangle}+\langle\tilde{F},v\rangle\overline{\langle X\tilde{F},v\rangle}

and

\displaystyle X(X|\langle\tilde{F},v\rangle|^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle X(X\tilde{F}),v\rangle\overline{\langle\tilde{F},v\rangle}+\langle\tilde{F},v\rangle\overline{\langle X(X\tilde{F}),v\rangle}+2|\langle X\tilde{F},v\rangle|^{2}

Since
|\tilde{F}|\equiv 1
we find

0=X|\tilde{F}|^{2}=2\text{Re}(\langle X\tilde{F},\tilde{F}\rangle).

With
v=\tilde{F}(y)
we conclude that

(3.1.1)

\displaystyle(X(Xg_{y}))|_{x=y}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\text{Re}(\langle X(X\tilde{F}),\tilde{F}\rangle)+2\text{Im}(\langle X\tilde{F},\tilde{F}\rangle)^{2}

Since
\tilde{F}=F/|F|
we have

\displaystyle X\tilde{F}=\frac{XF}{|F|}-\frac{\text{Re}(\langle XF,F\rangle)F}{|F|^{3}}

and hence

(3.1.2)

\displaystyle\langle X\tilde{F},\tilde{F}\rangle=\frac{i\text{Im}(\langle XF,F\rangle)}{|F|^{2}}.

Furthermore, we calculate

\displaystyle X(X\tilde{F})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{X(XF)}{|F|}-\frac{\text{Re}(\langle XF,F\rangle)XF}{|F|^{3}}-\frac{\text{Re}(\langle XF,F\rangle)XF}{|F|^{3}}

\displaystyle-\frac{\text{Re}(\langle X(XF),F\rangle)F}{|F|^{3}}-\frac{\langle XF,XF\rangle F}{|F|^{3}}+3\frac{\text{Re}(\langle XF,F\rangle)^{2}F}{|F|^{5}}

which leads to

\displaystyle\text{Re}(\langle X(X\tilde{F}),\tilde{F}\rangle)=-\frac{\langle XF,XF\rangle}{|F|^{2}}+\frac{\text{Re}(\langle XF,F\rangle)^{2}}{|F|^{4}}

With (
3.1.1
) and (
3.1.2
) we conclude

\displaystyle((X(Xg_{y}))(x))|_{x=y}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{|\langle XF,F\rangle|^{2}-\langle XF,XF\rangle|F|^{2}}{|F|^{4}}(x).

We have shown that
\text{Hess}_{y}(g_{y})(X,X)=\mathcal{H}_{y}(X,X)
for all
X\in T_{x}M
. Since for
X,Y\in T_{X}M
we have

\text{Hess}_{y}(g_{y})(X,Y)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\text{Hess}_{y}(g_{y})(X+X,X+Y)-\text{Hess}_{y}(g_{y})(X,X)-\text{Hess}_{y}(g_{y})(Y,Y)\right)

the claim follows from a direct calculation of
\mathcal{H}_{y}(X+Y,X+Y)
.
∎

We are interested in understanding whether
M\ni x\mapsto[F(x)]\in\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{N}
defines an embedding with respect to the properties of
h^{F}
and
\mathcal{H}^{F}
.
When
M
is compact, necessary and sufficient conditions can be obtained from the following observation.

Lemma 3.3
.

Denote by

\operatorname{dist}^{\mathbb{C}}\colon\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{N}\times\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}
the Fubini-Study distance on
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{N}
that is

\operatorname{dist}^{\mathbb{C}}([v],[w])=\sqrt{1-\frac{|\langle v,w\rangle|}{|v||w|}},\text{ for }[v],[w]\in\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{N}.

Then
\operatorname{dist}^{\mathbb{C}}([F(x)],[F(y)])=\sqrt{1-\sqrt{h^{F}(x,y)}}
for all
x,y\in M
. Furthermore, let
ds_{\operatorname{FS}}^{2}
be the Fubini-Study metric on
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{N}
. We have
\Re([F]^{*}ds_{\operatorname{FS}}^{2})=-\mathcal{H}^{F}
.

Proof.

The first part of the statement is obvious. In order to prove the second part consider the map
pr\colon\mathbb{C}^{N+1}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{N}
,
pr(z)=[z]
. We find

(pr^{*}ds_{\operatorname{FS}}^{2})(z)=\frac{|z|^{2}\sum_{j=0}^{N}dz_{j}\otimes d\overline{z}_{j}-\left(\sum_{j=0}^{N}\overline{z}_{j}dz_{j}\right)\otimes\left(\sum_{j=0}^{N}z_{j}d\overline{z}_{j}\right)}{|z|^{4}}

and hence
\Re([F]^{*}ds_{\operatorname{FS}}^{2})=\Re((pr\circ F)^{*}ds_{\operatorname{FS}}^{2})=-\mathcal{H}^{F}
.
∎

Using Lemma
3.3
we obtain the following.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
M
be a compact smooth real manifold and
F\colon M\to\mathbb{C}^{N+1}\setminus\{0\}
a smooth map. The following are equivalent.

(i)

The map
[F]\colon M\to\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{N}
,
[F](x)=[F(x)]
is an embedding of
M
into
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{N}
.

(ii)

One has
h^{F}(x,y)=1
if and only if
x=y
and
\mathcal{H}^{F}_{x}
is negative definite for all
x\in M
.

Proof.

Since
M
is compact we have that
[F]
is an embedding if and only if it is an injective immersion. Then the equivalence of (i) and (ii) follows from Lemma
3.3
.
∎

3.2.
Proof of Theorem
1.1

Now let
(X,T^{1,0}X)
be a compact orientable strictly pseudoconvex CR manifold such that the Kohn-Laplacian has closed range. Denote by
\xi
a contact form on
X
such that the respective Levi form is positive definite and let
\mathcal{T}
be the respective Reeb vector field uniquely determined by
\iota_{\mathcal{T}}\xi\equiv 1
and
\iota_{\mathcal{T}}d\xi\equiv 0
. Let
P\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{cl}}(X)
be a first order formally self-adjoint classical pseudodifferential operator with
\sigma_{P}(\xi)>0
where
\sigma_{P}
denotes the principal symbol of
P
. Denote by
T_{P}=\Pi P\Pi
the corresponding Toeplitz operator. Let
0<\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}\leq\ldots
be the positive eigenvalues of
T_{P}
counting multiplicity and let
f_{1},f_{2}\ldots\in H_{b}^{0}(X)\cap\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(X)
be a respective orthonormal system of eigenvectors. In particular, we have
T_{P}f_{j}=\lambda_{j}f_{j}
,
(f_{j},f_{j})=1
and
(f_{j},f_{\ell})=0
for all
j,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,
j\neq\ell
. Let
\chi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}((\delta_{1},\delta_{2}))
,
\chi\not\equiv 0
, be a cut-off function for some
0<\delta_{1}<\delta_{2}<1
and put
\eta(t):=|\chi(t)|^{2}
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
, with
\tau_{j}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}}t^{n+j}\eta(t)dt
for
j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Put
N_{k}=|\{j\in\mathbb{N}\mid\lambda_{j}\leq k\delta_{2}\}|
. Let
\kappa\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C}
be a function such that
|\kappa(k)|^{2}/(1+|k|^{n})
is bounded in
k
.
For
k>0
define
F_{k}\colon X\to\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}
,

(3.2.1)

\displaystyle F_{k}\colon X\to\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1},\,\,\,\,\,F_{k}(x)=(\kappa(k),\chi(k^{-1}\lambda_{1})f_{1}(x),\ldots,\chi(k^{-1}\lambda_{N_{k}})f_{N_{k}}(x)).

The term
\kappa(k)
is introduced here for later applications. We will mainly consider
\kappa\equiv 0
or
\kappa\equiv 1
then. Using Lemma
3.4
we would like to show that the projectivization
[F_{k}]
of
F_{k}
is an embedding when
k
is sufficiently large. We have the following.

Theorem 3.5
.

With the assumptions in Theorem
1.1
,
F_{k}
as in (
3.2.1
) and the notations above we have that there exists
k_{0}>0
such that
[F_{k}]\colon X\to\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{N_{k}}
,
[F_{k}](x)=[F_{k}(x)]
, is well defined and a CR embedding of
X
into
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{N_{k}}
for all
k\geq k_{0}
. Furthermore, we have that
[F_{k}]^{*}ds_{FS}^{2}
has an asymptotic expansion in
k
. More precisely, there exist smooth sesquilinear forms
r_{j}\colon\mathbb{C}TX\times\mathbb{C}TX\to\mathbb{C}
,
j\in\mathbb{N}
, such that for any
M,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a constant
C_{M,\ell}>0
with

\left\|[F_{k}]^{*}ds^{2}_{FS}-\sum_{j=0}^{M}k^{2-j}r_{j}\right\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\ell}(X)}\leq Ck^{-M+1},\,\,\,\text{ for all }k\geq k_{0}.

In addition, we have
r_{0}=\frac{\tau_{2}\tau_{0}-\tau_{1}^{2}}{\tau_{0}^{2}\sigma_{P}(\xi)^{2}}\xi\otimes\xi
and
r_{1}(Z,W)=\frac{\tau_{1}}{i\tau_{0}\sigma_{P}(\xi)}d\xi(Z,\overline{W})
for all
Z,W\in T_{x}^{1,0}X
,
x\in X
.

Proof.

Since
|F_{k}(x)|^{2}=|\kappa(k)|^{2}+\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,x)
it follows from Theorem
2.10
and the assumptions on
\kappa
that there exists
C,k_{1}>0
such that
|F_{k}(x)|^{2}\geq C
for all
x\in X
and
k\geq k_{1}
. Hence
[F_{k}]
is well defined for
k\geq k_{1}
. Furthermore, by definition we have that
F_{k}
is a CR map for all
k>0
. Since
\mathbb{C}^{N+1}\setminus\{0\}\ni v\mapsto[v]\in\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{N}
is holomorphic it follows that
[F_{k}]
is a CR map for all
k\geq k_{1}
. Since
X
is a CR manifold of codimension one we have that a CR map which is an embedding is automatically a CR embedding. Hence it remains to show that
[F_{k}]
is an embedding when
k
is large. In view of Lemma
3.4
the first part of the claim follows immediately from Corollary
3.7
and Lemma
3.8
below.

For the second part of the claim we consider

[F_{k}]^{*}ds_{FS}^{2}=(pr\circ F_{k})^{*}ds_{FS}^{2}=\frac{|F_{k}|^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}\eta_{k}(\lambda_{j})df_{j}\otimes d\overline{f}_{j}-\left(\sum_{j=0}^{N}\eta_{k}(\lambda_{j})\overline{f}_{j}df_{j}\right)\otimes\left(\sum_{j=0}^{N_{k}}\eta_{k}(\lambda_{j})f_{j}d\overline{f}_{j}\right)}{|F_{k}|^{4}}

with
pr\colon\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{N_{k}}
,
pr(z)=[z]
. Since
|F_{k}(x)|^{2}=|\kappa(k)|^{2}+\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,x)
we obtain

\displaystyle[F_{k}]^{*}ds_{FS}^{2}=\frac{(|\kappa(k)|^{2}+\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,x))(d_{x}\otimes d_{y}\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,y))|_{x=y}-d_{x}\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,y)|_{x=y}\otimes\overline{d_{x}\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,y)|_{x=y}}}{(|\kappa(k)|^{2}+\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,x))^{2}}.

Hence from Lemma
2.11
, Theorem
2.10
and the assumption on
\kappa
, we find that there exist smooth bilinear forms
\tilde{r}_{j}\colon TX\times TX\to\mathbb{C}
,
j\in\mathbb{N}
, on
TX
such that
[F_{k}]^{*}ds_{FS}^{2}\sim\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}k^{2-j}\tilde{r}_{j}
has an asymptotic expansion in
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}
-topology. Extending each
\tilde{r}_{j}
to a smooth sesquiliniear form
r_{j}
on
\mathbb{C}TX
the claim follows. Applying Lemma
2.11
again we immediately obtain

r_{0}=\frac{\tau_{2}\tau_{0}-\tau_{1}^{2}}{\tau_{0}^{2}\sigma_{P}(\xi)^{2}}\xi\otimes\xi.

Given
Z,W\in T_{x}^{1,0}X
for some
x\in X
we find with
\xi(Z)=\xi(W)=0
that

\displaystyle r_{1}(Z,W)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{(d_{x}\otimes d_{y}\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,y))|_{x=y}(Z,\overline{W})}{k(|\kappa(k)|^{2}+\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,x))}=\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{\langle ZF_{k},WF_{k}\rangle}{k|F_{k}|^{2}}.

We can take smooth sections
Z^{\prime},W^{\prime}
with values in
T^{1,0}X
in a small neighborhood around
x
such that
Z^{\prime}_{x}=Z
and
W^{\prime}_{x}=W
. It follows that

\displaystyle\langle Z^{\prime}F_{k},W^{\prime}F_{k}\rangle=\overline{W^{\prime}}\langle Z^{\prime}F_{k},F_{k}\rangle-\langle\overline{W^{\prime}}Z^{\prime}F_{k},F_{k}\rangle=\overline{W^{\prime}}\langle Z^{\prime}F_{k},F_{k}\rangle+\langle[Z^{\prime},\overline{W^{\prime}}]F_{k},F_{k}\rangle

with
\overline{W^{\prime}}\langle Z^{\prime}F_{k},F_{k}\rangle/|F_{k}|^{2}=O(1)
by Lemma
2.11
. Since

\displaystyle\frac{\langle[Z^{\prime},\overline{W^{\prime}}]F_{k},F_{k}\rangle}{|F_{k}|^{2}}(x)=\frac{d_{x}\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,y)|_{x=y}([Z^{\prime},\overline{W^{\prime}}]_{x})}{|\kappa(k)|^{2}+\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,x)}=ik\frac{\tau_{1}}{\tau_{0}}\alpha_{P}([Z^{\prime},\overline{W^{\prime}}]_{x})+O(1)

by Lemma
2.11
and using
\xi([Z^{\prime},\overline{W^{\prime}}]_{x})=-d\xi(Z,\overline{W})
we find
r_{1}(Z,W)=\frac{\tau_{1}}{i\tau_{0}\sigma_{P}(\xi)}d\xi(Z,\overline{W})
.
∎

As a consequence of Theorem
3.5
we obtain Theorem
1.1
.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

We note that
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{N-1}
can be isometrically identified with the set
\{[z_{0}:\ldots:z_{N}]\in\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{N}\mid z_{0}=0\}
. Then the claim follows from Theorem
3.5
by taking
\kappa\equiv 0
.
∎

In order to complete the proof of Theorem
3.5
it remains to show that
\mathcal{H}^{F_{k}}
is negative definite and that
h^{F_{k}}(x,y)=1
if and only if
x=y
. We have the following.

Lemma 3.6
.

Put
H=\{H_{x}\}_{x\in X}
,
H_{x}:=\text{Re}(T^{1,0}_{x}X)
, and
define an isomorphism
L_{k}\colon TX\to TX
by
L_{k}(\mathcal{T})=\frac{1}{k}\mathcal{T}
and
L_{k}(V)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{k}}V
for
V\in H
. Denote by
h
the positive symmetric bilinearform on
H
induced by the Levi-form. Extend
h
to a symmetric bilinearform on
TX
by
h(\mathcal{T},\cdot)\equiv 0
. Moreover, let
\langle\cdot\mid\cdot\rangle
be a smooth Hermitian metric on
\mathbb{C}TX
as in Section
2.2
. There exists positive smooth functions
a,b\colon X\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

L_{k}^{*}(\mathcal{H}^{F_{k}})=-a\xi\otimes\xi-bh+O(k^{-\frac{1}{2}})

for all sufficiently large
k>0
.
More precisely, there exists
k_{0},C>0
such that

\left|L_{k}^{*}(\mathcal{H}^{F_{k}})(V,W)+a\xi(V)\xi(W)+bh(V,W)\right|\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{k}}|V||W|

for all
k\geq k_{0}
,
V,W\in T_{p}X
,
p\in X
.

Proof.

We choose
k_{0},C_{0}>0
such that
|\kappa(k)|^{2}+\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,x)\geq C_{0}k^{n+1}
for all
x\in X
and
k\geq k_{0}
.
With Theorem
2.10
and Lemma
2.11
we have

\displaystyle L_{k}^{*}(\mathcal{H}^{F_{k}})(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{T})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle k^{-2}\left(\frac{|(d_{x}\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,y)|_{x=y})(\mathcal{T})|^{2}}{||\kappa(k)|^{2}+\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,x)|^{2}}-\frac{|(d_{x}\otimes d_{y}\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,y)|_{x=y})(\mathcal{T}\otimes\mathcal{T})|}{|\kappa(k)|^{2}+\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,x)}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\tau_{1}^{2}-\tau_{2}\tau_{0}}{\sigma_{P}(\xi)^{2}\tau_{0}^{2}}+O(k^{-1})

where
\tau_{j}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\eta(t)t^{n+j}dt
. By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality we find
\tau_{1}<\sqrt{\tau_{2}}\sqrt{\tau_{0}}
. Hence there exist a positive function
a\colon X\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
and a constant
C_{1}>0
with
|L_{k}^{*}(\mathcal{H}^{F_{k}})(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{T})+a|\leq C_{1}k^{-1}
for all
k\geq k_{0}
. From Lemma
2.11
we also have for
V\in H_{x}
that

\displaystyle\frac{|(d_{x}\otimes d_{y}\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,y)|_{x=y})(\mathcal{T}\otimes V)}{|\kappa(k)|^{2}+\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,x)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O(k)

\displaystyle\frac{(d_{x}\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,y)|_{x=y})(\mathcal{T})(d_{x}\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,y)|_{x=y})(V)}{||\kappa(k)|^{2}+\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,x)|^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O(k).

We conclude that there exist
C_{2}>0
such that
|L_{k}^{*}(\mathcal{H}^{F_{k}})(\mathcal{T},V)|=|k^{-3/2}\mathcal{H}^{F_{k}}(\mathcal{T},V)|\leq C_{2}k^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|V\|
holds for all
V\in H
and all
k\geq k_{0}
and
V\in H
. Furthermore, from Lemma
2.11
we also find that there exists a constant
C_{3}>0
such that

\displaystyle\left|\frac{(d_{x}\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,y)|_{x=y})(V_{1})(d_{x}\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,y)|_{x=y})(V_{2})}{||\kappa(k)|^{2}+\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,x)|^{2}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{3}|V_{1}||V_{2}|.

for all
k\geq k_{0}
, all
x\in X
and all
V_{1},V_{2}\in H_{x}
.
We define a symmetric bilinearform on
H
by
R_{k}=\{(R_{k})_{x}\}_{x\in X}
,
(R_{k})_{x}\colon H_{x}\times H_{x}\to\mathbb{C}
,

(R_{k})_{x}(V_{1},V_{2})=\frac{\text{Re}\langle V_{1}F_{k}(x),V_{2}F_{k}(x)\rangle}{|F_{k}(x)|^{2}}.

From Theorem
2.10
we find bilinearforms
r_{j}=\{(r_{j})_{x}\}_{x\in X}
,
(r_{j})_{x}\colon H_{x}\times H_{x}\to\mathbb{R}
,
j=0,1
, and a constant
C_{4}>0
such that

|R_{k}(V_{1},V_{2})-k^{2}r_{0}(V_{1},V_{2})-k^{1}r_{1}(V_{1},V_{2})|\leq C_{4}|V_{1}||V_{2}|

holds for all
k\geq k_{0}
,
x\in X
and
V_{1},V_{2}\in H_{x}
. From Lemma
2.11
we obtain
r_{0}\equiv 0
. So we will calculate
r_{1}
. Given
x\in X
and
V_{1},V_{2}\in H_{x}
we can find smooth sections
Z_{j}\in\Gamma(U,T^{1,0}X)
with
2\text{Re}(Z_{j})_{x}=V_{j}
,
j=1,2
defined in an open neighborhood
U
around
x
.
At
x\in X
we find

\langle V_{1}F_{k},V_{2}F_{k}\rangle=\langle Z_{1}F_{k},Z_{2}F_{k}\rangle=\overline{Z_{2}}(Z_{1}\langle F_{k},F_{k}\rangle)-\langle\overline{Z_{2}}(Z_{1}F_{k}),F_{k}\rangle=\overline{Z_{2}}(Z_{1}\langle F_{k},F_{k}\rangle)+\langle[Z_{1},\overline{Z_{2}}]F_{k},F_{k}\rangle.

Since

\displaystyle\frac{\overline{Z_{2}}(Z_{1}\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,x))}{|\kappa(k)|^{2}+\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,x)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O(1),

\displaystyle\frac{(d_{x}\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,y)|_{x=y})([Z_{1},\overline{Z_{2}}])}{|\kappa(k)|^{2}+\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,x)}-k\frac{\tau_{1}i}{\sigma_{P}(\xi)\tau_{0}}\xi([Z_{1},\overline{Z}_{2}])

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O(1),

by Lemma
2.11
, we find with
\xi([Z_{1},\overline{Z}_{2}])=-2i\mathcal{L}(Z_{1},Z_{2})
that
r_{1}(V_{1},V_{2})=\frac{2\tau_{1}}{\sigma_{P}(\xi)\tau_{0}}h(V_{1},V_{2})
. Hence we conclude that there exist a positive smooth function
b\colon X\to\mathbb{R}
and a constant
C_{5}>0
such that

|L_{k}^{*}(\mathcal{H}^{F_{k}})(V_{1},V_{2})+bh(V_{1},V_{2})|\leq C_{5}k^{-1}|V_{1}||V_{2}|

holds for all
k\geq k_{0}
and all
V_{1},V_{2}\in H_{x}
,
x\in X
. The statement follows.
∎

Corollary 3.7
.

There exists
k_{0}>0
such that
\mathcal{H}_{x}^{F_{k}}
is negative definite for all
x\in X
and
k\geq k_{0}
.

Lemma 3.8
.

With the assumptions and notation above there exists
k_{0}>0
such that for any
k\geq k_{0}
we have that
h^{F_{k}}(x,y)=1
if and only if
x=y
.

We will prove Lemma
3.8
by using that
h^{F_{k}}
is directly linked to the integral kernel of
\eta_{k}(T_{P})
. Let us recall this relation first.
We write
S_{k}(x,y)=\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,y)
and
