B_{k}(x)=\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,x)
. Note that there exists
C_{0}>0
such that
|S_{k}|\leq C_{0}k^{n+1}
and
C_{0}^{-1}\leq k^{-n-1}B_{k}\leq C_{0}
for all sufficiently large
k>0
. With

h^{F_{k}}(x,y)=\frac{||\kappa(k)|^{2}+S_{k}(x,y)|^{2}}{(|\kappa(k)|^{2}+B_{k}(x))(|\kappa(k)|^{2}+B_{k}(y))}=\frac{|\kappa(k)|^{4}+|\kappa(k)|^{2}(S_{k}(x,y)+\overline{S_{k}(x,y)})+|S_{k}|^{2}}{(|\kappa(k)|^{2}+B_{k}(x))(|\kappa(k)|^{2}+B_{k}(y))}

and using the assumptions on
\kappa
we find
k_{0}>0
and a constant
C>0
such that

(3.2.2)

\displaystyle\left|h^{F_{k}}(x,y)-\frac{|S_{k}(x,y)|^{2}}{B_{k}(y)B_{k}(x)}\right|\leq\frac{C}{k}

for all
k\geq k_{0}
and all
x,y\in X
.

Remark 3.9
.

The strategy for deducing results like Lemma
3.8
from Bergman and Szegő kernel asymptotics in a various kind of set-ups is due to Bouche
[
6
]
, Shiffman-Zelditch
[
48
]
, Ma-Marinescu
[
41
]
and Hsiao, Li
[
35
,
30
,
26
]
. The results in there are obtained via proof by contradiction arguments. In this work, we give a direct proof of Lemma
3.8
for the reason that we need to estimate integrals involving the function
h^{F_{k}}
(see Section
3.3
).

For the proof of Lemma
3.8
we will mainly work in local coordinates as follows.
Let
p\in X
be a point. Choose local coordinates
(x,y)
,
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{2n+1})=(x^{\prime},x_{2n+1})
,
y=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{2n+1})=(y^{\prime},y_{2n+1})
on an open neighborhood
D\times D\subset X\times X
around
(p,p)
as in Theorem
2.9
.
With these coordinates we identify
D
with an open neighborhood in
\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
such that
p
is identified with
0\in\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
.
In these coordinates we may write on
D\times D

(3.2.3)

\displaystyle S_{k}(x,y)=\int_{\delta_{1}}^{\delta_{2}}e^{ikt\varphi(x,y)}A(x,y,t,k)dt+O(k^{-\infty})

where
\varphi(x,y)
has the form (
2.2.20
),
T^{1,0}X
is locally given by (
2.2.19
) and
A(x,y,t,k)
satisfies (
2.3.2
). We then define

(3.2.4)

\displaystyle\tilde{S}_{k}(x,y)=\int_{\delta_{1}}^{\delta_{2}}e^{ikt\varphi(x,y)}A(x,y,t,k)dt

and find for
k_{0}>0
and
C>0
large enough that

(3.2.5)

\displaystyle\left|h^{F_{k}}(x,y)-\frac{|\tilde{S}_{k}(x,y)|^{2}}{B_{k}(y)B_{k}(x)}\right|\leq\frac{C}{k}

holds for all
k\geq k_{0}
and
x,y\in D
.

Recall that there exists
c>0
such that

(3.2.6)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Im}\varphi(x,y)\geq c|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|^{2}

for all
x,y\in D
. Furthermore, there exists
\hat{C}>0
such that

(3.2.7)

\displaystyle\left|\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\right)^{\beta}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}A(x,y,t,k)\right|\leq\hat{C}k^{n+1}

for all
k\geq 1
,
|\alpha|,\beta\leq 3
and all
x,y\in D
.
With these coordinates we will prove in three steps (see Lemma
3.10
, Lemma
3.11
and Lemma
3.14
) that Lemma
3.8
holds true near the diagonal in
X\times X
.

Lemma 3.10
.

There exist
C,k_{0}>0
such that
h^{F_{k}}(x,y)\leq\frac{1}{2}
for all
x,y\in D
with
|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|\geq\frac{C}{\sqrt{k}}
or
|\Re\varphi(x,y)|\geq\frac{C}{k}

Proof.

Choose
k_{0},C_{0}>1
such that (
3.2.5
),
|\tilde{S}_{k}|\leq C_{0}k^{n+1}
and
C_{0}^{-1}\leq k^{-n-1}B_{k}\leq C_{0}
hold for all
k\geq k_{0}
.
Assuming
|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|\geq\frac{C}{\sqrt{k}}
for some
C>0
we find

|\tilde{S}_{k}(x,y)|\leq\int_{\delta_{1}}^{\delta_{2}}e^{-ktc|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|^{2}}|A(x,y,t,k)|dt\leq e^{-\delta_{1}cC}(\delta_{2}-\delta_{1})\hat{C}k^{n+1}.

for all
k\geq k_{0}
.
Assuming
|\text{Re}\varphi(x,y)|\geq\frac{C}{k}
we find

(3.2.8)

\displaystyle|\tilde{S}_{k}(x,y)|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\delta_{1}}^{\delta_{2}}\frac{1}{ik\varphi(x,y)}e^{ikt\varphi(x,y)}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}A(x,y,t,k)dt\right|

(3.2.9)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{C}\int_{\delta_{1}}^{\delta_{2}}\left|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}A(x,y,t,k)\right|dt\leq\frac{\hat{C}}{C}(\delta_{2}-\delta_{1})k^{n+1}

for all
k\geq k_{0}
.
From
3.2.5
we conclude that there exist
C_{1},C_{2},k_{1}>0
such that

h^{F_{k}}(x,y)\leq C_{1}\max\{\frac{1}{C},e^{-\delta_{1}cC}\}+\frac{C_{2}}{k}

for all
C>0
,
x,y\in D
with
|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|\geq\frac{C}{\sqrt{k}}
or
|\Re\varphi(x,y)|\geq\frac{C}{k}
and all
k\geq k_{1}
.
The statement follows from taking
C,k_{1}
large enough.
∎

We are going to prove now a stronger version of Lemma
3.10
.

Lemma 3.11
.

There exists an open neighborhood
U\subset D
around zero such that for all
\varepsilon>0
there exist
\delta,k_{0}>0
with
h^{F_{k}}(x,y)\leq 1-\delta
for all
k\geq k_{0}
and
x,y\in U
satisfying
|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{k}}
or
|\Re\varphi(x,y)|\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{k}
.

For the proof of Lemma
3.11
we need the following.

Lemma 3.12
.

There exists an open neighborhood
U\subset D
around zero and
C>0
such that

|x_{2n+1}-y_{2n+1}|\leq C(|\operatorname{Re}\varphi(x,y)|+|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|)

and

\operatorname{Im}\varphi(x,y)\leq C(|\operatorname{Re}\varphi(x,y)|^{2}+|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|^{2})

for all
x,y\in U
.

Proof.

By Taylor expansion of
\text{Re}\varphi
at
x=y
in the variable
x
we obtain

|\text{Re}\varphi(x,y)-(d_{x}\text{Re}\varphi)_{x=y}(x-y)|\leq C_{1}(|x_{2n+1}-y_{2n+1}|^{2}+|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|^{2})

for some constant
C_{1}>0
for all
x,y\in D
.
Hence with

|(d_{x^{\prime}}\text{Re}\varphi)_{x=y}(x^{\prime}-y^{\prime})|\leq C_{2}|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|

for some constant
C_{2}>0
we obtain

|x_{2n+1}-y_{2n+1}|\left(\left|\frac{\partial\text{Re}\varphi}{\partial x_{2n+1}}(y,y)\right|-C_{1}|x_{2n+1}-y_{2n+1}|\right)\leq|\text{Re}\varphi(x,y)|+|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|(C_{2}+C_{1}|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|).

Since
\frac{\partial\text{Re}\varphi}{\partial x_{2n+1}}(0,0)\neq 0
the first part of the claim follows from taking
U
small enough.
In order to prove the second part we first observe that since
\text{Im}\varphi\geq 0
and
\text{Im}\varphi(y,y)=0
we have
(d_{x}\Im\varphi)_{x=y}=0
. Then it follows from Taylor expansion of
\text{Im}\varphi
at
x=y
in the variable
x
that there exists
C_{3}>0
with

\text{Im}\varphi(x,y)\leq C_{3}|x-y|^{2}

for all
x,y\in U
. Since
|x-y|^{2}=|x_{2n+1}-y_{2n+1}|^{2}+|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|^{2}
the claim follows from the already proven first part.
∎

Lemma 3.13
.

Let
\rho\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a smooth, non-negative compactly supported function with
\rho\not\equiv 0
. Given
C_{1},C_{2},\varepsilon>0
there exist
\delta>0
such that

\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i\lambda t}e^{-t\tau}\rho(t)dt\right|\leq(1-\delta)\int_{\mathbb{R}}\rho(t)dt

for all
\varepsilon\leq\lambda\leq C_{1}
and all
0\leq\tau\leq C_{2}
.

Proof.

By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,
e^{-\tau t}\leq 1
and since
t\mapsto e^{i\lambda t}
and
t\mapsto 1
are linearly independent for
\lambda>0
we have

\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{it\lambda}e^{-t\tau}\rho(t)dt\right|<\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\rho(t)dt\right|=:C_{0}

for all
\lambda>0
and
\tau\geq 0
.
Hence we have for the continuous function
f(\lambda,\tau):=C_{0}^{-1}|\int_{R}e^{it\lambda}e^{-t\tau}\rho(t)dt|
that
f(\lambda,\tau)<1
for all
\lambda>0
and
\tau\geq 0
. Since
[\varepsilon,C_{1}]\times[0,C_{2}]
is compact we find
\delta>0
such that
f(\lambda,\tau)\leq 1-\delta
for all
(\lambda,\tau)\in[\varepsilon,C_{1}]\times[0,C_{2}]
. The claim follows.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.11
.

From Lemma
3.12
it follows that after shrinking
D
there exists a constant
C_{0}>0
such that

(3.2.10)

\displaystyle|x-y|\leq C_{0}(|\text{Re}\varphi(x,y)|+|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|)

and

(3.2.11)

\displaystyle\Im\varphi(x,y)\leq C_{0}(|\operatorname{Re}\varphi(x,y)|^{2}+|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|^{2})

for all
x,y\in D
.
For
C,k>0
put

U_{k}:=\left\{(x,y)\in D\times D\mid|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{k}}\text{ and }|\text{Re}\varphi(x,y)|\leq\frac{C}{k}\right\}

Taking
C,k_{0}>0
large enough it follows from Lemma
3.10
that we only need to consider
(x,y)\in U_{k}
for
k\geq k_{0}
.
We conclude that there exists
C_{1}>0
such that

|x-y|\leq\frac{C_{1}}{\sqrt{k}}

for all
(x,y)\in U_{k}
and all
k\geq k_{0}
. Since
|A_{0}(x,y,t)-A_{0}(y,y,t)|\leq C_{2}|x-y|
for some constant
C_{2}>0
we find that there exists
C_{3}>0
such that

|A(x,y,t,k)-A_{0}(y,y,t)|\leq C_{3}k^{n+\frac{1}{2}}

or all
(x,y)\in U_{k}
and all
k\geq k_{0}
. Let
\varepsilon>0
be arbitrary.
Assuming
(x,y)\in U_{k}
with
|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{k}}
we find

k^{-n-1}|\tilde{S}_{k}(x,y)|\leq e^{-\delta_{1}c\varepsilon}\int_{\delta_{1}}^{\delta_{2}}|A_{0}(y,y,t)|dt+\frac{C_{4}}{\sqrt{k}}

for all
k\geq k_{0}
where
C_{4}>0
is a constant independent of
x,y,k
.
With
A_{0}(y,y,t)\geq 0
we recall that by Theorem
2.10
there exists
C_{5}>0
such that

\left|k^{-1-n}B_{k}(y)-\int_{\delta_{1}}^{\delta_{2}}|A_{0}(y,y,t)|dt\right|\leq C_{5}k^{-1}

for all sufficiently large
k
and all
y\in D
.
Then from (
3.2.5
) we conclude that there exist
C_{6},k_{1}>0
such that

(3.2.12)

\displaystyle h^{F_{k}}(x,y)\leq e^{-2\delta_{1}c\varepsilon}+\frac{C_{6}}{\sqrt{k}}

for all
(x,y)\in U_{k}
with
|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{k}}
and all
k\geq k_{1}
.
Now assume
|\text{Re}(\varphi(x,y))|\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{k}
.
We find that there exists a constant
C_{7}>0
such that

k^{-1-n}|\tilde{S}_{k}(x,y)|\leq\left|\int_{\delta_{1}}^{\delta_{2}}e^{itk\text{Re}\varphi(x,y)}e^{-tk\text{Im}\varphi(x,y)}A_{0}(y,y,t)\right|+\frac{C_{7}}{\sqrt{k}}

for all
(x,y)\in U_{k}
and all sufficiently large
k
. We have
A_{0}(y,y,t)=a(y)|\chi(t)|^{2}
for some positive function
a
. Since
D
was chosen small enough by (
3.2.10
) and (
3.2.11
) we can choose a constant
\tilde{C}>0
such that
\varepsilon\leq k|\text{Re}\varphi(x,y)|\leq C
and
0\leq k\text{Im}(x,y)\leq\tilde{C}
for all
k\geq 1
and all
(x,y)\in U_{k}
with
|\text{Re}\varphi(x,y)|\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{k}
. We obtain from Lemma
3.13
with
\lambda=k|\text{Re}\varphi(x,y)|
,
\tau=k\text{Im}\varphi(x,y)
and
\rho(t)=|\chi(t)|^{2}
that there exists
\delta>0
such that

k^{-1-n}|\tilde{S}_{k}(x,y)|\leq(1-\delta)\int_{\delta_{1}}^{\delta_{2}}A_{0}(y,y,t)+\frac{C_{7}}{\sqrt{k}}

for all
(x,y)\in U_{k}
with
|\Re\varphi(x,y)|\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{k}
and all sufficiently large
k
. With (
3.2.5
) we conclude that there are
C_{8},k_{2}>0
such that

(3.2.13)

\displaystyle h^{F_{k}}(x,y)\leq(1-\delta)^{2}+\frac{C_{8}}{\sqrt{k}}.

for all
(x,y)\in U_{k}
with
|\Re\varphi(x,y)|\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{k}
and all
k\geq k_{2}
.
Hence taking
k_{0}
large enough the claim follows from (
3.2.12
) and (
3.2.13
).
∎

Lemma 3.14
.

There exist an open neighborhood
U\subset D
around zero and
\varepsilon,\delta,k_{0}>0
such that

h^{F_{k}}(x,y)\leq 1-\delta(k^{2}(\Re\varphi(x,y))^{2}+k|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|^{2})

for all
x,y\in U
with
|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{k}}
and
|\Re\varphi(x,y)|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{k}
and all
k\geq k_{0}
.

Proof.

For
y\in D
fixed consider the map defined by
Q_{y}\colon D\to\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}

Q_{y}(x)=(x^{\prime}-y^{\prime},\text{Re}\varphi(x,y))
. Since
\frac{\partial\text{Re}\varphi}{\partial x_{2n+1}}(0,0)\neq 0
we find by shrinking
D
that
Q_{y}
defines a diffeomorphism on its image for all
y\in D
. We find that there exist open neighborhoods
U,V\subset\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
around zero such that
U\subset Q_{y}(D)
for all
y\in V
. Then we introduce new coordinates
\hat{x}=Q_{y}(x)
. For this coordinates we find
\varphi\circ Q_{y}^{-1}(\hat{x})=\hat{x}_{2n+1}+i\psi_{y}(\tilde{x})
,
\hat{x}\in U
. For some smooth real function
\psi_{y}
with
\psi_{y}\geq 0
,
\psi_{y}(0)=0
. It follows that there exists a constant
C_{1}>0
such that

(3.2.14)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\partial\psi_{y}}{\partial\hat{x}_{j}}(\hat{x})\right|\leq C_{1}|\hat{x}|

for all
1\leq j\leq 2n+1
and all
y\in V
,
\hat{x}\in U
. Define
L_{k}(\hat{x}):=(\sqrt{k}\hat{x}^{\prime},k\hat{x}_{2n+1})
and consider the coordinates
\tilde{x}=L_{k}(\hat{x})
. Put
\tilde{h}_{y,k}(\tilde{x}):=h^{F_{k}}(Q_{y}^{-1}L_{k}^{-1}(\tilde{x}),y)
. We are interested in the Taylor expansion of
\tilde{h}_{y,k}
at
\tilde{x}=0
. Therefore, we first show that all its derivatives up to order three are uniformly bounded in
k
. Note that there exists
C_{2}>0
such that

(3.2.15)

\displaystyle|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}A(x,y,t,k)|\leq C_{2}k^{n+1}

and

(3.2.16)

\displaystyle|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}B_{k}(x)|\leq C_{2}k^{n+1}

for all
x,y\in D
,
k\geq 1
and
|\alpha|\leq 3
. Then by the definition of
h^{F_{k}}
we find that the only way to increase the growth in
k
of its derivatives are derivatives acting on
e^{ikt\varphi}
.
Given
1\leq j\leq 2n
we have

\left|\frac{\partial\varphi\circ Q_{y}^{-1}}{\partial\hat{x_{j}}}(\hat{x})\right|=\left|\frac{\partial\psi_{y}}{\partial\hat{x}_{j}}(\hat{x})\right|\leq C_{1}|\hat{x}|

and hence

\left|e^{-ikt\varphi\circ(L_{k}\circ Q_{y})^{-1}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{x}_{j}}e^{ikt\varphi\circ(L_{k}\circ Q_{y})^{-1}}(\hat{x})\right|=\frac{tk}{\sqrt{k}}\left|\frac{\partial\psi_{y}}{\partial\hat{x_{j}}}(L_{k}^{-1}(\tilde{x}))\right|\leq\sqrt{k}tC_{1}|L_{k}^{-1}(\tilde{x})|\leq C_{1}\delta_{2}|\tilde{x}|

for all
y\in V
and
k\geq 1
.
Furthermore, we have

\left|e^{-ikt\varphi\circ(L_{k}\circ Q_{y})^{-1}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{x}_{2n+1}}e^{ikt\varphi\circ(L_{k}\circ Q_{y})^{-1}}(\hat{x})\right|=\frac{tk}{k}\left|1+\frac{\partial\psi_{y}}{\partial\hat{x_{2n+1}}}(L_{k}^{-1}(\tilde{x}))\right|\leq\delta_{2}(1+C_{1}|L_{k}^{-1}(\tilde{x})|).

It follows that there exist
C_{3},k_{0}>0
and a ball
\tilde{B}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
centered in zero such that

|\partial^{\alpha}_{\tilde{x}}\tilde{h}_{k,y}(\tilde{x})|\leq C_{3}

for all
y\in V
,
k\geq k_{0}
,
|\alpha|\leq 3
and
\tilde{x}\in\tilde{B}
.
Since
\tilde{h}_{k,y}
has a maximum at
\tilde{x}=0
, Taylor expansion of
\tilde{h}_{k,y}
at
\tilde{x}=0
yields

\tilde{h}_{k,y}(\tilde{x})=1+\tilde{x}^{T}M_{k,y}\tilde{x}+R_{k,y}(\tilde{x})

where
M_{k,y}
is a symmetric
(2n+1)\times(2n+1)
matrix and we have
|R_{k,y}(\tilde{x})|\leq C_{4}|\tilde{x}|^{3}
for some constant
C_{4}>0
independent of
k\geq k_{0}
,
y\in V
and
\tilde{x}\in\tilde{B}
.
Using the definition of
\tilde{h}_{k,y}
and the fact that
S_{k}
has an asymptotic expansion we further obtain that there exists
C_{5}>0
and for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{2n+1}
,
|\alpha|\leq 3
, a smooth function
r_{\alpha}\colon U\times\tilde{B}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

\left|\partial^{\alpha}_{\tilde{x}}\tilde{h}_{k,y}(\tilde{x})-r_{\alpha}(\tilde{x},y)\right|\leq\frac{C_{5}}{\sqrt{k}}.

Hence we can write
M_{k,y}=M_{y}+\tilde{M}_{k,y}
and there exists a constant
C_{6}>0
such that
|\tilde{x}^{T}\tilde{M}_{k,y}\tilde{x}|\leq\frac{C_{6}}{\sqrt{k}}|\tilde{x}|^{2}
for all
y\in V
,
k\geq k_{0}
and
\tilde{x}\in\tilde{B}
. We will now show that
M_{y}
is negative definite when
y
is sufficiently close to zero. Therefore we note that

dQ_{y}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&\ldots&0\\
0&1&\ldots&0\\
\dots&\dots&\dots&\dots\\
\frac{\partial\text{Re}(\varphi)}{\partial x_{1}}&\frac{\partial\text{Re}(\varphi)}{\partial x_{2}}&\ldots&\frac{\partial\text{Re}(\varphi)}{\partial x_{2n+1}}\end{pmatrix}\text{ and }dQ^{-1}_{y}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&\ldots&0\\
0&1&\ldots&0\\
\dots&\dots&\dots&\dots\\
-\frac{\frac{\partial\text{Re}(\varphi)}{\partial x_{1}}}{\frac{\partial\text{Re}(\varphi)}{\partial x_{2n+1}}}&-\frac{\frac{\partial\text{Re}(\varphi)}{\partial x_{2}}}{\frac{\partial\text{Re}(\varphi)}{\partial x_{2n+1}}}&\ldots&\frac{1}{\frac{\partial\text{Re}(\varphi)}{\partial x_{2n+1}}}.\end{pmatrix}

Hence we find

\frac{\partial}{\partial\hat{x}_{j}}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}-\left(\frac{\partial\text{Re}(\varphi)}{\partial x_{2n+1}}\right)^{-1}\frac{\partial\text{Re}(\varphi)}{\partial x_{1}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{2n+1}}

for
1\leq j\leq 2n
and

\frac{\partial}{\partial\hat{x}_{2n+1}}=\left(\frac{\partial\text{Re}(\varphi)}{\partial x_{2n+1}}\right)^{-1}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{2n+1}}.

By the choice of coordinates
x,y
(see Theorem
2.9
) we have
\mathcal{T}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{2n+1}}
and at
x=y=0
we have that
\{\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\}_{1\leq j\leq 2n}
is a basis of
\Re(T^{1,0}_{p}X)
. Furthermore, we have
\frac{\partial\Re\varphi}{\partial x_{j}}(0,0)=0
for all
1\leq j\leq 2n
. It follows that for
y=0
and
\hat{x}=0
we have that
\{\frac{\partial}{\partial\hat{x}_{j}}\}_{1\leq j\leq 2n}
is a basis of
\Re(T^{1,0}_{p}X)
and
\frac{\partial}{\partial\hat{x}_{2n+1}}
is a non-zero multiple of
\mathcal{T}
. Let
\hat{M}_{k}
be the matrix representation of
\mathcal{H}^{F_{k}}_{p}
in the coordinates
\hat{x}
. By Lemma
3.2
we have that
\mathcal{H}^{F_{k}}_{p}
is the Hessian of the function
g_{p}
defined by
g_{p}(q):=h^{F_{k}}(p,q)
. From the definition of
\tilde{h}_{k,y}
we observe that
M_{0,k}=L_{k}^{*}(\hat{M}_{k})
. Then it follows from Lemma
3.6
that
M_{0}
is negative definite. We conclude that by shrinking
V
we can achieve that
M_{y}
is negative definite for all
y\in V
. Then there exist
\delta,k_{2},\varepsilon>0
such that

\tilde{h}_{k,y}(\tilde{x})\leq 1-\delta|\tilde{x}|^{2}

for all
k\geq k_{2}
, all
y\in V
and all
\tilde{x}\in\tilde{B}
with
\|\tilde{x}\|\leq\varepsilon
. The claim follows from

h^{F_{k}}(x,y)=\tilde{h}_{k,y}(L_{k}\circ Q_{y}(x))\leq 1-\delta(k^{2}(\Re\varphi(x,y))^{2}+k|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|^{2}).

∎

Now we are ready to prove Lemma
3.8
.

Proof of Lemma
3.8
.

By the definition of
h^{F_{k}}
it follows that
h^{F_{k}}(x,x)=1
. So we need to show that for
k
large enough we have that
h^{F_{k}}(x,y)=1
implies
x=y
.
We first observe that given an open neighborhood
W
around the diagonal in
X\times X
we find by Theorem
2.10
and (
3.2.2
) that there exists
k_{1}>0
such that
h^{F_{k}}(x,y)\leq\frac{1}{2}
for all
(x,y)\not\in W
. From this observation and the compactness of
X
we need to show that given any point
p\in X
we can find an open neighborhood
D\times D
of
(p,p)
in
X\times X
and
k_{0}>0
such that for all
x,y\in D
and all
k\geq k_{0}
we have that
h^{F_{k}}(x,y)=1
implies
x=y
. Let
p\in X
be a point. We choose coordinates
(x,y)=(x^{\prime},x_{2n+1},y^{\prime},y_{2n+1})
around
(p,p)
as above. Recall that in these coordinates we have
\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,y)=:S_{k}(x,y)=\int_{\delta_{1}}^{\delta_{2}}e^{ikt\varphi(x,y)}A(x,y,t,k)dt+O(k^{-\infty})
. We can apply Lemma
3.12
, Lemma
3.11
and Lemma
3.14
to achieve after shrinking
D
that there exists
\varepsilon,\delta,k_{2},C,c>0
such that for all
x,y\in D
and all
k\geq k_{2}
we have that
|x-y|\leq C(|\text{Re}\varphi(x,y)|+|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|)
and

h^{F_{k}}(x,y)\leq\begin{cases}1-\delta,&\text{ if }|\text{Re}\varphi(x,y)|\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{k}\text{ or }|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{k}},\\
1-c(k^{2}(\text{Re}\varphi(x,y))^{2}+k|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|^{2}),&\text{ else.}\end{cases}

Hence given
k\geq k_{2}
and
x,y\in D
with
h^{F_{k}}(x,y)=1
we find
|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|=|\text{Re}\varphi(x,y)|=0
which leads to
|x-y|=0
that is
x=y
.
∎

3.3.
Integral Estimates for
h^{F_{k}}
and
\chi_{k}(T_{P})

We end this section by proving two integral estimates involving
S_{k}
and
h^{F_{k}}
which will be useful for later applications.

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
dV
be a volume form on
X\times X
. We have that
\left(\sqrt{1-h^{F_{k}}}\right)^{-1}
is integrable for all sufficiently large
k
and there exist
k_{0},\delta,C>0
such that

(3.3.1)

\displaystyle\int_{X\times X}\frac{|\psi|}{\sqrt{1-h^{F_{k}}}}dV\leq C\left(k^{-1-n}\operatorname{sup}|\psi|+\int_{h^{F_{k}}\leq 1-\delta}|\psi|dV\right)

holds for all
k\geq k_{0}
and all bounded measurable functions
\psi\colon X\times X\to\mathbb{C}
.

Proof.

We note that since
X\times X
is compact we have for any bounded measurable function
\psi\colon X\times X\to\mathbb{C}
that
\psi\in L^{1}(X\times X)
.
First, we observe that given an open neighborhood
W
around the diagonal in
X\times X
we find by Theorem
2.10
that there exists
k_{1}>0
such that
h^{F_{k}}(x,y)\leq\frac{1}{2}
for all
k\geq k_{1}
and all
(x,y)\not\in W
. Hence

\int_{X\times X}\frac{|\psi|}{\sqrt{1-h^{F_{k}}}}dV\leq\sqrt{2}\int_{h^{F_{k}}\leq\frac{1}{2}}|\psi|dV

for all
k\geq k_{1}
and all
\psi\in L^{1}(X\times X)
with
\text{supp}(\psi)\cap W=\emptyset
. From this observation and the compactness of
X
we just need to show that given any point
p\in X
we can find an open neighborhood
D\times D
around
(p,p)
and
k_{0},\delta,C>0
such that (
3.3.1
) holds for all
k\geq k_{0}
and all bounded, measurable functions
\psi\colon X\times X\to\mathbb{C}
with
\operatorname{supp}(\psi)\subset D\times D
.
Let
p\in X
be a point. We choose coordinates
(x,y)=(x^{\prime},x_{2n+1},y^{\prime},y_{2n+1})
around
(p,p)
as above. Denote the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
by
dx
or
dy
respectively. Recall that in these coordinates we have
\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,y)=S_{k}(x,y)=\int_{\delta_{1}}^{\delta_{2}}e^{ikt\varphi(x,y)}A(x,y,t,k)dt+O(k^{-\infty})
. As in the proof of Lemma
3.14
, after shrinking
D
, we can find open subsets
V,U\subset\subset D
around zero such that for any
y\in V
we can introduce new coordinates
\hat{x}=Q_{y}(x)
by
\hat{x}^{\prime}=x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}
and
\hat{x}_{2n+1}=\text{Re}\varphi(x,y)
with
U\subset\{(x^{\prime}-y^{\prime},\text{Re}\varphi(x,y))\mid x\in D\}
. For
\varepsilon>0
and
k>0
put

U_{k}:=\{\hat{x}\in U\mid k^{2}|\hat{x}_{2n+1}|^{2}+k|\hat{x}^{\prime}|^{2}\geq\varepsilon^{2}\}.

We can apply Lemma
3.11
and Lemma
3.14
to achieve after shrinking
V
and choosing
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small that there exist
\delta,k_{2},C,c>0
such that for
y\in V
and
\hat{x}\in U
we have

h^{F_{k}}(Q_{y}^{-1}(\hat{x}),y)\leq\begin{cases}1-\delta,&\text{ if }\hat{x}\in U_{k}\\
1-c(k^{2}(\hat{x}_{2n+1})^{2}+k|\hat{x}^{\prime}|^{2}),&\text{ else.}\end{cases}

Then

\int_{{U_{k}}}\frac{|\psi(\hat{x},y)|}{\sqrt{1-h^{F_{k}}(Q^{-1}_{y}(\hat{x}),y)}}d\hat{x}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}\int_{\{\hat{h}_{k,y}\leq 1-\delta\}}|\psi(\hat{x},y)|d\hat{x}

for all
k\geq k_{2}
,
y\in V
and
\psi\in L^{1}(U\times V)
where
\hat{h}_{k,y}(\hat{x}):=h^{F_{k}}(Q_{y}^{-1}(\hat{x}),y)
.
Using coordinates
\tilde{x}
by
\tilde{x}^{\prime}=\sqrt{k}\hat{x}^{\prime}
and
\tilde{x}_{2n+1}=k\hat{x}_{2n+1}
we find since
d\hat{x}=k^{-1-n}d\tilde{x}
that

\int_{U\setminus U_{k}}\frac{|\psi(\hat{x},y)|}{\sqrt{1-h^{F_{k}}(Q^{-1}_{y}(\hat{x}),y)}}d\hat{x}\leq(k)^{-1-n}\frac{\tilde{C}}{\sqrt{c}}\sup|\psi|.

for all
k\geq k_{2}
,
y\in V
and all bounded measurable functions
\psi\colon U\times V\to\mathbb{C}
where
\tilde{C}:=\int_{|\tilde{x}|<\varepsilon}\frac{d\tilde{x}}{|\tilde{x}|}<\infty
. We conclude that there exists a constant
C_{0}>0
such that

(3.3.2)

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\int_{U\times V}\frac{|\psi(\hat{x},y)|}{\sqrt{1-h^{F_{k}}(Q^{-1}_{y}(\hat{x}),y)}}d\hat{x}\otimes dy\leq C_{0}\left(k^{-1-n}\sup|\psi|+\int_{V}\left(\int_{\hat{h}_{k,y}\leq 1-\delta}|\psi(\hat{x},y)|d\hat{x}\right)dy\right)

for all
k\geq k_{2}
and all bounded measurable functions
\psi\colon U\times V\to\mathbb{C}
. We can find an open neighborhood
\tilde{D}\subset V
around zero such that
\tilde{D}\subset Q_{y}^{-1}(U)
for all
y\in\tilde{D}
. Furthermore, given
x,y\in\tilde{D}
with
\hat{h}_{k,y}(Q_{y}(x))\leq 1-\delta
we find
h^{F_{k}}(x,y)\leq 1-\delta
. Since
\tilde{D}\times\tilde{D}
is identified with an open neighborhood of
X\times X
around
(p,p)
it follows from (
3.3.2
) that there exist
C,k_{0}>0
such that

\int_{X\times X}\frac{|\psi|}{\sqrt{1-h^{F_{k}}}}dV\leq C\left(k^{-1-n}\sup|\psi|+\int_{h^{F_{k}}\leq 1-\delta}|\psi|dV\right)

for all
k\geq k_{0}
and all bounded measurable functions
\psi\colon X\times X\to\mathbb{C}
with
\operatorname{supp}(\psi)\subset\tilde{D}\times\tilde{D}
.
∎

Lemma 3.16
.

Let
L\colon\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(X\times X)\to\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(X\times X)
be a partial differential operator of order less or equal than
d\geq 0
and let
dV
be a volume form on
X\times X
. Put
S_{k}(x,y):=\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,y)
. Given
0<\varepsilon<1
there exist
C,k_{0}>0
such that

\int_{X\times X}|LS_{k}|dV\leq Ck^{d+\varepsilon}

for all
k\geq k_{0}
.

Proof.

We first observe that given an open neighborhood
W
around the diagonal in
X\times X
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
we find by Theorem
2.10
that there exists
k_{1}>0
such that
|LS_{k}|\leq k^{-N}
for all
k\geq k_{1}
and all
(x,y)\not\in W
. Hence

\int_{X\times X\setminus W}|LS_{k}|dV\leq k^{-N}\int_{X\times X}dV

for all
k\geq k_{1}
. From this observation and the compactness of
X
we just need to show that given any point
p\in X
we can find an open neighborhood
D\times D
around
(p,p)
and
k_{0},C>0
such that

\int_{D\times D}|LS_{k}|dV\leq Ck^{d+\varepsilon}

holds for all
k\geq k_{0}
.
Let
p\in X
be a point. We choose coordinates
(x,y)=(x^{\prime},x_{2n+1},y^{\prime},y_{2n+1})
on
D\times D
around
(p,p)
as above. Denote the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
by
dx
or
dy
respectively. Recall that in these coordinates we have
S_{k}(x,y)=\int_{\delta_{1}}^{\delta_{2}}e^{ikt\varphi(x,y)}A(x,y,t,k)dt+O(k^{-\infty})
. It follows immediately that

(LS_{k})(x,y)=\int_{\delta_{1}}^{\delta_{2}}e^{ikt\varphi(x,y)}A^{L}(x,y,t,k)dt+O(k^{-\infty})

where
A^{L}(x,y,t,k)\sim\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}A^{L}_{j}(x,y,t)k^{n+1+d-j}
in
S^{n+1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(1;D\times D\times(\delta_{1},\delta_{2}))
has an asymptotic expansion. Hence for any
m\geq 0
we find
\hat{C}_{m}>0
such that

\left|\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\right)^{m}A^{L}(x,y,k,t)\right|\leq\hat{C}_{m}k^{d+n+1}.

Put

S^{L}_{k}(x,y):=\int_{\delta_{1}}^{\delta_{2}}e^{ikt\varphi(x,y)}A^{L}(x,y,t,k)dt

for
x,y\in D
.
As in the proof of Lemma
3.14
, after shrinking
D
, we can find open subsets
V,U\subset D
around zero such that for any
y\in V
we can introduce new coordinates
\hat{x}=Q_{y}(x)
by
\hat{x}^{\prime}=x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}
and
\hat{x}_{2n+1}=\text{Re}\varphi(x,y)
with
U\subset\{(x^{\prime}-y^{\prime},\text{Re}\varphi(x,y))\mid x\in D\}
. Furthermore, we can achieve that
U
is of the form
U=(-r,r)\times U^{\prime}
for some
r>0
. Choose
k_{1}>1
with
k_{1}^{\varepsilon-1}<r
.
For
k>k_{1}
put
U_{k}:=(-k^{\varepsilon-1},k^{\varepsilon-1})\times U^{\prime}
. Recall that
\text{Im}\varphi(x,y)\geq c|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|
for some
c>0
.
Since
|S^{L}_{k}(x,y)|\leq k^{n+1+d}\hat{C}_{0}e^{-\delta_{1}c|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|}

it follows that

(3.3.3)

\displaystyle\int_{U_{k}}|S^{L}_{k}(Q_{y}^{-1}(\hat{x}),y)|d\hat{x}\leq\hat{C}_{0}k^{d+n+1}2k^{\varepsilon-1}\int_{U^{\prime}}e^{-c\delta_{1}k|\hat{x}^{\prime}|^{2}}d\hat{x}^{\prime}.

With
\int_{R^{2n}}e^{-c\delta_{1}k|x|^{2}}dx=\pi^{n}(c\delta_{1}k)^{-n}
it follows that there exist
C_{1},k_{2}>0
such that

\int_{U_{k}}|LS_{k}(Q_{y}^{-1}(\hat{x},y))|d\hat{x}\leq C_{1}k^{d+\varepsilon}

for all
y\in V
and
k\geq k_{2}
.
Recall that for all
x,y\in D
and
k
we have that
\text{supp}(A^{L}(x,y,\cdot,k))\subset(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})
.
Then for
\text{Re}\varphi(x,y)\neq 0
we find

|(S^{L}_{k})(x,y)|=\left|\frac{1}{(ik\varphi(x,y))^{m}}\int_{\delta_{1}}^{\delta_{2}}e^{ikt\varphi(x,y)}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\right)^{m}A^{L}(x,y,t,k)dt\right|\leq\frac{\hat{C}_{m}}{(k|\text{Re}\varphi(x,y)|)^{m}}

We obtain
|(S^{L}_{k})(Q_{y}^{-1}(\hat{x}),y)|\leq\hat{C}_{m}k^{-m\varepsilon}

for all
y\in V
,
\hat{x}\in U\setminus U_{k}
,
k\geq k_{1}
. Choosing
m\geq\frac{n+1}{\varepsilon}
we conclude

\int_{U\setminus U_{k}}|S^{L}_{k}(Q_{y}^{-1}(\hat{x}),y)|d\hat{x}\leq\hat{C}_{m}k^{d}2r\int_{U^{\prime}}d\hat{x}^{\prime}

for all
y\in V
and all
k\geq k_{1}
. It follows that there exist
k_{3},C_{2}>0
such that

\int_{V\times U}|LS_{k}(Q_{y}^{-1}(\hat{x}),y)|d\hat{x}\otimes dy\leq C_{2}k^{d+\varepsilon}

for all
k\geq k_{3}
. Since we can find an open neighborhood
\tilde{D}\subset V
around zero such that
\tilde{D}\subset Q_{y}^{-1}(U)
for all
y\in\tilde{D}
and since
\tilde{D}\times\tilde{D}
is identified with an open neighborhood of
X\times X
around
(p,p)
we have that there exist
C,k_{0}>0
such that

\int_{\tilde{D}\times\tilde{D}}|LS_{k}|dV\leq Ck^{d+\varepsilon}

for all
k\geq k_{0}
.
∎

4.
Lelong-Poincaré Formula for Domains with Boundary

Let
Y
be a complex manifold of dimension
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y=n+1
. Let
G\subset Y
be a domain with smooth boundary
bG
and denote by
\iota\colon bG\to Y
the inclusion map. We have that
bG
is a codimension one CR manifold with CR structure
T^{1,0}bG:=\mathbb{C}TbG\cap T^{1,0}Y
where
T^{1,0}Y
denotes the complex structure of
Y
. By
\Omega_{c}^{q}(\overline{G})
and
\Omega_{c}^{p,q}(\overline{G})
we denote the space of smooth
p
- and
(p,q)
-forms with compact support in
\overline{G}
. In particular, the elements in
\Omega_{c}^{q}(\overline{G})
and
\Omega_{c}^{p,q}(\overline{G})
are restrictions of the elements in
\Omega_{c}^{q}(Y)
and
\Omega_{c}^{p,q}(Y)
to
\overline{G}
. The aim of this section is to prove a version of the Lelong-Poincaré formula for test forms in
\Omega_{c}^{p,q}(\overline{G})
and holomorphic functions on
G
which are smooth up to the boundary
bG
(see Theorem
4.14
). More precisely, we want to show that for any holomorphic function
f\in\mathcal{O}(G)\cap\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\overline{G})
which is regular with respect to
bG
(see Definition
4.1
) and any
\psi\in\Omega_{c}^{n,n}(\overline{G})
we have

(4.0.1)

\displaystyle\left(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right],\psi\right)=\frac{i}{\pi}\int_{G}\partial\overline{\partial}\log(|f|)\wedge\psi

where
\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right]
is the zero divisor of
f
. We note that this formula is well-known for
\psi\in\Omega_{c}^{n,n}(G)
where the right-hand side is defined by
\frac{i}{\pi}\int_{G}\log(|f|)\wedge\partial\overline{\partial}\psi
. When
\psi
does not vanish along
bG
the definition of the left-hand side requires the understanding of the zero set of
f
near and on the boundary (see Remark
4.15
). Furthermore, the definition of the right-hand side will involve integrals on the boundary of
G
(see Definition
4.1
). In the end of this section we will prove two results (see Theorem
4.19
and Theorem
4.21
) regarding the zero sets of CR functions on strictly pseudoconvex CR manifolds.

4.1.
Existence of Some Integrals

We start with the following definition.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{O}(G)\cap\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\overline{G})
be a holomorphic function on
G
which is smooth up to the boundary. Given
p\in bG
with
f(p)=0
we say that
f
is regular at
p
with respect to
bG
if
\partial f_{p}\neq 0
and at least one of the following conditions is satisfied

(i)

\ker\partial f_{p}\cap T^{1,0}_{p}Y+T^{1,0}_{p}bG=T^{1,0}_{p}Y
,

(ii)

The Levi form of
bG
at
p
does not vanish.

We say that
f
is regular with respect to
bG
if
f
is regular at
p
with respect to
bG
at any point
p\in bG
with
f(p)=0
.

We note that the definition above does not dependent on the choice of Levi form at
p\in bG
.

Example 4.2
.

Put
Y=\mathbb{C}^{2}
and
G=\{z\in Y\mid\text{Re}(z_{1})<0\}
. Then
G
is a domain with smooth boundary. Let
f\in\mathcal{O}(G)\cap\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\overline{G})
be the holomorphic function defined by
f(z)=z_{1}
. It follows that
f
is not regular with respect to
bG
.

Example 4.3
.

Put
Y=\mathbb{C}^{2}
and
G=\{z\in Y\mid|z_{1}|^{2}+|z_{2}|^{2}<1\}
. Then
G
is a domain with smooth boundary. Let
f\in\mathcal{O}(G)\cap\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\overline{G})
be the holomorphic function defined by
f(z)=z_{1}-1
. It follows that
f
is regular with respect to
bG
.

In the following, given
f\in\mathcal{O}(G)\cap\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\overline{G})
, we denote by
\{f=0\}
the zero set of
f
that is
\{f=0\}:=\{p\in\overline{G}\mid f(p)=0\}
.

Lemma 4.4
.

With the notation above let
f\in\mathcal{O}(G)\cap\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\overline{G})
be a holomorphic function on
G
which is smooth up to the boundary and
p\in bG
a point with
f(p)=0
such that
f
is regular at
p
with respect to
bG
. There exists an open neighborhood
U\subset Y
around
p
such that
U\cap\{f=0\}
has measure zero in
G
and
U\cap\{f=0\}\cap bG
has measure zero in
bG
and

(i)

\frac{1}{f}\partial f\wedge\psi
is integrable on
U\cap\overline{G}
for all
\psi\in\Omega_{c}^{n,n+1}(\overline{G}\cap U)
.

(ii)

\log(|f|)\psi
is integrable on
U\cap\overline{G}
for all
\psi\in\Omega_{c}^{n+1,n+1}(\overline{G}\cap U)
.

(iii)

\iota^{*}(\frac{1}{f}\partial f\wedge\psi)
is integrable on
U\cap bG
for all
\psi\in\Omega_{c}^{2n}(\overline{G}\cap U)
.

(iv)

\iota^{*}(\log(|f|)\psi)
is integrable on
U\cap bG
for all
\psi\in\Omega_{c}^{2n+1}(\overline{G}\cap U)
.

(v)

\int_{G}\frac{1}{2f}\partial f\wedge\overline{\partial}\psi=\int_{bG}\iota^{*}(\log(|f|)\overline{\partial}\psi)-\int_{G}\log(|f|)\partial\overline{\partial}\psi
for all
\psi\in\Omega_{c}^{n,n}(\overline{G}\cap U)
.

Corollary 4.5
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{O}(G)\cap\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\overline{G})
,
f\not\equiv 0
, be regular with respect to
bG
. We have that the zero set of
f
has measure zero in
G
and that the zero set of
f|_{bG}
has measure zero in
bG
. Furthermore, we have that
\log(|f|)
is locally integrable on
\overline{G}
and that
\iota^{*}\frac{1}{f}\partial f
and
\iota^{*}\log(|f|)
are locally integrable on
bG
.

Proof.

We have to show that any point
p\in\overline{G}
has an open neighborhood
U
such that the statement holds on
U\cap\overline{G}
and
U\cap bG
respectively.
It is well known that
\log|f|
is locally integrable on
G
. Let
p\in bG
a point in the boundary such that
f(p)\neq 0
. Then the objects we consider are smooth in an open neighborhood around
p
and hence locally integrable. For
p\in bG
with
f(p)=0
the statement follows from Lemma
4.4
.
∎

Remark 4.6
.

In Corollary
4.5
we mean by locally integrable that all integrals with respect to the pairing with forms in
\Omega_{c}^{\bullet}(\overline{G})
and
\iota^{*}\Omega_{c}^{\bullet}(\overline{G})
respectively exist and are finite.

The proof of Lemma
4.4
requires some preparation which we will do in Lemma
4.7
, Lemma
4.8
and Lemma
4.9
.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
n\geq 2
, be a smooth orientable submanifold of dimension
\dim X=n-1
. We denote by
dV_{X}
the volume form on
X
and by
\iota\colon X\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
the inclusion map. Define
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
,
f(x)=-\log(x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2})
. We have
\iota^{*}f\in L^{1}_{\operatorname{loc}}(X)
.

Proof.

Let
p=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})\in X
be a point.
Given
p_{1}^{2}+p_{2}^{2}>0
we find an open neighborhood
U
in
X
around
p
such that
\iota^{*}f|_{U}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(U)
. Hence
\iota^{*}f
is locally integrable near
p
.
Now assume
p_{1}=p_{2}=0
. Let
\Pi_{j}\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
be the projection obtained by forgetting the
j
-th component. Consider the case that there exists
j\geq 3
such that the restriction of
\Pi_{j}
to an open neighborhood
U
in
X
around
p
is a diffeomorphism on its image. Without loss of generality assume
j=n\geq 3
. By shrinking
U
we can ensure that
\Pi_{j}(U)
is of the form
\Pi_{j}(U)=D_{\varepsilon}\times V
where
D_{\varepsilon}=\{(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\mid x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}<\varepsilon^{2}\}
,
0<\varepsilon<1
, and
V
is an open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n-3}
. By the transformation law we find
\int_{U}\iota^{*}fdV_{X}=\int_{\Pi_{j}(U)}-\log(x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2})\rho(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-1})dV_{R^{n-1}}
for some smooth positive function
\rho
. Since
(x_{1},x_{2})\mapsto-\log(x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2})
is positive and integrable on
D_{\varepsilon}
we conclude by the Fubini–Tonelli theorem that
\iota^{*}f
is integrable near
p
. Now consider the case that there exists
j\leq 2
such that the restriction of
\Pi_{j}
to an open neighborhood
U
in
X
around
p
is a diffeomorphism on its image. Without loss of generality assume
j=1
. By shrinking
U
we can ensure that
\Pi_{j}(U)
is of the form
\Pi_{j}(U)=(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\times V
,
0<\varepsilon<1
and
V
is an open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
. Furthermore, we can assume that for all
x\in U
we have
x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}<1
. By the transformation law we find
\int_{U}\iota^{*}fdV_{X}=\int_{\Pi_{j}(U)}-\log(g(x_{2},\ldots,x_{n})^{2}+x_{2}^{2})\rho(x_{2},\ldots,x_{n})dV_{R^{n-1}}
for some smooth positive function
\rho
and some smooth function
g
. We have
-\log(g(x_{2},\ldots,x_{n})^{2}+x_{2}^{2})\leq-2\log(|x_{2}|)
. Since
x_{2}\mapsto-\log(|x_{2}|)
is integrable on
(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
and
-\log(g(x_{2},\ldots,x_{n})^{2}+x_{2}^{2})
is positive we conclude by the Fubini–Tonelli theorem that
\iota^{*}f
is integrable near
p
.

We have shown that any point
p\in X
has an open neighborhood where
\iota^{*}f
is integrable hence it follows that
\iota^{*}f\in L^{1}_{\text{loc}}(X)
.
∎

Lemma 4.8
.

For any
0\leq c<1
and
-1<y<1
,
y\neq 0
, we have

I(c,y):=\int_{0}^{1}\left(\sqrt{(x^{2}-c)^{2}+y^{2}}\right)^{-1}dx\leq\left(\sqrt{|y|})\right)^{-1}(-\log|y|+4).

Proof.

With
t=(x^{2}-c)/|y|
we find

I(c,y)=\int_{-c/|y|}^{(1-c)/|y|}\frac{dt}{2\sqrt{|y|t+c}\sqrt{t^{2}+1}}.

We have

\int_{0}^{1}\frac{dt}{2\sqrt{|y|t+c}\sqrt{t^{2}+1}}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{|y|}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{dt}{2\sqrt{t}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|y|}}.

Given
c+|y|<1
that is
(1-c)/|y|>1
we obtain

\int_{1}^{(1-c)/|y|}\frac{dt}{2\sqrt{|y|t+c}\sqrt{t^{2}+1}}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{|y|+c}}\int_{1}^{(1-c)/|y|}\frac{dt}{|t|}\leq\frac{1}{2\sqrt{|y|}}\log\left(\frac{1-c}{|y|}\right)\leq-\frac{\log|y|}{2\sqrt{|y|}}.

Given
c\leq 2|y|
we have

\int_{-c/|y|}^{0}\frac{dt}{2\sqrt{|y|t+c}\sqrt{t^{2}+1}}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{|y|}}\int_{-c/|y|}^{0}\frac{dt}{2\sqrt{t+c/|y|}}\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{|y|}}.

Given
c>2|y|
we write
[-c/|y|,0]=[-c/|y|,1-c/|y|]\cup[1-c/|y|,-1]\cup[-1,0]
. We find

\int_{-1}^{0}\frac{dt}{2\sqrt{|y|t+c}\sqrt{t^{2}+1}}\leq\int_{-1}^{0}\frac{dt}{2\sqrt{c-|y|}}\leq\frac{1}{2\sqrt{|y|}}

and

\int_{1-c/|y|}^{-1}\frac{dt}{2\sqrt{|y|t+c}\sqrt{t^{2}+1}}\leq\int_{1-c/|y|}^{-1}\frac{dt}{2\sqrt{|y|}|t|}\leq\frac{1}{2\sqrt{|y|}}\log\left(c/|y|-1\right)\leq-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{|y|}}\log|y|

and

\int_{-c/|y|}^{1-c/|y|}\frac{dt}{2\sqrt{|y|t+c}\sqrt{t^{2}+1}}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{|y|}}\int_{-c/|y|}^{1-c/|y|}\frac{dt}{2\sqrt{t+c/|y|}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|y|}}.

From the calculations above the claim follows.
∎

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
Y\subset\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
be a domain around zero and
\varphi\colon Y\to\mathbb{R}
a smooth function such that
\varphi(0)=0
,
d\varphi_{p}\neq 0
for all
p\in Y
and at least one of the following conditions is satisfied

(i)

\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial z_{j}}(0)\neq 0
for some
j\geq 2
,

(ii)

there exists
v\in\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
with
\partial\varphi_{0}(v)=0
and
(\partial\overline{\partial}\varphi)_{0}(v,\overline{v})\neq 0
.

Consider the domain
G:=\{z\in Y\mid\varphi(z)<0\}
in
Y
with smooth boundary
bG=\{z\in Y\mid\varphi(z)=0\}
. Let
\iota\colon bG\to\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
denote the inclusion map. Define
\alpha,\beta\colon\mathbb{C}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
by
\alpha(z)=-\log|z_{1}|
,
\beta(z)=|z_{1}|^{-1}
for
z_{1}\neq 0
. There exists a neighborhood
U\subset Y
around zero such that
\alpha
and
\beta
are integrable on
U\cap G
and
\iota^{*}\alpha
and
\iota^{*}\beta
are integrable on
U\cap bG
.

Proof.

We have that
\alpha,\beta
are locally integrable on
\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
. Then for
U
small enough we have that
\int_{G\cap U}|\alpha|dV_{\mathbb{C}^{n+1}}
and
\int_{G\cap U}|\beta|dV_{\mathbb{C}^{n+1}}
are finite.

We have that
bG
is a real codimension one submanifold of
\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
. Then by Lemma
4.7
we find that
\iota^{*}\alpha
is integrable on
U\cap bG
for
U
small enough.

We have to show that
\iota^{*}\beta
is integrable in a neighborhood of zero in
bG
. In the following we will use the identification
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
,
(x,y)\mapsto z=x+iy
. First, let us consider the case that
\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial z_{j}}(0)\neq 0
for some
j\geq 2
. Without loss of generality assume
\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x_{n+1}}(0)-i\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial y_{n+1}}(0)=2\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial z_{n+1}}(0)\neq 0
. In particular, assume
\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial y_{n+1}}(0)\neq 0
. Then the restriction of the projection
\mathbb{R}^{2n+2}\ni(x,y)\mapsto(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
to a small neighborhood of zero in
bG
defines a diffeomorphism on its image.
Since
(x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})\mapsto\left(\sqrt{x^{2}_{1}+y^{2}_{1}}\right)^{-1}
is integrable on a neighborhood around zero in
\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
we obtain from the transformation law that
\iota^{*}\beta
is integrable on
bG\cap U
when the open set
U
around zero in
\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
is chosen small enough.

Now consider the case that
\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial z_{j}}(0)=0
for all
j\geq 2
. Then by the assumptions on
\varphi
we have
\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x_{1}}(0)-i\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial y_{1}}(0)=2\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial z_{1}}(0)\neq 0
. Without loss of generality assume
\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x_{1}}(0)\neq 0
. By the implicit function theorem there exists a smooth real valued function
g
defined on an open neighborhood of zero in
\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
with
g(0)=0
such that near the origin
bG
can be written as the graph of
g
that is
x_{1}=g(x^{\prime},y)
with
x^{\prime}=(x_{2},\ldots,x_{n+1})
. Then by the transformation it is enough to show that

(4.1.1)

\displaystyle(x^{\prime},y)\mapsto\left(\sqrt{(g(x^{\prime},y))^{2}+y_{1}^{2}}\right)^{-1}

is integrable near zero in order to prove that
\iota^{*}\beta
is integrable near zero. We will show now that under the assumptions on
\varphi
the integrability of (
4.1.1
) near zero follows from the estimate in Lemma
4.8
. By the Levi form assumptions on
\varphi
we can assume without loss of generality that
\frac{\partial^{2}\varphi}{\partial^{2}x_{2}}(0)-\frac{\partial^{2}\varphi}{\partial^{2}y_{2}}(0)=2\frac{\partial^{2}\varphi}{\partial\overline{z}_{2}\partial z_{2}}(0)\neq 0
. Let us say
\frac{\partial^{2}\varphi}{\partial^{2}x_{2}}(0)\neq 0
. Since
\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x_{2}}(0)=0
we find
\frac{\partial g}{\partial x_{2}}(0)=0
and hence that
\frac{\partial\eta}{\partial x_{2}}(0)\neq 0
where
\eta
is defined by
\eta(x^{\prime},y):=\left(\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x_{2}}\right)(g(x^{\prime},y),x^{\prime},y)
. By the implicit function theorem there exists a smooth real valued function
h
with
h(0)=0
such that near the origin
\eta^{-1}(0)
can be written as the graph of
h
that is
x_{2}=h(x_{3},\ldots,x_{n+1},y)
. For
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n+1})\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
write
x^{\prime\prime}=(x_{3},\ldots,x_{n+1})
.
Using Taylor expansion we find

g(h(x^{\prime\prime},y)+t,x^{\prime\prime},y)=g(h(x^{\prime\prime},y),x^{\prime\prime},y)+\frac{\partial g}{\partial x_{2}}(h(x^{\prime\prime},y),x^{\prime\prime},y)t+\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}g}{\partial^{2}x_{2}}\right)(h(x^{\prime},y),x^{\prime},y)t^{2}+O(t^{3}).

By the construction of
h
we have
\frac{\partial g}{\partial x_{2}}(h(x^{\prime\prime},y),x^{\prime\prime},y)=0
for all
(x^{\prime\prime},y)
near zero. Furthermore,
\frac{\partial^{2}\varphi}{\partial^{2}x_{2}}(h(x^{\prime\prime},y),x^{\prime\prime},y)\neq 0
for all
(x^{\prime\prime},y)
near zero. Hence we find
g(h(x^{\prime\prime},y)+t,x^{\prime\prime},y)=g(h(x^{\prime\prime},y),x^{\prime\prime},y)+(f(t,x^{\prime\prime},y)t)^{2}
where
f
is smooth function defined in a neighborhood of zero in
\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
where
f
is bounded and bounded from below by some positive constant and has bounded derivatives. Now it is enough to show that the function

(4.1.2)

\displaystyle(t,x^{\prime\prime},y)\mapsto\left(\sqrt{(g(h(x^{\prime\prime},y),x^{\prime},y)+t^{2})^{2}+y_{1}^{2}}\right)^{-1}

is integrable in a sufficiently small open neigborhood of zero in
\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
. Because then the integrability of
\iota^{*}\beta
on
bG\cap U
for some open neighbourhood
U\subset\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
around zero follows from the transformation law.

We find

\frac{1}{\sqrt{(g(h(x^{\prime\prime},y),x^{\prime\prime},y)+t^{2})^{2}+y_{1}^{2}}}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{(\min\{g(h(x^{\prime\prime},y),x^{\prime\prime},y),0)\}+t^{2})^{2}+y_{1}^{2}}}

and

\int_{-\varepsilon}^{\varepsilon}\left(\sqrt{(\min\{g(h(x^{\prime\prime},y),x^{\prime\prime},y),0\}+t^{2})^{2}+y_{1}^{2}}\right)^{-1}dt\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{|y_{1}|}}(|\log(|y_{1}|)|+4)

by Lemma
4.8
for sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
and all
(x^{\prime\prime},y)
,
y_{1}\neq 0
, in a neighborhood of zero in
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
. Note that the function
(x^{\prime\prime},y)\mapsto\left(\sqrt{|y_{1}|}\right)^{-1}(|\log(|y_{1}|)|+4)
is integrable in a small neighborhood of
0\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
. Then, since the function in (
4.1.2
) is non-negative, its integrability near zero follows from the Fubini-Tonelli theorem. ∎

From Lemma
4.9
we can deduce Lemma
4.4
.

Proof of Lemma
4.4
.

Since
f
is smooth up to
bG
we can assume that
f
is the restriction of a smooth function denoted by
f_{1}
defined on an open neighborhood of
\overline{G}
. Since
Y
is a complex manifold and
(\partial f)_{p}\neq 0
we find holomorphic functions
f_{2},\ldots,f_{n+1}
,
f_{2}(p)=\ldots=f_{n+1}(p)=0
, defined on an open neighborhood of
p
in
Y
such that the differential
dF_{p}
of
F=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n+1})
in
p
is invertable. Hence
F
is a diffeomorphism on its image in an open neighborhood
U
of
p
in
Y
such that
F(p)=0
,
F|_{U\cap G}
is holomorhic and
F|_{U\cap bG}
is CR. By shrinking
U
we can assume that
F(U\cap\overline{G})
can be written as
\{\varphi<0\}
for some smooth real valued function
\varphi
defined on an open neighborhood around zero in
\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
satisfying the properties in Lemma
4.9
. Furthermore, in the coordinates given by
F
we find that
f=z_{1}
and
\partial f=dz_{1}
on
F(U\cap\overline{G})
. Then the claims (i)-(iv) follow from Lemma
4.9
.

It remains to show that (v) holds. By Stokes formula we find

\int_{G}\frac{\overline{f}}{|f|^{2}+\delta}\partial f\wedge\overline{\partial}\psi=\int_{bG}\log(|f|^{2}+\delta)\wedge\overline{\partial}\psi-\int_{G}\log(|f|^{2}+\delta)\wedge\partial\overline{\partial}\psi

for all
\delta>0
and all
\psi\in\Omega_{c}^{n,n}(\overline{G})
since the forms in the integrals are smooth.
We have
|f|/(|f|^{2}+\delta)\leq 1/|f|
and
-\log(|f|^{2}+\delta)\leq-2\log(|f|)
for all
\delta>0
. Furthermore, we have
\lim_{\delta\to 0}\overline{f}/(|f|^{2}+\delta)=1/f
and
\lim_{\delta\to 0}\log(|f|^{2}+\delta)=2\log(|f|)
pointwise almost everywhere. Now by the already proven statements (i)-(iv) in Lemma
4.4
we can choose
U\subset Y
around
p
small enough such that
\iota^{*}(\frac{1}{f}\partial f\wedge\overline{\partial}\psi)
and
\iota^{*}(\log(|f|)\overline{\partial}\psi
are integrable on
bG
and
\log(|f|)\wedge\partial\overline{\partial}\psi
is integrable on
G
for all
\psi\in\Omega_{c}^{n,n}(\overline{G}\cap U)
. For
\psi\in\Omega_{c}^{n,n}(\overline{G}\cap U)
we then conclude from the dominated convergence theorem that

\displaystyle\int_{G}\frac{1}{f}\partial f\wedge\overline{\partial}\psi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0}\int_{G}\frac{\overline{f}}{|f|^{2}+\delta}\partial f\wedge\overline{\partial}\psi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0}\int_{bG}\log(|f|^{2}+\delta)\wedge\overline{\partial}\psi-\int_{G}\log(|f|^{2}+\delta)\wedge\partial\overline{\partial}\psi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{bG}\log(|f|^{2})\wedge\overline{\partial}\psi-\int_{G}\log(|f|^{2})\wedge\partial\overline{\partial}\psi.

∎

4.2.
Zero Sets of Holomorhic Functions on Domains with Boundary

Now we are ready to give a definition of
\int_{G}\partial\overline{\partial}\log(|f|)\wedge\psi
where
f\in\mathcal{O}\cap\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\overline{G})
is regular with respect to
bG
and
\psi\in\Omega_{c}^{n,n}(\overline{G})
does not need to vanish near the boundary.

Definition 4.10
.

With the notations above let
f\in\mathcal{O}(G)\cap\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\overline{G})
,
f\not\equiv 0
, be regular with respect to
bG
. Given
\psi\in\Omega_{c}^{n,n}(\overline{G})
we define

(4.2.1)

\displaystyle\text{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,}\int_{G}\partial\overline{\partial}\log(|f|)\wedge\psi:=-\int_{bG}\iota^{*}(\frac{1}{2f}\partial f\wedge\psi)-\int_{bG}\iota^{*}(\log(|f|)\wedge\overline{\partial}\psi)+\int_{G}\log(|f|)\wedge\partial\overline{\partial}\psi.

We note that the right-hand side of (
4.2.1
) is well defined by Corollary
4.5
. Furthermore, we see that for
\psi\in\Omega_{c}^{n,n}(G)
in (
4.2.1
) we obtain the usual definition of
\int_{G}\partial\overline{\partial}\log(|f|)\wedge\psi
. Recall that by Stokes formula we find for any
\alpha\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\overline{G})=\Omega^{0,0}(\overline{G})
and
\beta\in\Omega_{c}^{n,n}(\overline{G})
that

(4.2.2)

\displaystyle\int_{G}\partial\overline{\partial}\alpha\wedge\beta=-\int_{bG}\iota^{*}(\partial\alpha\wedge\beta)-\int_{bG}\iota^{*}(\alpha\overline{\partial}\beta)+\int_{G}\alpha\partial\overline{\partial}\beta.

Given
\psi\in\Omega_{c}^{n,n}(\overline{G})
with
\text{supp}(\psi)\cap\{f=0\}=\emptyset
we find that
\log|f|
is smooth in a neighborhood of
\text{supp}(\psi)
. Then from (
4.2.2
) it follows that
\int_{G}\partial\overline{\partial}\log(|f|)\wedge\psi=0
.

Remark 4.11
.

From (v) in Lemma
4.4
it follows that any point
p\in bG
with
f(p)=0
has an open neighborhood
U\subset Y
such that

\int_{G}\partial\overline{\partial}\log(|f|)\wedge\psi=-\int_{bG}\iota^{*}(\frac{1}{2f}\partial f\wedge\psi)-\int_{G}\frac{1}{2f}\partial f\wedge\overline{\partial}\psi

holds for all
\psi\in\Omega_{c}^{n,n}(\overline{G}\cap U)
.

Lemma 4.12
.

With the notations above let
f\in\mathcal{O}(G)\cap\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\overline{G})
,
f\not\equiv 0
, be regular with respect to
bG
. Let
\psi\in\Omega_{c}^{n,n}(\overline{G})
be a form which satisfies
(\psi\wedge\partial f)_{p}=0
or
(\psi\wedge\overline{\partial}f)_{p}=0
at any point
p\in\overline{G}
. Then

\int_{G}\partial\overline{\partial}\log(|f|)\wedge\psi=0.

Proof.

For
\delta>0
we have that
\alpha_{\delta}:=\log(|f|^{2}+\delta)
is smooth on
\overline{G}
. We calculate

\partial\overline{\partial}\alpha_{\delta}=\frac{\delta}{(|f|^{2}+\delta)^{2}}\partial f\wedge\overline{\partial}f.

Then for
\psi\in\Omega_{c}^{n,n}(\overline{G})
with
\psi\wedge\partial f=0
or
\psi\wedge\overline{\partial}f=0
at any point we observe that
\int_{G}\partial\overline{\partial}\alpha_{\delta}\wedge\psi=0
.
From Stokes formula (see (
4.2.2
)) we then find

0=-\int_{bG}\frac{\overline{f}}{|f|^{2}+\delta}\partial f\wedge\psi-\int_{bG}\log(|f|^{2}+\delta)\wedge\overline{\partial}\psi+\int_{G}\log(|f|^{2}+\delta)\partial\overline{\partial}\psi

for all
\delta>0
. We have
|f|/(|f|^{2}+\delta)\leq 1/|f|
and the pullback of
z\mapsto 1/|f|
to
bG
is locally integrable by Corollary
4.5
. Furthermore,
-\log(|f|^{2}+\delta)\leq-2\log(|f|)
and
z\mapsto-\log(|f|)
is locally integrable on
G
and its pullback to
bG
is also locally integrable by Corollary
4.5
. It then follows from the dominated convergence theorem that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0}\left(-\int_{bG}\frac{\overline{f}}{|f|^{2}+\delta}\wedge\psi-\int_{bG}\log(|f|^{2}+\delta)\wedge\overline{\partial}\psi+\int_{G}\log(|f|^{2}+\delta)\partial\overline{\partial}\psi\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{bG}\frac{1}{f}\partial f\wedge\psi-\int_{bG}\log(|f|^{2})\wedge\overline{\partial}\psi+\int_{G}\log(|f|^{2})\partial\overline{\partial}\psi

Hence we obtain

\int_{G}\partial\overline{\partial}\log(|f|)\wedge\psi:=-\int_{bG}\iota^{*}(\frac{1}{2f}\partial f\wedge\psi)-\int_{bG}\iota^{*}(\log(|f|)\wedge\overline{\partial}\psi)+\int_{G}\log(|f|)\wedge\partial\overline{\partial}\psi=0.

∎

Before we can state the main theorem of this section we need the following.

Lemma 4.13
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{O}(G)\cap\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\overline{G})
be a holomorphic function on
G
which is smooth up to the boundary and
p\in bG
a point with
f(p)=0
such that
f
is regular at
p
with respect to
bG
. There exists an open neighborhood
U\subset Y
around
p
and a smooth submanifold
X
of
Y
through
p
with real dimension
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}X=2n
such that

(i)

X
is connected and orientable.

(ii)

U\cap G\cap\{f=0\}
is a complex submanifold of
G
of complex dimension
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}=n
,

(iii)

U\cap G\cap\{f=0\}
is an open relatively compact subset of
X
,

(iv)

U\cap bG\cap\{f=0\}
has measure zero in
X
.

Proof.

We can write
f=F|_{\overline{G}}
where
F
is a smooth function defined on an open neighborhood of
\overline{G}
in
Y
. Since
\partial f_{p}\neq 0
and
f(p)=0
we have
that there exists an open set
V
of
Y
around
p
such that
X:=F^{-1}(0)\cap V
is a real connected orientable submanifold of
Y
with real dimension
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}X=2n
and
p\in X
which proves (i). Since
G
is open in
Y
we find that
G\cap X
is open in
X
. In addition we can choose
V
such that
\partial f
does not vanish on
X\cap G
. Then, since
X\cap G=\{f=0\}\cap V
and
f
is holomorphic on
G
, we have that
X\cap G
is a complex
n
-dimensional submanifold of
G
. Choosing
U
around
p
small enough we have that (ii) and (iii) holds. In order to prove (iv) we choose coordinates around
p
as in the proof of Lemma
4.4
keeping in mind that
f=F|_{\overline{G}}
. Hence we can consider the case that
V
is an open neighborhood of
\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
around zero,
X=\{z\in V\mid z_{1}=0\}
,
G\cap V=\{z\in V\mid\varphi(z)<0\}
,
bG\cap V=\{z\in V\mid\varphi(z)=0\}
for some smooth function
\varphi\colon V\to\mathbb{R}
with
\varphi(0)=0
,
d\varphi_{p}\neq 0
for all
p\in V
and
\varphi
satisfies at least one of the conditions (i) or (ii) in the assumptions of Lemma
4.9
. Given the case that
\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial z_{j}}(0)\neq 0
for some
j\geq 2
we can choose
U
around zero small enough such that
U\cap bG\cap X
is a submanifold of
X
of real dimension
2n-1
. Hence
U\cap bG\cap\{f=0\}
has measure zero in
X
. Now consider the case that
\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial z_{j}}(0)=0
for all
j\geq 2
. By the Levi form assumptions on
\varphi
we can assume without loss of generality that
\frac{\partial^{2}\varphi}{\partial^{2}x_{2}}(0)-\frac{\partial^{2}\varphi}{\partial^{2}y_{2}}(0)=2\frac{\partial^{2}\varphi}{\partial\overline{z}_{2}\partial z_{2}}(0)\neq 0
. Let us say
\frac{\partial^{2}\varphi}{\partial^{2}x_{2}}(0)\neq 0
. Then choosing
U
around zero small enough we can assume
\frac{\partial^{2}\varphi}{\partial^{2}x_{2}}(z)\neq 0
on
U
. Consider the sets
A:=\{z\in U\mid\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x_{2}}(z)\neq 0\}
and
B:=U\setminus A
. Then
A\cap X\cap bG
is a submanifold of
X
of real dimension
2n-1
. Hence
A\cap X\cap bG
has measure zero in
X
. Furthermore,
B\cap X
is real submanifold of
U
of real dimension
2n-1
. It follows that
B\cap X\cap bG
has measure zero in
X
. We conclude
bG\cap U\cap X=(A\cup B)\cap X\cap bG
has measure zero in
X
.
∎

Now we are ready to state the Lelong-Poincaré formula for domains with boundary.

Theorem 4.14
.

Let
Y
be a complex manifold of dimension
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y=n+1
. Let
G\subset Y
be a domain with smooth boundary
bG
. Let
f\in\mathcal{O}(G)\cap\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\overline{G})
,
f\not\equiv 0
, be regular with respect to
bG
. We have

(4.2.3)

\displaystyle\left(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right],\psi\right)=\frac{i}{\pi}\int_{G}\partial\overline{\partial}\log(|f|)\wedge\psi

for all
\psi\in\Omega_{c}^{n,n}(\overline{G})
where
\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right]
denotes the zero divisor of
f
and the right-hand side is given by Definition
4.10
.

Remark 4.15
.

We note that the left-hand side in (
4.2.3
) is well-defined
by Lemma
4.13
. Furthermore, it follows from (iv) in Lemma
4.13
that the value of
\left(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right],\psi\right)
for
\psi\in\Omega_{c}^{n,n}(\overline{G})
stays the same when integration is performed on
G
instead of
\overline{G}
. This issue should be considered with respect to Example
4.2
and Example
4.3
.

Proof of Theorem
4.14
.

It is enough to show that for any point
p\in\overline{G}
there is an open neighborhood
U\subset Y
around
p
such that the statement holds for all
\psi\in\Omega_{c}^{n,n}(U\cap\overline{G})
. For
p\in G
we can take
U=G
and the statement follows from the usual Lelong-Poincaré formula. Given
p\in bG
with
f(p)\neq 0
we can choose
U
around
p
such that
f
does not vanish on
U\cap\overline{G}
. Then on the one hand one has
(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right],\psi)=0
for all
\psi\in\Omega_{c}^{n,n}(U\cap\overline{G})
. On the other hand we have that
\log|f|
is smooth on
U\cap\overline{G}
. Hence
\int_{G}\partial\overline{\partial}\log(|f|)\wedge\psi=0
for all
\psi\in\Omega_{c}^{n,n}(U\cap\overline{G})
by Definition
4.10
and (
4.2.2
) which shows that (
4.2.3
) holds in that case. Now take
p\in bG
with
f(p)=0
. Choosing local coordinates as in the proof of Lemma
4.4
we can consider an open neighborhood of
V\subset\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
around
p=0
,
f(z)=z_{1}
,
G\cap V=\{z\in V\mid\varphi(z)<0\}
,
bG\cap V=\{z\in V\mid\varphi(z)=0\}
for some smooth function
\varphi\colon V\to\mathbb{R}
with
\varphi(0)=0
,
d\varphi_{0}\neq 0
and there exists
v\in\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
with
\partial\varphi_{0}(v)=0
and
(\partial\overline{\partial}\varphi)_{0}(v,\overline{v})\neq 0
. We will first show that we can assume
\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial z_{1}}(z)\neq 0
on
V
. If
\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial z_{1}}(0)\neq 0
this follows immediately by shrinking
V
. If
\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial z_{1}}(0)=0
we have that
\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial z_{j}}(0)\neq 0
for some
j\geq 2
. Then replace the coordinate
z_{j}
by
z_{j}+z_{1}
. In the new coordinates we have
f(z)=z_{1}
,
\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial z_{1}}(0)\neq 0
and the other assumptions on
\varphi
are still valid on some small open neighborhood around zero. Then by shrinking
V
it follows that
\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial z_{1}}(z)\neq 0
on
V
.
Let
B
be the open ball in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
centered at zero of radius
\varepsilon>0
. Let
D
be the open disc in
\mathbb{C}
centered at zero of radius
\varepsilon>0
. Given
\varepsilon>0
small enough we have
U:=D\times B\subset\subset V
is an open neighborhood around zero relatively compact in
V
. For
z=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n+1})
write
z^{\prime}=(z_{2},\ldots,z_{n+1})
and for
z^{\prime}\in B
put
D(z^{\prime})=\{z\in D\mid\varphi(z,z^{\prime})<0\}
. By the assumptions on
\varphi
we have for any
z^{\prime}\in B
that
D(z^{\prime})
is an open set with smooth boundary in
D
(that is the boundary of
D(z^{\prime})
relative to
D
is smooth). We define
A_{1}:=\{z^{\prime}\in B\mid\varphi(0,z^{\prime})<0\}
,
A_{2}:=\{z^{\prime}\in B\mid\varphi(0,z^{\prime})=0\}
and
A_{3}:=\{z^{\prime}\in B\mid\varphi(0,z^{\prime})>0\}
. Then
A_{j}
,
j=1,2,3
, are pairwise disjoint with
B=A_{1}\cup A_{2}\cup A_{3}
. Furthermore,
A_{1}
and
A_{3}
are open and from (iv) in Lemma
4.13
we have that
A_{2}
has measure zero in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
. Now take
\psi\in\Omega_{c}^{n,n}(U\cap\overline{G})
. We can write
\psi=\sum_{1\leq j,k\leq n+1}\psi_{j,k}\widehat{dz_{j}}\wedge\widehat{d\overline{z}_{k}}
for some
\psi_{j,k}\in\mathscr{C}_{c}^{\infty}(U\cap\overline{G})
where
\widehat{dz_{j}}=dz_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dz_{j-1}\wedge dz_{j+1}\wedge\ldots\wedge dz_{n+1}
and
\widehat{d\overline{z}_{k}}=\overline{\widehat{dz_{k}}}
. Since
\{f=0\}\cap U\cap G=\{0\}\times A_{1}
it follows that

\left(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right],\psi\right)=\int_{A_{1}}\psi_{1,1}\widehat{dz_{1}}\wedge\widehat{d\overline{z}_{1}}.

Put
\psi^{\prime}=\psi-\psi_{1,1}\widehat{dz_{1}}\wedge\widehat{d\overline{z}_{1}}
.
Since
\partial f=dz_{1}
we obtain from Lemma
4.12
that
\int_{G}\partial\overline{\partial}\log(|f|)\wedge\psi^{\prime}=0
.
It remains to show that
\int_{G}\partial\overline{\partial}\log(|f|)\wedge\psi_{1,1}\widehat{dz_{1}}\wedge\widehat{d\overline{z}_{1}}=-i\pi\int_{A_{1}}\psi_{1,1}\widehat{dz_{1}}\wedge\widehat{d\overline{z}_{1}}
. Choosing
\varepsilon>0
in the definition of
U
small enough we find by Remark
4.11
that

\int_{G}\partial\overline{\partial}\log(|f|)\wedge\psi_{1,1}\widehat{dz_{1}}\wedge\widehat{d\overline{z}_{1}}=-\int_{bG}\iota^{*}(\frac{1}{2z_{1}}\psi_{1,1}dz_{1}\wedge\widehat{dz_{1}}\wedge\widehat{d\overline{z}_{1}})-\int_{G}\frac{1}{2z_{1}}\frac{\partial\psi_{1,1}}{\partial\overline{z}_{1}}dz_{1}\wedge d\overline{z}_{1}\wedge\widehat{dz_{1}}\wedge\widehat{d\overline{z}_{1}}.

Given
z^{\prime}\in A_{3}
we have that
0\notin D(z^{\prime})
and hence
z_{1}\mapsto 1/z_{1}
is smooth on
D(z^{\prime})
. We obtain from Stokes formula in
\mathbb{C}
that

(4.2.4)

\displaystyle\int_{bD(z^{\prime})}\frac{\psi_{1,1}}{z_{1}}dz_{1}=-\int_{D(z^{\prime})}\frac{1}{z_{1}}\frac{\partial\psi_{1,1}}{\partial\overline{z}_{1}}dz_{1}\wedge d\overline{z}_{1}.

Given
z^{\prime}\in A_{1}
we have that
0\in D(z^{\prime})
. Then it follows from the Cauchy-Pompeiu formula (see Remark
4.16
below) that

(4.2.5)

\displaystyle 2\pi i\psi_{1,1}(0,z^{\prime})=\int_{bD(z^{\prime})}\frac{\psi_{1,1}}{z_{1}}dz_{1}+\int_{D(z^{\prime})}\frac{1}{z_{1}}\frac{\partial\psi_{1,1}}{\partial\overline{z}_{1}}dz_{1}\wedge d\overline{z}_{1}.

Since
B
is the union of the pairwise disjoint sets
A_{j}
,
j=1,2,3
, and
A_{2}
has measure zero we find by the Fubini theorem that

\int_{bG}\iota^{*}(\frac{1}{z_{1}}\psi_{1,1}dz_{1}\wedge\widehat{dz_{1}}\wedge\widehat{d\overline{z}_{1}})=\int_{A_{1}\cup A_{3}}\left(\int_{bD(z^{\prime})}\frac{1}{z_{1}}\psi_{1,1}dz_{1}\right)\widehat{dz_{1}}\wedge\widehat{d\overline{z}_{1}}

and

\int_{G}\frac{1}{z_{1}}\frac{\partial\psi_{1,1}}{\partial\overline{z}_{1}}dz_{1}\wedge d\overline{z}_{1}\wedge\widehat{dz_{1}}\wedge\widehat{d\overline{z}_{1}}=\int_{A_{1}\cup A_{3}}\left(\int_{D(z^{\prime})}\frac{1}{z_{1}}\frac{\partial\psi_{1,1}}{\partial\overline{z}_{1}}dz_{1}\wedge d\overline{z}_{1}\right)\widehat{dz_{1}}\wedge\widehat{d\overline{z}_{1}}.

From (
4.2.4
) and (
4.2.5
) it follows that

\int_{A_{3}}\left(-\int_{bD(z^{\prime})}\frac{\psi_{1,1}}{z_{1}}dz_{1}-\int_{D(z^{\prime})}\frac{1}{z_{1}}\frac{\partial\psi_{1,1}}{\partial\overline{z}_{1}}dz_{1}\wedge d\overline{z}_{1}.\right)\widehat{dz_{1}}\wedge\widehat{d\overline{z}_{1}}=0.

and

\int_{A_{1}}\left(-\int_{bD(z^{\prime})}\frac{\psi_{1,1}}{z_{1}}dz_{1}-\int_{D(z^{\prime})}\frac{1}{z_{1}}\frac{\partial\psi_{1,1}}{\partial\overline{z}_{1}}dz_{1}\wedge d\overline{z}_{1}.\right)\widehat{dz_{1}}\wedge\widehat{d\overline{z}_{1}}=-2\pi i\int_{A_{1}}\psi_{1,1}\widehat{dz_{1}}\wedge\widehat{d\overline{z}_{1}}.

Hence we can conclude

\frac{i}{\pi}\int_{G}\partial\overline{\partial}\log(|f|)\wedge\psi=\int_{A_{1}}\psi_{1,1}\widehat{dz_{1}}\wedge\widehat{d\overline{z}_{1}}=\left(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right],\psi\right)

and the claim follows.
∎

Remark 4.16
.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{C}
be a domain. Let
D
be an open subset of
U
such that its boundary relative to
U
denoted by
b^{U}D
is smooth. Let
\overline{D}^{U}
be the closure of
D
relative to
U
. For any
\psi\in\mathscr{C}_{c}^{\infty}(\overline{D}^{U})
and any
w\in D
one has

\psi(w)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\left(\int_{b^{U}D}\psi\frac{dz}{z-w}+\int_{D}\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial\overline{z}}\frac{dz\wedge d\overline{z}}{z-w}\right).

This formula is a version of the Cauchy-Pompeiu formula for non bounded domains in
\mathbb{C}
. For the proof take a small disc
B
around
w
relatively compact in
D
. The statement then follows from Stokes theorem applied with respect to the domain
D\setminus\overline{B}
and the Cauchy-Pompeiu formula for the disc
B
.

4.3.
Zero Sets of CR Functions

We are now going to prove two results (see Theorem
4.19
and Theorem
4.21
) concerning the zero sets of CR functions on strictly pseudoconvex CR manifolds. For the proofs we need the following.

Proposition 4.17
.

Let
(X,T^{1,0}X)
be as in Theorem
1.1
and let
p\in X
be a point. There exists an open neighborhood
V
around
p
, a domain
Y\subset\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
around zero, a smooth function
\varphi\colon Y\to\mathbb{R}
with non-vanishing differential
d\varphi
and a CR embedding
F\colon V\to Y
with the following properties:

(i)

F(V)=\{\varphi=0\}
,
F(p)=0
and
\{\varphi=0\}
is connected,

(ii)

G:=\{z\in Y\mid\varphi(z)<0\}
is a domain in
Y
with smooth boundary (relative to
Y
),

(iii)

\sum_{0\leq j,k\leq n}\frac{\partial^{2}\varphi}{\partial z_{j}\partial\overline{z}_{k}}(z)w_{j}\overline{w}_{k}>0
for all
z\in\{\varphi=0\}
and all
w=(w_{0},\ldots,w_{n})
with
\sum_{j=0}^{n}w_{j}\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial z_{j}}(z)=0
.

Proof.

By the assumptions on
X
we have that
X
is CR embeddable into the complex euclidean space. Hence we can find an open neighborhood
V\subset X
around
p
, an open set
Y\subset\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
around zero, a smooth function
\varphi\colon Y\to\mathbb{R}
with non-vanishing differential
d\varphi
and a CR embedding
F\colon V\to Y
with
F(V)=\{\varphi=0\}
and
F(p)=0
. Shrinking
V
and
Y
we can assume that
\{\varphi=0\}
is connected. Since
X
is strictly pseudoconvex, by possibly changing the sign of
\varphi
we can assume that (iii) is satisfied. Shrinking
Y
again we obtain that
G
is a domain in
Y
.
∎

Remark 4.18
.

In the following when we apply Proposition
4.17
we consider the closure and boundary of
G
always relative to
Y
, that is,
\overline{G}:=\overline{G}^{Y}
and
bG:=b^{Y}G
.

Theorem 4.19
.

Let
(X,T^{1,0}X)
be as in Theorem
1.1
and let
f\in H^{0}_{b}(X)\cap\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(X)
be a smooth CR function such that
df_{p}\neq 0
for any
p\in X
with
f(p)=0
. Then
1/f
is integrable on
X
.

Remark 4.20
.

We note that the assumptions on
f
do not imply in general that
0
is a regular value of the map
f\colon X\to\mathbb{C}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{2}
. (See Example
4.3
).

Proof of Theorem
4.19
.

We show that any point
p\in X
has an open neighborhood such that
1/f
is integrable there. Let
p\in X
be a point. If
f(p)\neq 0
there is nothing to show. So we assume
f(p)=0
. By Proposition
4.17
we find an open neighborhood around
p
which can be CR isomorphically identified with a set
bG=\{\varphi=0\}\subset Y\subset\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
where
Y,G,\varphi
satisfy the conditions in Proposition
4.17
. By
[
29
, Theorem 2.6.13]
after shrinking
Y
we find a function
F\in\mathcal{O}(G)\cap\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\overline{G})
with
F|_{bG}=f
. In addition, since
df_{p}\neq 0
, we can achieve by shrinking
Y
again that
df
does not vanish on
bG
. Since
bG
is strictly pseudoconvex we have that
F
is regular with respect to
bG
. Then it follows from Lemma
4.4
that
1/f
is integrable in a small neighborhood around
p
.
∎

In the situation of Theorem
4.19
assuming that
0
is a regular value of the map
f\colon X\to\mathbb{C}
we have that
\{f=0\}:=f^{-1}(0)
is a smooth compact oriented submanifold in
X
of real codimension two. Given
\psi\in\Omega^{2n-1}(X)
we then define

(4.3.1)

\displaystyle\left(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right],\psi\right):=\int_{\{f=0\}}\ell^{*}\psi

where
\ell\colon\{f=0\}\to X
denotes the inclusion map. We have the following.

Theorem 4.21
.

Let
(X,T^{1,0}X)
be as in Theorem
1.1
and let
f\in H^{0}_{b}(X)\cap\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(X)
be a smooth CR function such that
0
is a regular value of
f\colon X\to\mathbb{C}
. Then for any
\psi\in\Omega^{2n-1}(X)
we have

\left(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right],\psi\right)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{X}\frac{df}{f}\wedge d\psi.

Proof.

The theorem follows immediately from Theorem
4.23
below.
∎

It turns out that the result in Theorem
4.21
holds for any oriented smooth real manifold and any smooth complex valued function which has zero as a regular value what we are going to explain now.

Let
M
be an oriented smooth real manifold of dimension
n\geq 2
. Given a smooth function
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(M)
such that zero is a regular value of the map
f\colon M\to\mathbb{C}
we have that
\{f=0\}:=\{x\in M\mid f(x)=0\}
is an oriented smooth submanifold of dimension
n-2
. We then define

(4.3.2)

\displaystyle\left(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right],\psi\right)=\int_{\{f=0\}}\iota^{*}\psi

for
\psi\in\Omega^{n-2}_{c}(M)
where
\iota\colon\{f=0\}\to M
denotes the inclusion map. We would like to show that
\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right]
is given by
(2\pi i)^{-1}d(df/f)
in the sense of distributions (see Theorem
4.23
). Therefore we need the following.

Lemma 4.22
.

Let
M
be an oriented smooth real manifold of dimension
n\geq 2
. Given a function
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(M)
such that zero is a regular value of the map
f\colon M\to\mathbb{C}
we have that
1/f
is locally integrable.

Proof.

We need to show that any point
p\in M
has an open neighborhood where
1/f
is integrable. Given
f(p)\neq 0
there is nothing to show. So let us assume
f(p)=0
. By the assumptions on
f
we can choose local coordinates
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
in an open neighborhood
U
around
p
such that
x_{1}=\Re f
,
x_{2}=\Im f
. In this coordinates we find
1/f=\frac{1}{x_{1}+ix_{2}}
. We note that
(x_{1},x_{2})\mapsto|x_{1}+ix_{2}|^{-1}=|(x_{1},x_{2})|^{-1}

is locally integrable on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Then the claim follows from the Fubini-Tonelli theorem.
∎

Theorem 4.23
.

Let
M
be an oriented smooth real manifold of dimension
n\geq 2
. For any function
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(M)
such that zero is a regular value of the map
f\colon M\to\mathbb{C}
we have
\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right]=(2\pi i)^{-1}d(df/f)
in the sense of distributions, that is,

(4.3.3)

\displaystyle\left(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right],\psi\right)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{M}\frac{df}{f}\wedge d\psi

for all
\psi\in\Omega^{n-2}_{c}(M)
.

We note that the right-hand side of (
4.3.3
) is well-defined by Lemma
4.22
.

Remark 4.24
.

Theorem
4.23
can be obtained from the results due to Harvey–Semmes
[
24
]
under much more general assumptions on the function
f
. Here, we will give an elementary proof for the simple case that zero is a regular value of the map
f\colon X\to\mathbb{C}
.

Proof of Theorem
4.23
.

It is enough to show that any point
p\in M
has an open neighborhood where the statement holds. Let
p\in M
be a point with
f(p)\neq 0
. Consider the open neighborhood
U:=M\setminus\{f=0\}
around
p
. We have that
df/f
is smooth on
U
with
d(df/f)=0
. Given
\psi\in\Omega^{n-2}_{c}(U)
we find by Stokes theorem that

\int_{M}\frac{df}{f}\wedge d\psi=\int_{U}\frac{df}{f}\wedge d\psi=\int_{U}d\left(\frac{df}{f}\right)\wedge\psi=0.

On the other hand we have
\left(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right],\psi\right)=0
. Hence (
4.3.3
) holds on
U
.

Now let
p\in M
be a point with
f(p)=0
. By the assumptions on
f
we find local coordinates
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}
on an open neighborhood
U\subset M
around
p
such that
\Re f=x_{1}
,
\Im f=x_{2}
and so that
p
is mapped to the origin in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. In these coordinates, after shrinking
U
, we can identify
U
with an open neighborhood in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
of the form
U=D\times B
, where
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
are balls centered in zero, such that
p=0\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\{f=0\}\cap U=\{x\in U\mid x_{1}=x_{2}=0\}=\{0\}\times B
. We use the notation
x=(x_{1},x_{2},x^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
with
x^{\prime}=(x_{3},\ldots,x_{n})
. Furthermore, put
z:=x_{1}+ix_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
with

dz=dx_{1}+idx_{2},\,\,d\overline{z}=dx_{1}-idx_{2},\,\,\frac{\partial}{\partial z}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}}-i\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{2}}\right),\,\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{z}}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}}+i\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{2}}\right).

Using this notation we find
df/f=dz/z
and

d\nu=\partial\nu+\overline{\partial}\nu+d^{\prime}\nu

for all
\nu\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(U)
where
\partial\nu=\frac{\partial\nu}{\partial z}dz
,
\overline{\partial}\nu=\frac{\partial\nu}{\partial\overline{z}}d\overline{z}
,
d^{\prime}\nu=\sum_{j=3}^{n}\frac{\partial\nu}{\partial x_{j}}dx_{j}
. Note that we can declare
\partial,\overline{\partial},d^{\prime}
to act on forms in
\Omega^{\bullet}(U)
in the standard way.

Let
\psi\in\Omega^{n-2}_{c}(U)
be arbitrary. Since
2dx_{1}=dz+d\overline{z}
,
2idx_{2}=dz-d\overline{z}
we can write
\psi=\alpha_{0}+\psi^{\prime}
with

\psi^{\prime}=dz\wedge\alpha_{1}+d\overline{z}\wedge\alpha_{2}+dz\wedge d\overline{z}\wedge\alpha_{3}

such that
\alpha_{0}\in\Omega^{n-2}_{c}(U)
,
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\Omega^{n-3}_{c}(U)
,
\alpha_{3}\in\Omega^{n-4}_{c}(U)
and
\iota_{\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}}\alpha_{k}=0
for
j=1,2
and
k=0,\ldots,3
. It follows that

\alpha_{0}=\nu dx_{3}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx_{n}

for some smooth function
\nu\in C^{\infty}_{c}(U)
and hence we obtain
\iota^{*}\psi=\nu\circ\iota dx_{3}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx_{n}
.
We conclude that

(4.3.4)

\displaystyle\left(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right],\psi\right)=\int_{\{f=0\}\cap U}\iota^{*}\psi=\int_{\{0\}\times B}\iota^{*}\psi=\int_{B}\nu(0,x^{\prime})dx_{3}\ldots dx_{n}.

We will now show that (
4.3.3
) holds on
U
in two steps: First we prove
\int_{U}\frac{df}{f}\wedge d\psi^{\prime}=0
. Second we calculate
\int_{U}\frac{df}{f}\wedge d\alpha_{0}
.

We have

dz\wedge d\psi^{\prime}=dz\wedge d\overline{z}\wedge d^{\prime}\alpha_{2}
.
For
z\neq 0
fixed put
U_{z}=\{z\}\times B
and denote by
\ell\colon U_{z}\to U
the inclusion map. We obtain

\displaystyle\int_{U_{z}}\ell^{*}(d^{\prime}\alpha_{2})=\int_{U_{z}}\ell^{*}(d\alpha_{2})=\int_{U_{z}}d\ell^{*}\alpha_{2}=0.

since
\ell^{*}\alpha_{2}
has compact support in
U_{z}
. Since
z\mapsto\frac{1}{z}
is locally integrable on
\mathbb{C}

we conclude from the Fubini-Tonelli theorem that

(4.3.5)

\displaystyle\int_{U}\frac{df}{f}\wedge d\psi^{\prime}=\int_{U}\frac{dz}{z}\wedge d\overline{z}\wedge d^{\prime}\alpha_{2}=\int_{D}\left(\int_{U_{z}}\ell^{*}(d^{\prime}\alpha_{2})\right)\frac{dz\wedge d\overline{z}}{z}=0.

We have
dz\wedge d\alpha_{0}=\frac{\partial\nu}{\partial\overline{z}}dz\wedge d\overline{z}\wedge dx_{3}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx_{n}
. Again by the Fubini-Tonelli theorem we find

\int_{U}\frac{df}{f}\wedge d\alpha_{0}=\int_{U}\frac{\partial\nu}{\partial\overline{z}}\frac{dz}{z}\wedge d\overline{z}\wedge dx_{3}\wedge\ldots\wedge dx_{n}=\int_{B}\left(\int_{D}\frac{\partial\nu}{\partial\overline{z}}\frac{dz\wedge d\overline{z}}{z}\right)dx_{3}\ldots dx_{n}.

Note that for any
x^{\prime}\in B
we have that
z\mapsto\nu(z,x^{\prime})
has compact support in
D
. Then, using the Cauchy-Pompeiu formula, we obtain

(4.3.6)

\displaystyle\int_{U}\frac{df}{f}\wedge d\alpha_{0}=2\pi i\int_{B}\nu(0,x^{\prime})dx_{3}\ldots dx_{n}.

Since
\psi=\alpha_{0}+\psi^{\prime}
we conclude from (
4.3.4
), (
4.3.5
) and (
4.3.6
) that

\left(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right],\psi\right)=\int_{B}\nu(0,x^{\prime})dx_{3}\ldots dx_{n}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{U}\frac{df}{f}\wedge d\psi.

∎

5.
Equidistribution on CR manifolds

In this section we are going to prove Theorem
1.6
and Theorem
1.7
stated in the introduction. We will first understand
\mathcal{C}_{f}
as a distribution valued random variable defined on a sequence of probability spaces
(A_{k})_{k>0}
contained in the space of smooth CR functions (see Definition
5.1
and Lemma
5.2
). A direct calculation leads to the proof of Theorem
1.6
(see Theorem
5.5
and Corollary
5.6
). In order to prove Theorem
1.7
(see Corollary
5.15
) we need variance estimates for
\mathcal{C}_{f}
(see Theorem
5.13
). It turns out that those estimates can be obtained from the understanding of a sequence of bilinear forms
(\theta_{k})_{k>0}
(see Definition
5.10
) when
k
becomes large (see Lemma
5.11
and Lemma
5.12
).

Let
(X,T^{1,0}X)
be a compact orientable strictly pseudoconvex CR manifold such that the Kohn-Laplacian has closed range. Denote by
\xi
a contact form on
X
such that the respective Levi form is positive definite and let
\mathcal{T}
be the respective Reeb vector field uniquely determined by
\iota_{\mathcal{T}}\xi\equiv 1
and
\iota_{\mathcal{T}}d\xi\equiv 0
. Let
P\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{cl}}(X)
be a first order formally self-adjoint classical pseudodifferential operator with
\sigma_{P}(\xi)>0
where
\sigma_{P}
denotes the principal symbol of
P
. Denote by
T_{P}=\Pi P\Pi
the corresponding Toeplitz operator. Let
0<\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}\leq\ldots
be the positive eigenvalues of
T_{P}
counting multiplicity and let
f_{1},f_{2}\ldots\in H_{b}^{0}(X)\cap\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(X)
be a respective orthonormal system of eigenvectors. In particular, we have
T_{P}f_{j}=\lambda_{j}f_{j}
,
(f_{j},f_{j})=1
and
(f_{j},f_{\ell})=0
for all
j,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,
j\neq\ell
. Let
\chi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}((\delta_{1},\delta_{2}))
,
\chi\not\equiv 0
, be a cut-off function for some
0<\delta_{1}<\delta_{2}<1
and put
\eta(t):=|\chi(t)|^{2}
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
, with
\tau_{j}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}}t^{n+j}\eta(t)dt
for
j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Put
N_{k}=|\{j\in\mathbb{N}\mid\lambda_{j}\leq k\delta_{2}\}|
. Let
\kappa\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C}
be a function such that
|\kappa(k)|^{2}/(1+|k|^{n})
is bounded in
k
. For
k>0
put
A_{k}=\text{span}\left(\{1\}\cup\{f_{j}\mid\lambda_{j}\leq\delta_{2}k\}\right)
. On
A_{k}
we consider the probability measure
\mu_{k}
induced by the standard complex Gaussian measure
\mu^{G}
on
\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}
and the map

\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}\ni a\mapsto a_{0}\kappa(k)+\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}a_{j}\chi_{k}(\lambda_{j})f_{j}\in A_{k}

where
\chi_{k}(t)=\chi(k^{-1}t)
.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
f\in H^{0}_{b}(X)\cap\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(X)
such that
df_{p}\neq 0
for all
p\in X
with
f(p)=0
. We define

\mathcal{C}_{f}\colon\Omega^{2n}(X)\to\mathbb{C},\,\,\,\,\mathcal{C}_{f}(\psi)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{X}\frac{df}{f}\wedge\psi.

We note that under the assumptions on
f
in Definition
5.1
we have that
\mathcal{C}_{f}
is well defined by Theorem
4.19
. Furthermore, given
f\in H^{0}_{b}(X)\cap\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(X)
such that zero is a regular value of the map
f\colon X\to\mathbb{C}
it follows that
df_{p}\neq 0
for all
p\in X
with
f(p)=0
(cf. Remark
4.20
).

Since we wish to understand
\mathcal{C}_{f}
as a random variable on
A_{k}
, we have to make sure that it is defined on a set
\tilde{A}_{k}\subset A_{k}
with
\mu_{k}(\tilde{A_{k}})=1
.
We have the following.

Lemma 5.2
.

Put
\tilde{A}_{k}:=\{f\in A_{k}\mid\text{0 is a regular value of }f\colon X\to\mathbb{C}\}
. For any
k>0
with

|\kappa(k)|^{2}+\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}|\chi_{k}(\lambda_{j})|^{2}|f_{j}(x)|^{2}>0,\,\,\,\forall x\in X

we have that
\tilde{A}_{k}
is
\mu_{k}
-measurable with
\mu_{k}(A_{k}\setminus\tilde{A}_{k})=0
.

Lemma
5.2
is a direct consequence of the following.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
M
be a compact manifold and let
g_{0},g_{1},\ldots,g_{N}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(M)
such that
|g_{0}(x)|^{2}+\ldots+|g_{N}(x)|^{2}>0
for all
x\in M
. Define
\tau:\mathbb{C}^{N}\to\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(M)
,
\tau(a)=\sum_{j=0}^{N}a_{j}g_{j}
and put

Q:=\{a\in\mathbb{C}^{N+1}\mid\text{0 is a regular vlaue of the map }\tau(a)\colon X\to\mathbb{C}\}.

We have that
Q
is open such that
\mathbb{C}^{N+1}\setminus Q
has Lebesgue measure zero.

Proof.

Given an open set
U\subset M
we define
Q(U)\subset\mathbb{C}^{N+1}
by saying
a\in Q(U)
if and only if there exists an open neighborhood
V
around
\overline{U}
such that 0 is a regular value of the map
\tau(a)|_{V}\colon V\to\mathbb{C}
.
The compactness of
M
implies that
Q(U)
is open for any open set
U\subset M
. Since
Q=Q(M)
we conclude that
Q
is open. Let
p\in M
be a point. Since
|g_{0}(x)|^{2}+\ldots+|g_{N}(x)|^{2}>0
there exist an open neighborhood
U\subset M
around
p
,
j\leq j\leq N
and a constant
c>0
such that
|g_{j}(x)|\geq c
for all
x\in U
. Without loss of generality assume
j=0
. We define
\tau^{\prime}:\mathbb{C}^{N}\to\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(M)
,
\tau^{\prime}(a)=\sum_{j=1}^{N}a_{j}g_{j}
. Given
a^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}^{N}
we define
W(a^{\prime},U)\subset\mathbb{C}
by saying
\lambda\in W(a^{\prime},U)
if and only if there exists an open neighborhood
V
around
\overline{U}
such that
-\lambda
is a regular value of the map
\tau^{\prime}(a)/g_{0}|_{V}\colon V\to\mathbb{C}
. Let
x\in M
be a point with
g_{0}(x)\neq 0
and
a=(a_{0},a^{\prime})\in\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}^{N}
with
\tau(a)(x)=0
. It follows that
\tau^{\prime}(a^{\prime})(x)=-a_{0}g_{0}(x)
and we find

\left(d\frac{\tau^{\prime}(a^{\prime})}{g_{0}}\right)_{x}=\frac{(d\tau^{\prime}(a^{\prime}))_{x}}{g_{0}(x)}-\frac{\tau^{\prime}(a^{\prime})}{g_{0}(x)}\frac{(dg_{0})_{x}}{g_{0}(x)}=\frac{(d\tau(a))_{x}}{g_{0}(x)}.

From this observation it follows that for any
a=(a_{0},a^{\prime})\in\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}^{N}
we have
a\in Q(U)
if and only if
a_{0}\in W(a^{\prime},U)
. By the Theorem of Sard we have that
\mathbb{C}\setminus W(a^{\prime},U)
has Lebesgue measure zero for any
a^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}^{N}
. Furthermore, since
Q(U)
is open we have that
\mathbb{C}^{N+1}\setminus Q(U)
is Lebesgue measurable. Since
