\mathbb{C}^{N+1}\setminus Q(U)=\{(a_{0},a^{\prime})\in\mathbb{C}^{N+1}\mid a_{0}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus W(a^{\prime},U)\}
it follows from the Fubini theorem that
\mathbb{C}^{N+1}\setminus Q(U)
has Lebesgue measure zero.

Using the construction above, by the compactness of
M
we can find finitely many open sets
U_{1},\ldots,U_{m}\subset M
with
\bigcup_{j=1}^{m}U_{j}=M
such that
\mathbb{C}^{N+1}\setminus Q(U_{j})
has Lebesgue measure zero for all
1\leq j\leq m
. Since
\mathbb{C}^{N+1}\setminus Q=\bigcup_{j=1}^{m}\mathbb{C}^{N+1}\setminus Q(U_{j})
we conclude that
\mathbb{C}^{N+1}\setminus Q
has Lebesgue measure zero.
∎

5.1.
Proof of Theorem
1.6

We are now going to prove Theorem
1.6
. We need to introduce some useful notation before. For
d\in\mathbb{N}
we denote by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
the standard inner product on
\mathbb{C}^{d+1}
that is

\langle a,b\rangle=\sum_{j=0}^{d}a_{j}\overline{b_{j}}

for
a=(a_{0},\ldots,a_{d}),b=(b_{0},\ldots,b_{d})\in\mathbb{C}^{d+1}
. Furthermore, given
a\in\mathbb{C}^{d+1}
we put
|a|=\sqrt{\langle a,a\rangle}
. For
k>0
consider the map

(5.1.1)

\displaystyle F_{k}\colon X\to\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1},\,\,\,\,F_{k}=(\kappa(k),\chi_{k}(\lambda_{1})f_{1},\ldots,\chi_{k}(\lambda_{N_{k}})f_{N_{k}}).

Recall that
\eta_{k}=|\chi_{k}|^{2}
. We put
S_{k}(x,y):=\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,y)=\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}\eta_{k}(\lambda_{j})f_{j}(x)\overline{f_{j}(y)}
and
B_{k}(x):=S_{k}(x,x)
for
x,y\in X
and
k>0
. It turns out that
|F_{k}|^{2}=|\kappa(k)|^{2}+B_{k}
and
\langle F_{k}(x),F_{k}(y)\rangle=|\kappa(k)|^{2}+S_{k}(x,y)
for all
x,y\in X
and
k>0
. We further consider
dF_{k}
component wise, that is,

dF_{k}=(0,\chi_{k}(\lambda_{1})df_{1},\ldots,\chi_{k}(\lambda_{N_{k}})df_{N_{k}}).

Then
\langle a,\overline{dF_{k}}\rangle
is a one form on
X
for any
a\in\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}
. We have for example
\langle F_{k}(x),(dF_{k})_{y}\rangle=d_{y}S_{k}(x,y)
and for
f=\langle a,\overline{F_{k}}\rangle
we obtain
df=\langle a,\overline{dF_{k}}\rangle
.

Lemma 5.4
.

There exists
k_{0}>0
such that for any
k\geq k_{0}
we find a constant
C_{k}>0
with

(5.1.2)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}}\frac{|\langle a,\overline{dF_{k}}\rangle(V)|}{|\langle a,\overline{F_{k}(x)}\rangle|}d\mu^{G}(a)\leq C_{k}|V|.

for all
x\in X
and
V\in T_{x}X
.

Proof.

By Theorem
2.10
we can choose
k_{0}>0
such that there exists a constant
C>0
with
|F_{k}|^{2}\geq Ck^{n+1}
for all
k\geq k_{0}
. Furthermore, we have that
\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}}|a_{0}|^{-1}\|a\|d\mu^{G}(a)<\infty
. Since
F_{k}(x)/\|F_{k}(x)\|
,
x\in X
, has unit length we obtain from the unitary invariance of
d\mu^{G}
that

\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}}\frac{|a|}{|a_{0}|}d\mu^{G}(a)=\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}}\frac{|a|}{|\langle a,\overline{F_{k}(x)}/|F_{k}(x)|\rangle|}d\mu^{G}(a).

Given
k\geq k_{0}
,
x\in X
and
V\in T_{x}X
we find

(5.1.3)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\langle a,\overline{dF_{k}}\rangle(V)}{\langle a,\overline{F_{k}(x)}\rangle}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{|a||VF_{k}/|F_{k}(x)||}{|\langle a,\overline{F_{k}(x)}/|F_{k}(x)|\rangle|}

It follows that

\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}}\left|\frac{\langle a,\overline{dF_{k}}\rangle(V)}{\langle a,\overline{F_{k}(x)}\rangle}\right|d\mu^{G}(a)\leq\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}}\frac{|a|}{|a_{0}|}d\mu^{G}(a)\frac{|VF_{k}|}{|F_{k}(z)|}<\infty.

Since
|VF_{k}|^{2}=\left((d_{x}\otimes d_{y}S_{k}(z,w))\right)|_{x=y}(V\otimes V)
we conclude from Lemma
2.11
that there is a constant
\tilde{C}_{k}>0
with
|VF_{k}|\leq\tilde{C}_{k}|V|
for all
x\in X
and
V\in T_{x}X
. Hence we can choose a constant
C_{k}>0
independent of
x
and
V
such that (
5.1.2
) holds.
∎

Theorem
1.6
follows from the next theorem by putting
\kappa(k)=0
and replacing
\psi
by
d\psi
(see Corollary
5.6
).

Theorem 5.5
.

Under the same assumptions as in Theorem
1.1
and with the notations above there exists
k_{0}>0

such that for each
k\geq k_{0}
we have that
\mathcal{C}_{f}
is a well-defined current for all
f
outside a subset in
A_{k}
of
\mu_{k}
-measure zero, and given
\psi\in\Omega^{2n}(X)
the expectation value

\mathbb{E}_{k}(\mathcal{C}_{f})(\psi):=\int_{A_{k}}\mathcal{C}_{f}(\psi)d\mu_{k}(f)

exists
with
\mathbb{E}_{k}(\mathcal{C}_{f})(\psi)=\int_{X}\beta_{k}\wedge\psi
where
\beta_{k}
is the smooth one form given by

(5.1.4)

\displaystyle\beta_{k}(x)=\frac{d_{x}\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,y)|_{x=y}}{2\pi i(|\kappa(k)|^{2}+\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,x))}

for all
x\in X
and
k\geq k_{0}
where
\eta=|\chi|^{2}
.

Proof.

Choose
k_{0}>0
such that
|F_{k}(x)|>0
holds for all
k\geq k_{0}
and
x\in X
. From Lemma
5.2
it follows for
k\geq k_{0}
that
\mathcal{C}_{f}
is a well-defined distribution for all
f
outside a subset in
A_{k}
of
\mu_{k}
-measure zero. For
x\in X
let
U^{x}_{k}
be a unitary transformation of
\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}
with
U^{x}_{k}\overline{F_{k}(x)}/|F_{k}(z)|=(1,0,\ldots,0)
and given
b\in\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}
let us denote by
(U^{x}_{k}b)_{j}
the
j
-th component of the vector
U^{x}_{k}b
. We have
(U^{x}_{k}b)_{0}=\langle F_{k}(x),\overline{b}\rangle/|F_{k}(x)|
since
U^{x}_{k}
is a unitary transformation and basic properties of the inner product in
\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}
. We find
\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}}a_{j}/a_{0}d\mu^{G}(a)=0
for all
j\geq 1
. With
\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}}d\mu^{G}(a)=1
it follows that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}}\frac{\langle a,\overline{(dF_{k})_{x}}\rangle(V)}{\langle a,\overline{F_{k}(x)}\rangle}d\mu^{G}(a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}}\frac{\langle a,U^{z}_{k}\overline{VF_{k}}\rangle}{a_{0}}d\mu^{G}(a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{(U^{x}_{k}\overline{(VF_{k})(z)}/|F_{k}(x)|)_{0}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\langle VF_{k},F_{k}(x)\rangle}{|F_{k}(x)|^{2}}

for all
k\geq k_{0}
,
x\in X
and
V\in T_{x}X
.
We have
|F_{k}(x)|^{2}=|\kappa(k)|^{2}+\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,x)
and
\langle VF_{k},F_{k}(x)\rangle=(d_{x}\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,y)|_{x=y})(V)
for any
V\in T_{x}X
. Hence given
\psi\in\Omega^{2n}(X)
we conclude

\displaystyle\int_{X}2\pi i\beta_{k}\wedge\psi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{X}\left(\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}}\frac{\langle a,\overline{dF_{k}}\rangle}{\langle a,\overline{F_{k}}\rangle}d\mu^{G}(a)\right)\wedge\psi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{X}\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}}\frac{\langle a,\overline{dF_{k}}\rangle}{\langle a,\overline{F_{k}}\rangle}\wedge\psi d\mu^{G}(a).

By Lemma
5.2
we have the
\mathcal{C}_{f}
is well defined for all
f
in a subset
\tilde{A}_{k}
of
A_{k}
with
\mu_{k}(\tilde{A}_{k})=1
. Using Lemma
5.4
we can apply the Fubini-Tonelli theorem and get

\displaystyle\int_{X}\beta_{k}\wedge\psi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}}\int_{X}\frac{1}{2\pi i}\frac{\langle a,\overline{dF_{k}}\rangle}{\langle a,\overline{F_{k}}\rangle}\wedge\psi d\mu^{G}(a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{A_{k}}\int_{X}\frac{1}{2\pi i}\frac{df}{f}\wedge\psi d\mu_{k}(f)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\tilde{A}_{k}}\mathcal{C}_{f}(\psi)d\mu_{k}(f)=\mathbb{E}_{k}(\mathcal{C}_{f})

for all
\psi\in\Omega^{2n}(X)
.
∎

Corollary 5.6
.

Under the same assumptions as in Theorem
1.1
and with the notations above there exist
k_{0}>0

such that for each
k\geq k_{0}
we have that
\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right]
is a well-defined current for all
f
outside a subset in
A_{k}
of
\mu_{k}
-measure zero and the expectation value
\mathbb{E}_{k}(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right])
defined by

\left(\mathbb{E}_{k}(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right],\psi\right)=\int_{A_{k}}\left(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right],\psi\right)d\mu_{k}(f),\,\,\,\,\psi\in\Omega^{2n-1}(X)

exists
with
\mathbb{E}_{k}(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right])=d\beta_{k}
where
\beta_{k}
is given by (
5.1.4
).

Proof.

From Theorem
4.21
we obtain
\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right]=d\mathcal{C}_{f}
in the sense of distributions for any
f\in H^{0}_{b}(X)\cap C^{\infty}(X)
such that zero is a regular value of the map
f\colon X\to\mathbb{C}
. Hence, using Lemma
5.2
and integration by parts, we obtain from Theorem
5.5
with
\kappa(k)=0
that

\left(\mathbb{E}_{k}(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right]),\psi\right)=\mathbb{E}_{k}(\mathcal{C}_{f})(d\psi)=\int_{X}\beta_{k}\wedge d\psi=\int_{X}d\beta_{k}\wedge\psi

holds for all
\psi\in\Omega^{2n-1}(X)
when
k
is large enough.
∎

Lemma 5.7
.

Under the same assumptions as in Theorem
1.1
and with
\beta_{k}
given by (
5.1.4
) we have

k^{-1}\beta_{k}=\frac{\operatorname{mv}(|\chi|^{2})}{2\pi}\frac{\xi}{\sigma_{P}(\xi)}+O(k^{-1})

in
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}
-topology for
k\to+\infty
where

(5.1.5)

\displaystyle\operatorname{mv}(|\chi|^{2})=\frac{\int_{0}^{+\infty}t^{n+1}|\chi(t)|^{2}dt}{\int_{0}^{+\infty}t^{n}|\chi(t)|^{2}dt}.

Proof.

The statement follows immediately from Theorem
2.10
and Lemma
2.11
.
∎

Corollary 5.8
.

In the situation of Theorem
5.5
there exists
k_{0},C>0
such that

\left|k^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{k}(\mathcal{C}_{f})(\psi)-\frac{\operatorname{mv}(|\chi|^{2})}{2\pi}\int_{X}\frac{\xi}{\sigma_{P}(\xi)}\wedge\psi\right|\leq k^{-1}C\|\psi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{0}(X,\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}^{2n}T^{*}X)}

holds for all
\psi\in\Omega^{2n}(X)
and
k\geq k_{0}
where
\operatorname{mv}(|\chi|^{2})
is given by (
5.1.5
).
As a consequence one has

\lim_{k\to\infty}k^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{k}(\mathcal{C}_{f})=\frac{\operatorname{mv}(|\chi|^{2})}{2\pi}\frac{\xi}{\sigma_{P}(\xi)}.

Proof.

The result is a direct consequence of Theorem
5.5
and Lemma
5.7
.
∎

5.2.
Proof of Theorem
1.7

We will now prove Theorem
1.7
. We start with the following lemma.

Lemma 5.9
.

There is a constant
C>0
such that for all
w\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
we have

\int_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{d\mu^{G}(a)}{|a||a-w|}\leq\frac{C}{|w|}.

Proof.

We have

\int_{|a|\leq|w|/2}\frac{d\mu^{G}(a)}{|a||a-w|}\leq\frac{1}{|w|}\int_{|a|\leq|w|/2}\frac{d\mu^{G}(a)}{|a||a/w-1|}\leq\frac{2}{|w|}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{d\mu^{G}(a)}{|a|}

and

\int_{|a|\geq|w|/2}\frac{d\mu^{G}(a)}{|a||a-w|}\leq\frac{2}{|w|}\int_{|a|\geq|w|/2}\frac{d\mu^{G}(a)}{|a-w|}\leq\frac{2}{|w|}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{d\mu^{G}(a)}{|a-w|}.

We have that the function
w\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{d\mu^{G}(a)}{|a-w|}
is bounded. The claim follows.
∎

It turns out that it in order to understand the variance of
\mathcal{C}_{f}
, it is useful to consider the following objects.

Definition 5.10
.

With the notation and assumptions above for any
k>0
such that
|F_{k}|^{2}>0
put

(Z_{k})_{x}:=\overline{(dF_{k})_{x}}-\frac{\langle F_{k}(x),(dF_{k})_{x}\rangle}{|F_{k}(x)|^{2}}\overline{F_{k}(x)}

and
\theta_{k}(a,x,y)\colon T_{x}X\times T_{y}X\to\mathbb{C}
,

\displaystyle\theta_{k}(a,x,y)(V,W)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\frac{\langle a,Z_{k}(V)\rangle\overline{\langle a,Z_{k}(W)\rangle}}{\langle a,\overline{F_{k}(x)}\rangle\overline{\langle a,\overline{F_{k}(y)}\rangle}}

for
x,y\in X
and
a\in\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}
such that
\langle a,\overline{F_{k}(x)}\rangle\overline{\langle a,\overline{F_{k}(y)}\rangle}\neq 0
.

By the assumption
|F_{k}|^{2}>0
, for
x,y\in X
fixed, it follows that
\theta_{k}(a,x,y)
is well defined for
\mu^{G}
-almost every
a\in\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}
. Recall the definition of
h^{F_{k}}
(see Definition
3.1
) that is

h^{F_{k}}(x,y)=\frac{|\langle F_{k}(x),F_{k}(y)\rangle|^{2}}{|F_{k}(x)|^{2}|F_{k}(y)|^{2}},\,\,\,x,y\in X

for all
k>0
with
|F_{k}|^{2}>0
.
In order to obtain estimates for the variance of
\mathcal{C}_{f}
we need to understand the behavior of
\theta_{k}(a,x,y)
and its integrals with respect to
a,x,y
when
k
becomes large. In the following two lemmata we consider
\theta_{k}
for sufficiently large
k>0
.

Lemma 5.11
.

With the notation and assumptions above there exist
k_{0}>0
and constants
c_{k}>0
such that

(5.2.1)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}}|\theta_{k}(a,x,y)(V,W)|d\mu^{G}(a)\leq\frac{c_{k}|V||W|}{\sqrt{1-h^{F_{k}}(z,w)}}

for all
k\geq k_{0}
,
x,y\in X
with
x\neq y
and all
V\in T_{x}X
,
W\in T_{y}X
. Furthermore we can choose
k_{0}>0
such that
\gamma_{k}(x,y)\colon T_{x}X\times T_{y}X\to\mathbb{C}
,

(5.2.2)

\displaystyle\gamma_{k}(x,y)(V,W):=\sqrt{1-h^{F_{k}}(x,y)}\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}}\theta_{k}(a,x,y)(V,W)d\mu^{G}(a)

is well defined for all
k\geq k_{0}
and
x,y\in X
,
x\neq y
, and that there exist a constant
C>0
with

(5.2.3)

\displaystyle|\gamma_{k}(x,y)(V,W)|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\left|\left\langle\frac{Z_{k}(V)}{|F_{k}(x)|},\frac{Z_{k}(W)}{|F_{k}(y)|}\right\rangle\right|+\frac{1}{1-h^{F_{k}}(x,y)}\left|\left\langle\frac{Z_{k}(V)}{|F_{k}(x)|},\frac{\overline{F_{k}(y)}}{{|F_{k}(y)|}}\right\rangle\right|\left|\left\langle\frac{Z_{k}(W)}{|F_{k}(y)|},\frac{\overline{F_{k}(x)}}{|F_{k}(x)|}\right\rangle\right|\right).

for all
k\geq k_{0}
,
x,y\in X
,
x\neq y
and
V\in T_{x}X
,
W\in T_{y}X
.

Proof.

Recalling that
|F_{k}|^{2}=|\kappa(k)|^{2}+B_{k}
by Theorem
2.10
we can choose
k_{1}>0
and a constant
C_{0}>0
such that
C_{0}^{-1}k^{n+1}\leq|F_{k}|^{2}\leq C_{0}k^{n+1}
for all
k\geq k_{1}
. In addition, we choose
k_{1}
large enough such that for all
k\geq k_{1}
and
x,y\in X
we have
h^{F_{k}}(x,y)=1
if and only if
x=y
(see Lemma
3.8
). Furthermore, since

|VF_{k}|^{2}=(d_{x}\otimes d_{y}\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,y))|_{x=y}V\otimes V

for
V\in T_{x}X
we find by Lemma
2.11
that there is a constant
C_{1}>0
such that

(5.2.4)

\displaystyle|VF_{k}|\leq C_{1}k|F_{k}(x)||V|

for all
k\geq k_{1}
,
x\in X
and
V\in T_{x}X
.
For
x,y\in X
,
V\in T_{x}X
and
W\in T_{y}X
put

I_{k}(x,y)(V,W):=\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}}+1}|\theta_{k}(a,x,y)(V,W)|d\mu^{G}(a).

For
x,y\in X
we can choose a unitary transformation
U^{x,y}_{k}
of
\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}
such
U^{x,y}_{k}\frac{\overline{F_{k}(x)}}{|F_{k}(x)|}=(1,0,\ldots,0)
and
U^{x,y}_{k}\frac{\overline{F_{k}(y)}}{|F_{k}(y)|}=(\alpha,\beta,0\ldots,0)
with
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{C}
,
|\alpha|^{2}+|\beta|^{2}=1
. One observes that
\alpha|F_{k}(x)||F_{k}(y)|=\langle F_{k}(x),F_{k}(y)\rangle
and hence
|\alpha|^{2}=h^{F_{k}}(x,y)
. Then in addition we can choose
U^{x,y}_{k}
such that
\beta=\sqrt{1-h^{F_{k}}(x,y)}
.
Since
\|Z_{k}(V)\|\leq 2|VF_{k}|
for all
V\in T_{x}X
we find with (
5.2.4
) for
x\neq y
that

\displaystyle I_{k}(x,y)(V,W)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}}+1}\left|\frac{\langle a,U_{k}^{x,y}Z_{k}(V)\rangle\overline{\langle a,U^{x,y}_{k}Z_{k}(W)\rangle}}{|F_{k}(x)||F_{k}(y)||a_{0}||\alpha a_{0}+\beta a_{1}|}\right|d\mu^{G}(a)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}}+1}\frac{|a|^{2}|Z_{k}(V)||Z_{k}(W)|}{|F_{k}(x)||F_{k}(y)||a_{0}||\alpha a_{0}+\beta a_{1}|}d\mu^{G}(a)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(2kC_{1})^{2}|V||W|\left(\int_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}\frac{|a_{0}|}{|\alpha a_{0}+\beta a_{1}|}d\mu^{G}(a)+\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}}+1}\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}+1}|a_{j}|^{2}}{|a_{0}||\alpha a_{0}+\beta a_{1}|}d\mu^{G}(a)\right)

By some unitary transformation argument we find that
\int_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}\frac{|a_{0}|}{|\alpha a_{0}+\beta a_{1}|}d\mu^{G}(a)
is uniformly bounded in
\alpha,\beta
when
|\alpha|^{2}+|\beta|^{2}=1
. Using Lemma
5.9
we find a constant
c>0
independent of
\beta
and
a_{1}\in\mathbb{C}
such that

(5.2.5)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{1}{|a_{0}||\alpha a_{0}+\beta a_{1}|}d\mu^{G}(a_{0})\leq\frac{c}{\beta|a_{1}|}.

With

\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}}+1}\frac{\sum_{j=2}^{N_{k}+1}|a_{j}|^{2}}{|a_{0}||\alpha a_{0}+\beta a_{1}|}d\mu^{G}(a)\leq\frac{c}{\beta}\left(\int_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{1}{|a_{1}|}d\mu^{G}(a_{1})\right)\left(\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}-1}}|a^{\prime}|^{2}d\mu^{G}(a^{\prime})\right)

we conclude that for any
k\geq k_{1}
there is a constant
c_{k}>0
depending on
k
such that

I_{k}(x,y)(V,W)\leq c_{k}(1+\frac{1}{\beta})|V||W|
holds for all
x,y\in X
,
x\neq y
, and
V\in T_{x}X
,
W\in T_{y}X
. Since
\beta=\sqrt{1-h^{F_{k}}(z,w)}\leq 1
we obtain (
5.2.1
).

Now we can prove the second part of the statement. For
x,y\in X
,
x\neq y
, define
II_{k}(x,y)\colon T_{x}X\times T_{y}Y\to\mathbb{C}
,

II_{k}(z,w)(V,W):=\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}}\theta_{k}(a,z,w)(V,W)d\mu^{G}(a).

First, from (
5.2.1
) we obtain by the choice of
k_{1}
at the beginning of the proof that
II_{k}(x,y)
is well defined for all
x,y\in X
,
x\neq y
, and all
k\geq k_{1}
. In the following we will calculate
II_{k}(x,y)
.

Fix
V\in T_{x}X
,
W\in T_{y}X
where
x\neq y
. Put
\kappa_{j}=\left(U^{x,y}_{k}Z_{k}(V)\right)_{j}
and
\lambda_{j}=\left(U^{x,y}_{k}Z_{k}(W)\right)_{j}
where we denote by
(b)_{j}
the
j
-th component of a vector
b\in\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}
. We find

\kappa_{0}=\left\langle U^{x,y}_{k}Z_{k}(V),U^{x,y}_{k}\frac{\overline{F_{k}(x)}}{|F_{k}(x)|}\right\rangle=\left\langle Z_{k}(V),\frac{\overline{F_{k}(x)}}{|F_{k}(x)|}\right\rangle=0

and

\beta\kappa_{1}=\alpha\kappa_{0}+\beta\kappa_{1}=\left\langle U^{x,y}_{k}Z_{k}(V),U^{x,y}_{k}\frac{\overline{F_{k}(y)}}{|F_{k}(y)|}\right\rangle=\left\langle Z_{k}(V),\frac{\overline{F_{k}(y)}}{|F_{k}(y)|}\right\rangle.

Furthermore, we find

\lambda_{0}=\left\langle U^{x,y}_{k}Z_{k}(W),U^{x,y}_{k}\frac{\overline{F_{k}(x)}}{|F_{k}(x)|}\right\rangle=\left\langle Z_{k}(W),\frac{\overline{F_{k}(x)}}{|F_{k}(x)|}\right\rangle

and

\alpha\lambda_{0}+\beta\lambda_{1}=\left\langle U^{x,y}_{k}Z_{k}(W),U^{x,y}_{k}\frac{\overline{F_{k}(y)}}{|F_{k}(y)|}\right\rangle=\left\langle U^{x,y}_{k}Z_{k}(W),U^{x,y}_{k}\frac{\overline{F_{k}(y)}}{|F_{k}(y)|}\right\rangle=0.

Summing up we have

\displaystyle\kappa_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle\kappa_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\beta}\left\langle Z_{k}(V),\frac{\overline{F_{k}(y)}}{|F_{k}(y)|}\right\rangle,

\displaystyle\lambda_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\langle Z_{k}(W),\frac{\overline{F_{k}(x)}}{|F_{k}(x)|}\right\rangle,

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}\left\langle Z_{k}(W),\frac{\overline{F_{k}(x)}}{|F_{k}(x)|}\right\rangle.

Furthermore, we observe that

\sum_{j=0}^{N_{k}}\kappa_{j}\overline{\lambda_{j}}=\langle U^{z,w}_{k}Z_{k}(V),U^{z,w}_{k}Z_{k}(W)\rangle=\langle Z_{k}(V),Z_{k}(W)\rangle.

Put
c^{\prime}=\int_{\mathbb{C}}|a|^{2}d\mu^{G}(a)
. Since
x\neq y
we have
\beta\neq 0
. Then
\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}}\frac{a_{j}\overline{a_{l}}}{a_{0}\overline{(\alpha a_{0}+\beta a_{1})}}d\mu^{G}(a)=0
for all
0\leq j,l\leq N_{k}
with
j\neq l
and
(j,l)\notin\{(1,0),(0,1)\}
. We check that

\displaystyle II_{k}(x,y)(V,W)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}}+1}\frac{\langle a,U^{x,y}_{k}Z_{k}(V)\rangle\overline{\langle a,U^{x,y}Z_{k}(W)\rangle}}{|F_{k}(x)||F_{k}(y)|a_{0}\overline{(\alpha a_{0}+\beta a_{1})}}d\mu^{G}(a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}\frac{c^{\prime}d\mu^{G}(a)}{a_{0}\overline{(\alpha a_{0}+\beta a_{1})}}\right)\sum_{j=2}^{N_{k}}\frac{\kappa_{j}\overline{\lambda_{j}}}{|F_{k}(x)||F_{k}(y)|}

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}\frac{|a_{0}|^{2}\kappa_{0}\overline{\lambda_{0}}+|a_{1}|^{2}\kappa_{1}\overline{\lambda_{1}}+a_{0}\overline{a_{1}}\kappa_{0}\overline{\lambda_{1}}+a_{1}\overline{a_{0}}\kappa_{1}\overline{\lambda_{0}}}{|F_{k}(x)||F_{k}(y)|a_{0}\overline{(\alpha a_{0}+\beta a_{1})}}d\mu^{G}(a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}\frac{c^{\prime}d\mu^{G}(a)}{a_{0}\overline{(\alpha a_{0}+\beta a_{1})}}\right)\left\langle\frac{Z_{k}(V)}{|F_{k}(x)|},\frac{Z_{k}(W)}{|F_{k}(y)|}\right\rangle

\displaystyle+\left(\int_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}\frac{c^{\prime}d\mu^{G}(a)}{a_{0}\overline{(\alpha a_{0}+\beta a_{1})}}\right)\frac{\overline{\alpha}}{\beta^{2}}\left\langle\frac{Z_{k}(V)}{|F_{k}(x)|},\frac{\overline{F_{k}(y)}}{|F_{k}(y)|}\right\rangle\overline{\left\langle\frac{Z_{k}(W)}{|F_{k}(y)|},\frac{\overline{F_{k}(x)}}{|F_{k}(x)|}\right\rangle}

\displaystyle+\left(\int_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}\frac{a_{1}\overline{(\beta a_{0}-\alpha a_{1})}}{a_{0}\overline{(\alpha a_{0}+\beta a_{1})}}d\mu^{G}(a)\right)\frac{1}{\beta^{2}}\left\langle\frac{Z_{k}(V)}{|F_{k}(x)|},\frac{\overline{F_{k}(y)}}{{|F_{k}(y)|}}\right\rangle\overline{\left\langle\frac{Z_{k}(W)}{|F_{k}(y)|},\frac{\overline{F_{k}(x)}}{|F_{k}(x)|}\right\rangle}.

With (
5.2.5
) and since

\left|\int_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}\frac{a_{1}\overline{(\beta a_{0}-\alpha a_{1})}}{a_{0}\overline{(\alpha a_{0}+\beta a_{1})}}d\mu^{G}(a)\right|\leq\int_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}\frac{|a_{1}|^{2}+|a_{0}||a_{1}|}{|a_{0}||\alpha a_{0}+\beta a_{1}|}d\mu^{G}(a)\leq\frac{c}{\beta}c^{\prime}+\int_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}\frac{|a_{1}|}{|\alpha a_{0}+\beta a_{1}|}d\mu^{G}(a)

we conclude with
\beta=\sqrt{1-h^{F_{k}}(x,y)}
that there is a constant
C_{2}>0
independent of
k
,
x
,
y
and
V\in T_{x}X
,
W\in T_{y}X
such that

\gamma_{k}(x,y)\colon T_{x}X\times T_{y}X\to\mathbb{C}
defined by
\gamma_{k}(x,y)=\sqrt{1-h^{F_{k}}(x,y)}II_{k}(x,y)
satisfies

\displaystyle|\gamma_{k}(x,y)(V,W)|

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}\left(\left|\left\langle\frac{Z_{k}(V)}{|F_{k}(x)|},\frac{Z_{k}(W)}{|F_{k}(y)|}\right\rangle\right|+\frac{1}{1-h^{F_{k}}(x,y)}\left|\left\langle\frac{Z_{k}(V)}{|F_{k}(x)|},\frac{\overline{F_{k}(y)}}{{|F_{k}(y)|}}\right\rangle\right|\left|\left\langle\frac{Z_{k}(W)}{|F_{k}(y)|},\frac{\overline{F_{k}(x)}}{|F_{k}(x)|}\right\rangle\right|\right).

∎

Lemma 5.12
.

With the notation and assumptions above
given
0<\varepsilon<1
and a volume form
dV
on
X\times X
there exist
C,k_{0}>0
such that

(5.2.6)

\displaystyle\int_{X\times X}\left|\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}}\theta_{k}d\mu^{G}\right|dV\leq Ck^{2-n-1}(|\kappa(k)|^{2}+k^{\varepsilon})

for all
k\geq k_{0}
.

Proof.

As in the proof of Lemma
5.11
we can find
k_{1},C_{0},C_{1}>0
such that

(5.2.7)

\displaystyle C_{0}^{-1}k^{n+1}\leq|F_{k}|^{2}\leq C_{0}k^{n+1}

and

(5.2.8)

\displaystyle|VF_{k}|\leq C_{1}k|F_{k}(x)||V|

for all
k\geq k_{1}
,
x\in X
and
V\in T_{x}X
.
In addition, we choose
k_{1}
large enough such that for all
k\geq k_{1}
and
x,y\in X
we have
h^{F_{k}}(x,y)=1
if and only if
x=y
(see Lemma
3.8
). Recall that given
x\neq y
,
V\in T_{x}X
,
W\in T_{y}X
and
k\geq k_{1}
we have

\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}}\theta_{k}(a,x,y)(V,W)d\mu^{G}(a)=\frac{\gamma_{k}(x,y)(V,W)}{\sqrt{1-h^{F_{k}}(x,y)}}

where
\gamma_{k}(x,y)(V,W)
defined in (
5.2.2
) satisfies the estimate (
5.2.3
).
Using (
5.2.3
) we would like to estimate
\left|\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}}\theta_{k}(a,x,y)d\mu^{G}(a)\right|
and its integral over
X\times X
. First we will show that
k^{-2}|\gamma_{k}(x,y)|
is uniformly bounded in
x
,
y
and
k
. Then we will estimate
|\gamma_{k}(x,y)|
outside the diagonal in
X\times X
interms of
\eta(T_{P})(x,y)
. As consequence we obtain (
5.2.6
) using Lemma
3.15
and Lemma
3.16
.

One can check that

\displaystyle\left\langle\frac{Z_{k}(V)}{|F_{k}(x)|},\frac{\overline{F_{k}(y)}}{{|F_{k}(y)|}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\langle\frac{\overline{VF_{k}}}{|F_{k}(x)|},\frac{\overline{F_{k}(y)}}{{|F_{k}(y)|}}\right\rangle-\left\langle\frac{\overline{VF_{k}}}{|F_{k}(x)|},\frac{\overline{F_{k}(x)}}{{|F_{k}(x)|}}\right\rangle\left\langle\frac{\overline{F_{k}(x)}}{|F_{k}(x)|},\frac{\overline{F_{k}(y)}}{|F_{k}(y)|}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\langle\frac{\overline{VF_{k}}}{|F_{k}(x)|},\frac{\overline{F_{k}(y)}}{{|F_{k}(y)|}}-\left\langle\frac{\overline{F_{k}(x)}}{|F_{k}(x)|},\frac{\overline{F_{k}(y)}}{|F_{k}(y)|}\right\rangle\frac{\overline{F_{k}(x)}}{|F_{k}(x)|}\right\rangle

and

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\overline{F_{k}(y)}}{{|F_{k}(y)|}}-\left\langle\frac{\overline{F_{k}(x)}}{|F_{k}(x)|},\frac{\overline{F_{k}(y)}}{|F_{k}(y)|}\right\rangle\frac{\overline{F_{k}(x)}}{|F_{k}(x)|}\right|^{2}=1-h^{F_{k}}(x,y).

Hence it follows

\left|\left\langle\frac{Z_{k}(V)}{|F_{k}(x)|},\frac{\overline{F_{k}(y)}}{{|F_{k}(y)|}}\right\rangle\right|\leq\sqrt{1-h_{k}(x,y)}\frac{|VF_{k}|}{|F_{k}(x)|}\leq\sqrt{1-h_{k}(z,w)}C_{1}k|V|

and in the same way we obtain

\left|\left\langle\frac{Z_{k}(W)}{|F_{k}(y)|},\frac{\overline{F_{k}(x)}}{{|F_{k}(x)|}}\right\rangle\right|\leq\sqrt{1-h_{k}(z,w)}C_{1}k|W|.

With

\left|\left\langle\frac{Z_{k}(V)}{|F_{k}(x)|},\frac{Z_{k}(W)}{|F_{k}(y)|}\right\rangle\right|\leq\frac{4|VF_{k}||WF_{k}|}{|F_{k}(x)||F_{k}(y)|}\leq 4(C_{1}k)^{2}|V||W|

we conclude that there exists a constant
C_{3}>0
such that
|\gamma_{k}(x,y)(V,W)|\leq C_{3}k^{2}|V||W|

for all
k\geq k_{1}
,
x,y\in X
,
x\neq y
, and
V\in T_{x}X
,
W\in T_{x}Y
. The metric on TX induces a metric on the bilinear forms from
T_{x}X\times T_{y}X
to
\mathbb{C}
. It follows that we can choose the constant
C_{3}>0
such that

(5.2.9)

\displaystyle|\gamma_{k}(x,y)|\leq C_{3}k^{2}

for all
k\geq k_{1}
,
x,y\in X
,
x\neq y
.

In order to obtain estimates for the integral of
\left|\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}}\theta_{k}(a,x,y)d\mu^{G}(a)\right|
over
X\times X
using Lemma
3.16
we need to estimate
|\gamma_{k}(x,y)|
in terms of
\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,y)
.

With
|\langle VF_{k},F_{k}(x)\rangle|\leq kC_{1}|F_{k}(x)|^{2}|V|
(see
5.2.8
) we find

(5.2.10)

\displaystyle|\langle Z_{k}(V),Z_{k}(W)\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|\langle VF_{k},WF_{k}\rangle|+(C_{1}k)^{2}|\langle F_{k}(x),F_{k}(y)\rangle|V||W|

\displaystyle+C_{1}k|\langle VF_{k},F_{k}(y)\rangle||W|+C_{1}k|V||\langle F_{k}(x),WF_{k}\rangle|

and

(5.2.11)

\displaystyle|\langle Z_{k}(V),\overline{F_{k}(y)}\rangle|\leq|\langle VF_{k},F_{k}(y)\rangle|+k|\langle F_{k}(x),F_{k}(y)\rangle|.

We note that

\displaystyle\langle VF_{k},WF_{k}\rangle=d_{x}\otimes d_{y}\eta_{k}(T_{p})(x,y).

Then, by the compactness of
X
, we can find finitely many partial differential operators
Q_{j}\colon\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(X)\to\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(X)
,
1\leq j\leq r
of order at most two such that

(5.2.12)

\displaystyle|\langle VF_{k},WF_{k}\rangle|\leq\sum_{j=1}^{r}|Q_{j}\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,y)||V||W|

for all
k\geq k_{1}
,
x,y\in X
,
V\in T_{x}X
and
W\in T_{y}X
. Since

\displaystyle\langle VF_{k},F_{k}(y)\rangle=d_{x}\eta_{k}(T_{p})(x,y)\text{ and }\langle F_{k}(x),WF_{k}\rangle=d_{y}\eta_{k}(T_{p})(x,y)

we can find finitely many partial differential operators
L_{j}\colon\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(X)\to\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(X)
,
1\leq j\leq s
of order at most one such that

(5.2.13)

\displaystyle|\langle VF_{k},F_{k}(x)\rangle||W|+|\langle F_{k}(x),WF_{k}\rangle||V|\leq\sum_{j=1}^{s}|L_{j}\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,y)||V||W|

for all
k\geq k_{1}
,
x,y\in X
,
V\in T_{x}X
and
W\in T_{y}X
. Put

\gamma^{\prime}_{k}(x,y):=k^{2}|\kappa(k)|^{2}+k^{2}|\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,y)|+k\sum_{j=1}^{s}|L_{j}\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,y)|+\sum_{j=1}^{r}|Q_{j}\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,y)|.

Recall that
\langle F_{k}(x),F_{k}(y)\rangle=|\kappa(k)|^{2}+\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,y)
,
\langle F_{k}(x),F_{k}(y)\rangle\leq|F_{k}(x)||F_{k}(y)|
,
|\langle VF_{k},F_{k}(y)\rangle|\leq C_{1}k|F_{k}(x)||F_{k}(y)|
and
|F_{k}|^{2}\geq C_{0}^{-1}k^{n+1}
for all
k\geq k_{1}
and
x,y\in X
.
Then from (
5.2.3
), (
5.2.10
), (
5.2.11
), (
5.2.12
) and (
5.2.13
) we have that given any
\delta>0
we can find a constant
C_{4}>0
such that

|\gamma_{k}(x,y)|\leq C_{4}k^{-n-1}\gamma^{\prime}_{k}(x,y).

for all
x,y\in X
with
h^{F_{k}}(x,y)\leq 1-\delta
and all
k\geq k_{1}
.
Given
0<\varepsilon<1
we can choose
k_{2}\geq k_{1}
to conclude from Lemma
3.16
that there exists
C_{5}>0
with

(5.2.14)

\displaystyle\int_{h^{F_{k}}<1-\delta}|\gamma_{k}|dV\leq C_{4}k^{-n-1}\int_{X\times X}\gamma^{\prime}_{k}dV\leq C_{5}(|\kappa(k)|^{2}k^{2-n-1}+k^{2+\varepsilon-n-1})

for all
k\geq k_{2}
.
From (
5.2.9
), (
5.2.14
) and Lemma
3.15
we conclude that there exist a constant
C>0
and
k_{0}>0
such that

\int_{X\times X}\left|\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}}\theta_{k}d\mu^{G}\right|dV=\int_{X\times X}\frac{|\gamma_{k}|}{\sqrt{1-h^{F_{k}}}}dV\leq Ck^{2-n-1}(|\kappa(k)|^{2}+k^{\varepsilon})

for all
k\geq k_{0}
.
∎

Using Lemma
5.11
and Lemma
5.12
we obtain variance estimate for
\mathcal{C}_{f}
.

Theorem 5.13
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
1.1
and with the notations above
put

\mathbb{V}_{k}(\mathcal{C}_{f}(\psi)):=\int_{A_{k}}\left|\mathcal{C}_{f}(\psi)-\mathbb{E}_{k}(\mathcal{C}_{f})(\psi)\right|^{2}d\mu_{k}(f),\,\,\,\psi\in\Omega^{2n}(X).

Given
0<\varepsilon<1
there exist
k_{0}>0
and
C>0
such that
\mathbb{V}_{k}(\mathcal{C}_{f}(\psi))
is well-defined with

\mathbb{V}_{k}(\mathcal{C}_{f}(\psi))\leq Ck^{2-n-1}(|\kappa(k)|^{2}+k^{\varepsilon})\|\psi\|^{2}_{\mathscr{C}^{0}(X,\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}^{2n}T^{*}X)}

for all
k\geq k_{0}
and all
\psi\in\Omega^{2n}(X)
.

Proof.

Let
dV
be a volume form on
X\times X
.
Choose
k_{1}>0
such that
|F_{k}|>0
for all
k\geq k_{1}
and that the statements in Lemma
5.11
hold true. We have that
\theta_{k}
as in Definition
5.10
induces an alternating multiliniear form
\hat{\theta}_{k}(a,x,y)
on
T_{(x,y)}X\times X
for all
x,y\in X
and
\mu^{G}
-almost every
a\in\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}
. Define
p_{1},p_{2}\colon X\times X\to X
,
p_{1}(x,y)=x
,
p_{2}(x,y)=y
and put

(5.2.15)

\displaystyle R_{k}(\psi):=\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}}\int_{X\times X}\hat{\theta}_{k}\wedge p_{1}^{*}\psi\wedge\overline{p_{2}^{*}\psi}d\mu^{G}(a),\,\,\,\,\psi\in\Omega^{2n}(X).

By Lemma
5.11
and the Fubini-Tonelli theorem we have that
R_{k}(\psi)
exists for any
\psi\in\Omega^{2n}(X)
and
k\geq k_{1}
with

\displaystyle|R_{k}(\psi)|=\left|\int_{X\times X}\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}}\hat{\theta}_{k}\wedge p_{1}^{*}\psi\wedge\overline{p_{2}^{*}\psi}\right|\leq C_{1}\left(\int_{X\times X}\left|\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}}\theta_{k}d\mu^{G}\right|dV\right)\|\psi\|^{2}_{\mathscr{C}^{0}(X,\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}^{2n}T^{*}X)},

where
C_{1}>0
is a constant independent of
k
and
\psi
.
Hence, using Lemma
5.12
, we conclude that for any
0<\varepsilon<1
there exist
k_{0}\geq k_{1}
and
C>0
such that

(5.2.16)

\displaystyle|R_{k}(\psi)|\leq Ck^{2-n-1}(|\kappa(k)|^{2}+k^{\varepsilon})\|\psi\|^{2}_{\mathscr{C}^{0}(X,\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}^{2n}T^{*}X)}

for all
\psi\in\Omega^{2n}(X)
and
k\geq k_{0}
.
Recall that for any
k\geq k_{0}
there exists
\tilde{A}_{k}\subset A_{k}
with
\mu_{k}(\tilde{A}_{k})=1
such that for any
f\in\tilde{A}_{k}
we have

(5.2.17)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{f}(\psi)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{X}\frac{df}{f}\wedge\psi,\,\,\,\,\,\psi\in\Omega^{2n}(X).

By Theorem
5.5
we have
\mathbb{E}_{k}(\mathcal{C}_{f})(\psi)=\int_{X}\beta_{k}\wedge\psi
for all
\psi\in\Omega^{2n}(X)
with

(5.2.18)

\displaystyle\beta_{k}=\frac{\langle dF_{k},F_{k}\rangle}{2\pi i|F_{k}|^{2}}.

Writing
f=\langle a,\overline{F_{k}}\rangle
,
a\in\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}
, we find

(5.2.19)

\displaystyle\frac{df}{f}-2\pi i\beta_{k}=\frac{\langle a,\overline{dF_{k}}\rangle-\frac{\langle dF_{k},F_{k}\rangle}{|F_{k}|^{2}}\langle a,\overline{F_{k}}\rangle}{\langle a,\overline{F_{k}}\rangle}=\frac{\langle a,Z_{k}\rangle}{\langle a,\overline{F_{k}}\rangle}

for all
k\geq k_{0}
and
\mu^{G}
-almost every
a\in\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}
.
Given
\psi\in\Omega^{2n}(X)
and
k\geq k_{0}
we obtain from the definition of
\theta_{k}
and (
5.2.19
) that

\displaystyle R_{k}(\psi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}}\int_{X\times X}p_{1}^{*}\left(\frac{\langle a,Z_{k}\rangle}{\langle a,\overline{F_{k}}\rangle}\wedge\psi\right)\wedge\overline{p_{2}^{*}\left(\frac{\langle a,Z_{k}\rangle}{\langle a,\overline{F_{k}}\rangle}\wedge\psi\right)}d\mu^{G}(a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}}\left|\int_{X}\frac{\langle a,Z_{k}\rangle}{\langle a,\overline{F_{k}}\rangle}\wedge\psi\right|^{2}d\mu^{G}(a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{A_{k}}\left|\int_{X}\left(\frac{df}{f}-2\pi i\beta_{k}\right)\wedge\psi\right|^{2}d\mu_{k}(f)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\pi^{2}\mathbb{V}_{k}(\mathcal{C}_{f}(\psi)).

Then the claim follows from (
5.2.16
).
∎

Theorem
1.7
follows from the next result by taking
\kappa(k)\equiv 0
and replacing
\psi
by
d\psi
(see Corollary
5.15
). Consider
k\in\mathbb{N}
and put

A_{\infty}=\Pi_{k=1}^{\infty}A_{k}
,
d\mu_{\infty}=\Pi_{k=1}^{\infty}d\mu_{k}
. We have the following.

Theorem 5.14
.

Under the same assumptions as in Theorem
1.1
and with the notations above, assuming that
|\kappa(k)|^{2}/(1+|k|^{n-1})|
is bounded, there exists
k_{0}>0
such that for
\mu_{\infty}
-almost every
f=(f_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in A_{\infty}
we have
that
\mathcal{C}_{f_{k}}(\psi)
is well defined for all
\psi\in\Omega^{2n}(X)
and
k\geq k_{0}
. Furthermore, given
\psi\in\Omega^{2n}(X)
, we have for
\mu_{\infty}
-almost every
f=(f_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in A_{\infty}
that

\lim_{k\to\infty}k^{-1}\mathcal{C}_{f_{k}}(\psi)=\frac{\operatorname{mv}(|\chi|^{2})}{2\pi}\int_{X}\frac{\xi}{\sigma_{P}(\xi)}\wedge\psi

where
\operatorname{mv}(|\chi|^{2})
is given by (
5.1.5
). In addition, there exists
C>0
such that for any
k\geq k_{0}
one has

(5.2.20)

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\,\,\mu_{k}\left(\left\{f\in A_{k}\colon\left|k^{-1}\mathcal{C}_{f}(\psi)-\frac{\operatorname{mv}(|\chi|^{2})}{2\pi}\int_{X}\frac{\xi}{\sigma_{P}(\xi)}\wedge\psi\right|\geq\frac{\|\psi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{0}(X,\Lambda^{2n-1}_{\mathbb{C}}T^{*}X)}}{\sqrt{k}}\right\}\right)\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{k}}

for all
\psi\in\Omega^{2n-1}(X)
.

Proof.

Given
\psi\in\Omega^{2n}(X)
and
k>0
put

R_{k}(\psi):=\int_{A_{k}}\left|k^{-1}\mathcal{C}_{f}(\psi)-\frac{\operatorname{mv}(|\chi|^{2})}{2\pi}\int_{X}\frac{\xi}{\sigma_{P}(\xi)}\wedge\psi\right|^{2}d\mu_{k}(f).

We will show that there exist
k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
and a constant
C>0
such that
R_{k}(\psi)
exists for all
k\geq k_{0}
with
R_{k}(\psi)\leq Ck^{-\frac{3}{2}}\|\psi\|^{2}_{\mathscr{C}^{0}(X,\Lambda^{2n}_{\mathbb{C}}T^{*}X)}
for all
k\geq k_{0}
and
\psi\in\Omega^{2n}(X)
. From the Markov inequality, the last part of the claim follows. Furthermore, considering
k\in\mathbb{N}
, it follows that
\sum_{k=k_{0}}^{\infty}R_{k}(\psi)\leq C_{0}\|\psi\|^{2}_{\mathscr{C}^{0}(X,\Lambda^{2n}_{\mathbb{C}}T^{*}X)}
for some constant
C_{0}
(independent of
\psi
) which proves the second part of the claim. The first part of the claim follows immediately from Theorem
5.5
.

Given
\psi\in\Omega^{2n}(X)
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
put

R^{\prime}_{k}(\psi):=\left|\frac{1}{k}\int_{X}\beta_{k}\wedge\psi-\frac{\operatorname{mv}(|\chi|^{2})}{2\pi}\int_{X}\frac{\xi}{\sigma_{P}(\xi)}\wedge\psi\right|^{2}

with
\beta_{k}
as in (
5.1.4
).
From Theorem
5.5
and Corollary
5.8
it follows that there exist
k_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
and
C_{1}>0
such that
R^{\prime}_{k}(\psi)\leq C_{1}k^{-2}\|\psi\|^{2}_{\mathscr{C}^{0}(X,\Lambda^{2n}_{\mathbb{C}}T^{*}X)}

holds for all
k\geq k_{0}
and
\psi\in\Omega^{2n}(X)
.
With Theorem
5.5
and
\mathbb{V}_{k}
as in Theorem
5.13
we obtain

R_{k}(\psi)\leq 2k^{-2}\mathbb{V}_{k}(\mathcal{C}_{f}(\psi))+2R^{\prime}_{k}(\psi)\int_{A_{k}}d\mu_{k}

Then it follows from Theorem
5.13
with
\varepsilon=\frac{1}{2}
that there are
k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
and a constant
C>0
such that
R_{k}(\psi)\leq Ck^{-\frac{3}{2}}\|\psi\|^{2}_{\mathscr{C}^{0}(X,\Lambda^{2n}_{\mathbb{C}}T^{*}X)}
for all
k\geq k_{0}
and
\psi\in\Omega^{2n}(X)
.
∎

Corollary 5.15
.

Under the same assumptions as in Theorem
1.1
, with the notations above and assuming
\kappa(k)\equiv 0
there exists
k_{0}>0
such that for
\mu_{\infty}
-almost every
f=(f_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in A_{\infty}
we have that
(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f_{k}\right)\right],\psi)
is well defined for all
\psi\in\Omega^{2n-1}(X)
and
k\geq k_{0}
. Furthermore, given any
\psi\in\Omega^{2n-1}(X)
, we have for
\mu_{\infty}
-almost every
f=(f_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in A_{\infty}
that

\lim_{k\to\infty}\left(k^{-1}\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f_{k}\right)\right],\psi\right)=\frac{\operatorname{mv}(|\chi|^{2})}{2\pi}\int_{X}d\alpha_{P}\wedge\psi

where
\alpha_{P}=(\sigma_{P}(\xi))^{-1}\xi
.
In addition, there exists
C>0
such that for any
k\geq k_{0}
one has

\displaystyle\mu_{k}\left(\left\{f\in A_{k}\colon\left|\left(k^{-1}\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right],\psi\right)-\frac{\operatorname{mv}(|\chi|^{2})}{2\pi}\int_{X}d\alpha_{P}\wedge\psi\right|\geq\frac{\|\psi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{1}(X,\Lambda^{2n-1}_{\mathbb{C}}T^{*}X)}}{\sqrt{k}}\right\}\right)\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{k}}

for all
\psi\in\Omega^{2n-1}(X)
.

Proof.

From Theorem
4.21
we obtain
\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right]=d\mathcal{C}_{f}
in the sense of distributions for any
f\in H^{0}_{b}(X)\cap C^{\infty}(X)
such that zero is a regular value of the map
f\colon X\to\mathbb{C}
. Then the first part of the claim follows from Theorem
5.5
. Fix
\psi\in\Omega^{2n-1}(X)
. Using Lemma
5.2
and integration by parts, we obtain from Theorem
5.14
with
\kappa(k)\equiv 0
that for
\mu_{\infty}
-almost every
f=(f_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in A_{\infty}
we have that

\lim_{k\to\infty}\left(k^{-1}\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f_{k}\right)\right],\psi\right)=\lim_{k\to\infty}k^{-1}\mathcal{C}_{f_{k}}(d\psi)=\frac{\operatorname{mv}(|\chi|^{2})}{2\pi}\int_{X}\alpha_{P}\wedge d\psi=\frac{\operatorname{mv}(|\chi|^{2})}{2\pi}\int_{X}d\alpha_{P}\wedge\psi.

Furthermore, replacing
\psi
by
d\psi
in (
5.2.20
) leads to the last part of the claim.
∎

6.
Some Remarks on Complex Manifolds with Boundary

In this section we will recall basic definitions and notations for complex manifolds with boundary and formulate Theorem
4.14
in this set-up.

Let
M
be a real smooth
N
-dimensional manifold with smooth boundary. We denote by
\text{int}(M)
the set of points in
M
which have an open neighborhood homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{N}
and put
bM:=M\setminus\text{int}(M)
to denote the boundary of
M
. We note that
\text{int}(M)
is a smooth
N
-dimensional manifold without boundary and that
bM
, in case
bM\neq\emptyset
, is a smooth
(N-1)
-dimensional smooth submanifold of
M
. We call
V
a complex domain with boundary if there exists a complex manifold
Y
(without boundary) and a domain
G\subset Y
with smooth boundary such that
V=\overline{G}
. Note that
V
is automatically a smooth manifold with boundary with a complex structure on
\text{int}(V)
.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
M
be a real smooth connected manifold with boundary. A complex structure on
M
is a complex structure on
\text{int}(M)
such that for any point
p\in bM
there exist an open neighborhood
U\subset M
around
p
and a diffeomorphism
F\colon U\to V
where
V
is a complex domain with boundary such that
F
restricted to
U\cap\text{int}(M)
is a holomorphic map.

A smooth connected manifold with smooth boundary together with a complex structure is called complex manifold with boundary of (complex) dimension
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}M:=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\text{int}(M)
. Furthermore, we denote by
\mathcal{O}^{\infty}(M):=\mathcal{O}(\text{int}(M))\cap C^{\infty}(M)
the space of holomorphic functions which are smooth up to the boundary.

Example 6.2
.

Put
M=\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\mid 1<|z|\leq 2\}
. Then
M
(together with differential and complex structure induced by
\mathbb{C}^{2}
) is a complex manifold with boundary. We have
\text{int}(M)=\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\mid 1<|z|<2\}
and
bM=\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\mid|z|=2\}
. Furthermore, with
Y=\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\mid|z|>1\}
and
G=\{z\in Y\mid|z|<2\}
we find that
M=\overline{G}
is a complex domain with boundary.

For further constructions it is necessary to give an infinitesimal interpretation of complex manifolds with boundary.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
M
be an
n
-dimensional complex manifold with boundary. There exists a uniquely defined smooth complex subbundle
T^{1,0}M\subset\mathbb{C}TM
of rank
n
such that
T^{1,0}M|_{\operatorname{int}(M)}
coincides with the complex structure on
\operatorname{int}(M)
. Furthermore,
T^{1,0}M
is formally integrable and
T^{1,0}M\oplus\overline{T^{1,0}M}=\mathbb{C}TM
.

Proof.

Given
p\in\text{int}(M)
we choose
T^{1,0}_{p}M
in accordance with the complex structure on
\text{int}(M)
, that is,
T^{1,0}_{p}M=T^{1,0}_{p}\text{int}(M)
. Given
p\in bM
put

T_{p}^{1,0}M:=\{W_{p}\mid W\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{C}TM),\,W|_{\text{int}(M)}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\text{int}(M),T^{1,0}\text{int}(M))\}.

We find an open neighborhood
U\subset M
around
p
and a diffeomorphism
F\colon U\to V
where
V
is a complex domain with boundary such that
F
restricted to
U\cap\text{int}(M)
is a holomorphic map. Furthermore, we can write
V=\overline{G}
for some smoothly bounded domain
G
of some complex manifold
Y
. Since
F
is a diffeomorphism and holomorphic on
U\cap\text{int}(M)
we observe by continuity reasons
T^{1,0}_{p}M=(dF_{p})^{-1}T^{1,0}_{F(p)}Y
. Hence
T^{1,0}_{p}M
is a complex vector space of dimension
n
and
T^{1,0}M
is a smooth subbundle. With
\overline{T^{1,0}_{p}M}=(dF_{p})^{-1}\overline{T^{1,0}_{F(p)}Y}
and
\mathbb{C}TY_{F(p)}=T^{1,0}Y\oplus\overline{T^{1,0}Y}
we find
\mathbb{C}TX=T^{1,0}_{p}M\oplus\overline{T^{1,0}_{p}M}
. Since
T^{1,0}Y
is formally integrable it follows that
T^{1,0}M
is formally integrable. Moreover, by construction we have that
T^{1,0}M
is contained in any smooth complex subbundle which coincides with
T^{1,0}\text{int}(M)
on
\text{int}(M)
. Since
\text{rank}T^{1,0}M=n
the uniqueness follows.
∎

Put
T^{0,1}M:=\overline{T^{1,0}M}
. The decomposition
\mathbb{C}TM=T^{1,0}M\oplus T^{0,1}M
immediately leads to the complex vector bundles
\Lambda^{\bullet,\bullet}\mathbb{C}T^{*}M
over
M
defined in the usual way. For any
p,q\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
we denote by

\Omega^{p,q}(M):=\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(M,\Lambda^{p,q}\mathbb{C}T^{*}M)

the space of smooth
(p,q)
-forms on
M
and let
\Omega_{c}^{p,q}(M)\subset\Omega^{p,q}(M)
be the space of those forms with compact support. The operators
\partial,\overline{\partial}
, well understood for
(p,q)
forms on
\text{int}(M)
, uniquely extend to operators

\partial\colon\Omega^{p,q}(M)\to\Omega^{p+1,q}(M),\,\,\,\overline{\partial}\colon\Omega^{p,q}(M)\to\Omega^{p,q+1}(M).

One can easily check that those construction coincides with the definitions in Section
4
when
M
is a complex domain with boundary.
By definition
M
is locally isomorphic to complex domains with boundary. Then, the notion of
f\in\mathcal{O}^{\infty}(M)
being regular with respect to
bM
(see Definition
4.1
) as in Section
4
can be extended for complex manifold with boundary. We immediately observe the following.

Lemma 6.4
.

Lemma
4.4
and Corollary
4.5
hold when
G
and
\overline{G}
are replaced by
\text{int}(M)
and
M
respectively.

In conclusion we obtain the following.

Theorem 6.5
.

Let
M
be an
(n+1)
-dimensional complex manifold with boundary,
f\in\mathcal{O}^{\infty}(M)
regular with respect to
bM
and
\psi\in\Omega^{n,n}_{c}(M)
. We have that

\text{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,}\int_{M}\partial\overline{\partial}\log(|f|)\wedge\psi:=-\int_{bM}\iota^{*}(\frac{1}{2f}\partial f\wedge\psi)-\int_{bM}\iota^{*}(\log(|f|)\wedge\overline{\partial}\psi)+\int_{M}\log(|f|)\wedge\partial\overline{\partial}\psi

and
\left(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right],\psi\right)
are well defined with

(6.0.1)

\displaystyle\left(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right],\psi\right)=\frac{i}{\pi}\int_{M}\partial\overline{\partial}\log(|f|)\wedge\psi.

Here,
\iota\colon bM\to M
denotes the inclusion map.

Proof.

It is enough to show that any point
p\in M
has an open neighborhood
U\subset M
such that the statement holds for
\psi\in\Omega^{n,n}_{c}(U)
. Given
p\in\text{int}(M)
we can choose
U=\text{int}(M)
and the claim follows. Given
p\in bM
we find an open neighborhood
U\subset M
around
p
and a diffeomorphism
F\colon U\to V
where
V
is a complex domain with boundary such that
F
restricted to
U\cap\text{int}(M)
is a holomorphic map. Then the claim follows from Theorem
4.14
.
∎

The following lemma shows that
bM
naturally carries the structure of a CR manifold.

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
M
be an
(n+1)
-dimensional complex manifold with boundary such that
bM\neq\emptyset
and
n\geq 1
. Then
T^{1,0}bM:=\mathbb{C}TbM\cap T^{1,0}M
defines a (codimension one) CR structure on
bM
such that the restriction
f|_{bM}
of any
f\in\mathcal{O}^{\infty}(M)
is a smooth CR function.

Proof.

We first observe that
bM
is an orientable smooth manifold of real dimension
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}bM=2n+1
. Basic arguments from linear algebra show that
\text{rank}_{\mathbb{C}}T^{1,0}bM=n
and
T^{1,0}bM\cap T^{0,1}bM=\text{'zero section'}
. Since
TbM
and
T^{1,0}M
are formally integrable it follows that
T^{1,0}bM
defines a (codimension one) CR structure on
bM
. Given
f\in\mathcal{O}^{\infty}(M)
we have that
f|_{bM}
is smooth and we obtain for continuity reasons that
\overline{Z}f=0
for all
Z\in T^{1,0}M
. Hence
\overline{Z}(f|_{bM})=0
for all
Z\in T^{1,0}bM
which proves that
f|_{bM}
is a CR function.
∎

Definition 6.7
.

Let
M
be an
(n+1)
-dimensional complex manifold with boundary. We say that
p\in bM
is a strictly pseudoconvex boundary point if there exists a diffeomorphism
F\colon U\to V
where
U
is an open neighborhood around
p
and
V
is complex domain with boundary such that
F
is holomorphic on
U\cap\text{int}(M)
and
F(p)
is a strictly pseudoconvex boundary point of
V
. We call
M
a complex manifold with strictly pseudoconvex boundary if any point
p\in bM
is a strictly pseudoconvex boundary point.

We observe that if
M
is a complex manifold with strictly pseudoconvex boundary it follows that
(bM,T^{1,0}bM)
is a strictly pseudoconvex CR manifold.
There is the following relation between complex manifolds with strictly pseudoconvex boundary and complex domains with boundary due to Heunemann
[
27
]
and Ohsawa
[
44
]
, see also Catlin
[
12
]
, and Hill-Nacinovich
[
28
]
.

Theorem 6.8

(cf.
[
41
, Theorem 6.3.15]
)
.

Let
M
be a compact complex manifold
with strictly pseudoconvex boundary. Then
M
can be realized as a domain with
boundary in a larger complex manifold
Y
. More preciesely, there exists a strictly pseudoconvex domain with boundary
V\subset Y
and a diffeomorphism
F\colon M\to V
which is a biholomorphism
between
\operatorname{int}(M)
and
\operatorname{int}(V)
,
F(bM)=bV
.

Definition 6.9
.

Let
M
be an
(n+1)
-dimensional complex manifold with boundary. We say that
M
has the CR extension property if for any smooth CR function
f
on
bM
there exists a function
F\in\mathcal{O}^{\infty}(M)
with
F|_{bM}=f
.

Let us give some examples for complex manifolds with boundary satisfying the CR extension property.

Example 6.10
.

Let
M
be a complex domain with boundary. As already mentioned, it follows that
M
is a complex manifold with boundary. Assume
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}M>1
and that each point of
p\in bM
is a strictly pseudoconvex boundary point. If
bM
is compact we have that
bM
is CR embedabble into the complex Euclidean space (see
[
31
]
). If
M
is compact it follows from results due to Grauert
[
19
]
and Kohn–Rossi
[
37
]
that
bM
is connected and that
M
has the CR extension property.

Example 6.11
.

Let
(L,h)\to N
be a positive holomorphic line bundle with Hermitian metric
h
over a compact complex manifold
N
(without boundary). Put
M=\{v\in L^{*}\mid|v|_{h^{*}}\leq 1\}
where
L^{*}
denotes the dual line bundle of
L
and
h^{*}
the Hermitian metric on
L^{*}
induced by
h
. Then
M
is a compact complex manifold with strictly pseudoconvex boundary which has the CR extension property.

Example 6.12
.

Let
(N,g)
be a compact (without boundary) connected real analytic Riemannian manifold with real analytic metric
g
. Assume
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}N\geq 2
. For any sufficiently small
r>0
we have that
M:=\{v\in TN\mid|v|_{g}\leq r\}
is a compact complex manifold with strictly pseudoconvex boundary with respect to the adapted complex structure induced by
g
(see Grauert
[
19
]
and Guillemin–Stenzel
[
22
]
). Furthermore,
bM
is CR embeddable into the complex Euclidean space and
M
has the CR extension property by Example
6.10
.

Example 6.13
.

Let
(X,T^{1,0}X)
be a compact strictly pseudoconvex CR manifold which is CR embeddable into the complex Euclidean space. There exists
r>0
such that
M:=X\times[0,r)
carries the structure of a complex manifold with strictly pseudoconvex boundary such that
(X,T^{1,0}X)
is CR isomorphic to
(bM,T^{1,0}bM)
(see Harvey–Lawson
[
23
]
). Furthermore, choosing
r>0
small enough, it follows that
M
has the CR extension property.

7.
Equidistribution on Complex Manifolds with Strictly Pseudoconvex Boundary

In this section we are going to prove Theorem
1.10
, Theorem
1.11
and Theorem
1.12
(see Theorem
7.9
, Theorem
7.3
and Theorem
7.11
, respectively). Let
M
be an
(n+1)
-dimensional complex manifold with strictly pseudoconvex boundary,
n\geq 1
, such that
M
has the CR extension property (see Section
6
). Put
(X,T^{1,0}X):=(bM,T^{1,0}bM)
and assume that
X
is non-empty, compact and CR embeddable. Then
X
(seen as the boundary of
M
) is a compact oriented strictly pseudoconvex CR manifold which satisfies the assumptions in Theorem
1.1
. We denote by
\iota\colon bM\to M
the inclusion map. Choose a volume form
dV
on
X
. Denote by
\xi
a contact form on
X
such that the respective Levi form is positive definite and let
\mathcal{T}
be the respective Reeb vector field uniquely determined by
\iota_{\mathcal{T}}\xi\equiv 1
and
\iota_{\mathcal{T}}d\xi\equiv 0
. Let
P\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{cl}}(X)
be a first order formally self-adjoint classical pseudodifferential operator with
\sigma_{P}(\xi)>0
where
\sigma_{P}
denotes the principal symbol of
P
. Denote by
T_{P}=\Pi P\Pi
the corresponding Toeplitz operator.
Let

0<\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}\leq\ldots
denote the the positive eigenvalues of
T_{P}
and denote by
\{f_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subset H_{b}^{0}(X)\cap\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(X)
an orthonormal system of eigenfunctions.
Given a function
\eta\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}\left(\mathbb{R}_{+},[0,\infty)\right)

we have that the integral kernel of
\eta_{k}(T_{P})
can be written as
\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,y)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\eta_{k}(\lambda_{j})f_{j}(x)\overline{f_{j}(y)}
where
\eta_{k}(t)=\eta(k^{-1}t)
,
t,k>0
. From the CR extension property assumption on
M
any smooth CR function
f
on
X
extends holomorphically to
\text{int}(M)
. In particular, since
M
is connected, there exists a uniquely determined function
\mathcal{F}\in\mathcal{O}^{\infty}(M):=\mathcal{O}(\text{int}(M))\cap\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(M)
such that
f=\mathcal{F}
on
X
. Hence we have a linear mapping

(7.0.1)

\displaystyle L\colon H_{b}^{0}(X)\cap\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(X)\to\mathcal{O}^{\infty}(M),\,\,\,\,L(f)=\mathcal{F}.

We define

(7.0.2)

\displaystyle B^{\eta}_{k}\colon M\to\mathbb{R},\,\,\,\,\,B^{\eta}_{k}(z)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\eta_{k}(\lambda_{j})L(f_{j})(z)\overline{L(f_{j})(z)}.

Then
B^{\eta}_{k}
is smooth and for any
c>0
we have that
k^{-1}\partial\overline{\partial}\log(c+B^{\eta}_{k})
defines a non negative
(1,1)
-form on
\text{int}(M)
which is smooth up to the boundary.
Let
\chi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
,
\chi\not\equiv 0
, be a smooth function with
\text{supp}(\chi)\subset(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})
for some
0<\delta_{1}<\delta_{2}
. Put
N_{k}=\#\{j\in\mathbb{N}\colon\lambda_{j}\leq\delta_{2}k\}
. For
k>0
put
A_{k}=\text{span}\left(\{1\}\cup\{L(f_{j})\mid\lambda_{j}\leq\delta_{2}k\}\right)
. On
A_{k}
we consider the probability measure
\mu_{k}
induced by the standard complex Gaussian measure
\mu^{G}
on
\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}
and the map

\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}\ni a\mapsto a_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}a_{j}\chi_{k}(\lambda_{j})L(f_{j})\in A_{k}

where
\chi_{k}(t)=\chi(k^{-1}t)
. We use the same notation
(A_{k},\mu_{k})
for the probability spaces as in Section
5
since the constructions coincides. In particular, compared to Section
5
, we choose
\kappa(k)\equiv 1
in this section.

7.1.
Proof of Theorem
1.11

To simplify the notations in the proofs we consider the following object.

Definition 7.1
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{O}^{\infty}(M)
such that
\partial f_{p}\neq 0
for all
p\in bM
with
f(p)=0
. We define

\mathcal{Z}_{f}\colon\Omega_{c}^{n,n}(M)\to\mathbb{C},\,\,\,\mathcal{Z}_{f}(\psi)=\left(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right],\psi\right).

where
\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right]
denotes the zero divisor of
f
.

We note that under the assumptions on
f
in Definition
7.1
we have that
\mathcal{Z}_{f}
is well defined by Lemma
4.13
(cf. Section
6.4
). Furthermore, using Theorem
6.5
,
Z_{f}(\psi)
can be computed via the formula

(7.1.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{f}(\psi)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\left(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right],\psi\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{\pi}\left(-\int_{bM}\iota^{*}(\frac{1}{2f}\partial f\wedge\psi)-\int_{bM}\iota^{*}(\log(|f|)\wedge\overline{\partial}\psi)+\int_{M}\log(|f|)\wedge\partial\overline{\partial}\psi\right)

for all
\psi\in\Omega_{c}^{n,n}(M)
.
Since we wish to understand
\mathcal{Z}_{f}
as a random variable on
A_{k}
, we have to make sure that it is defined on a set
\tilde{A}_{k}\subset A_{k}
with
\mu_{k}(\tilde{A_{k}})=1
.
We have the following.

Lemma 7.2
.

Put
\tilde{A}_{k}:=\{f\in A_{k}\mid\text{0 is a regular value of }f|_{bM}\colon bM\to\mathbb{C}\}
. We have that
\tilde{A}_{k}
is
\mu_{k}
-measurable with
\mu_{k}(A_{k}\setminus\tilde{A}_{k})=0
.

Proof.

We have
1+\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}|\chi_{k}(\lambda_{j})|^{2}|f_{j}(x)|^{2}\geq 1>0
for all
k>0
and all
x\in bD
. Then the claim follows from Lemma
5.3
.
∎

We note that given
f\in\tilde{A}_{k}
with
\tilde{A}_{k}
as in Lemma
7.2
it follows that
\partial f_{p}\neq 0
for all
p\in bM
with
f(p)=0
. Hence
\mathcal{Z}_{f}
is well defined and we can apply (
7.1.1
) for its computation.
Theorem
1.10
follows from the next result by taking
c=1
and assuming that
M
is compact (see Example
6.10
).

Theorem 7.3
.

Let
M
be an
(n+1)
-dimensional complex manifold with strictly pseudoconvex boundary,
n\geq 1
, such that
M
has the CR extension property (see Section
6
). Put
X:=bM
and assume that
X
is non-empty, compact and CR embeddable. Let
\xi
be a contact form for
X
such that its induced Levi form is positive definite. Choose a volume form
dV
on
X
and consider a Toeplitz operator
T_{P}=\Pi P\Pi
on
X
as in the set-up of Theorem
1.1
. With the notations above we have
for each
k>0
that
\mathcal{Z}_{f}
is a well-defined distribution for all
f
outside a subset in
A_{k}
of
\mu_{k}
-measure zero and the expectation value
\mathbb{E}_{k}\left(\mathcal{Z}_{f}\right)
defined by

\left(\mathbb{E}_{k}\left(\mathcal{Z}_{f}\right),\psi\right):=\int_{A_{k}}\mathcal{Z}_{f}(\psi)d\mu_{k}(f),\,\,\,\,\,\psi\in\Omega^{n,n}_{c}(M)

exists
with

\mathbb{E}_{k}\left(\mathcal{Z}_{f}\right)=\frac{i}{2\pi}\partial\overline{\partial}\log(1+B^{|\chi|^{2}}_{k}).

Here,
B^{|\chi|^{2}}_{k}
is given by (
7.0.2
) where
\chi
appears in the definition of
A_{k}
.

Similar to Section
5
we introduce the following notation.
For
d\in\mathbb{N}
we denote by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
the standard inner product on
\mathbb{C}^{d+1}
that is

\langle a,b\rangle=\sum_{j=0}^{d}a_{j}\overline{b_{j}}

for
a=(a_{0},\ldots,a_{d}),b=(b_{0},\ldots,b_{d})\in\mathbb{C}^{d+1}
. Furthermore, given
a\in\mathbb{C}^{d+1}
we put
|a|=\sqrt{\langle a,a\rangle}
. For
k>0
consider the map

(7.1.2)

\displaystyle F_{k}\colon M\to\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1},\,\,\,\,F_{k}=(1,\chi_{k}(\lambda_{1})L(f_{1}),\ldots,\chi_{k}(\lambda_{N_{k}})L(f_{N_{k}})).

Define
S_{k}(z,w)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}|\chi_{k}(\lambda_{j})|^{2}L(f_{j})(z)\overline{L(f_{j})(w)}
,
z,w\in M
and
put
B_{k}:=B_{k}^{|\chi|^{2}}
. It turns out that
|F_{k}|^{2}=1+B_{k}
,
S_{k}(z,z)=B_{k}(z)
and
\langle F_{k}(w),F_{k}(w)\rangle=1+S_{k}(z,w)
for all
z,w\in M
and
k>0
. With
\eta_{k}=|\chi_{k}|^{2}
we find
S_{k}(z,w):=\eta_{k}(T_{P})(z,w)=\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}\eta_{k}(\lambda_{j})f_{j}(z)\overline{f_{j}(w)}
for all
z,w\in bM
and
k>0
. We further consider
dF_{k}
component wise, that is,

dF_{k}=(0,\chi_{k}(\lambda_{1})df_{1},\ldots,\chi_{k}(\lambda_{N_{k}})df_{N_{k}}).

Then
\langle a,\overline{dF_{k}}\rangle
is a one form on
M
for any
a\in\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}
. We have for example
\langle F_{k}(z),(dF_{k})_{w}\rangle=d_{w}S_{k}(z,w)
and for
f=\langle a,\overline{F_{k}}\rangle
we obtain
df=\langle a,\overline{dF_{k}}\rangle
.

We will prove Theorem
7.3
using the decomposition of
\mathcal{Z}_{f}
given by (
7.1.1
) and consider each term in the sum separately. The first term in the right-hand side of (
7.1.1
) can be handled using the following lemma which is a direct consequence of the results in Section
5
.

Lemma 7.4
.

With the notations and assumptions above given
k>0
and
\psi\in\Omega_{c}^{n,n}(M)
we have that

I_{k}(\psi):=\int_{A_{k}}\int_{bM}\iota^{*}\left(\frac{\partial f}{f}\wedge\psi\right)d\mu_{k}(f)

exists with

I_{k}(\psi)=\int_{bM}\iota^{*}\left((\partial\log(1+B_{k}))\wedge\psi\right).

Proof.

Fix
k>0
and
\psi\in\Omega_{c}^{n,n}(M)
. We note that
|F_{k}|^{2}\geq 1>0
. Given
f\in\mathcal{O}^{\infty}(M)
we have
\iota^{*}(\partial f)=\iota^{*}(df)=d(f\circ\iota)
with
f\circ\iota\in H_{b}^{0}(X)
,
X=bM
. Then it follows from Lemma
7.2
and Lemma
6.4
that

\int_{bM}\iota^{*}\left(\frac{\partial f}{f}\wedge\psi\right)=\int_{bM}\iota^{*}\left(\frac{\partial f}{f}\right)\wedge\iota^{*}\psi=\int_{bM}\frac{d(f\circ\iota)}{f\circ\iota}\wedge\iota^{*}\psi

holds for
\mu_{k}
-almost every
f\in A_{k}
. Since
\iota^{*}\psi\in\Omega^{2n}(X)
and
|F_{k}|^{2}>0
we conclude from Theorem
5.5
that
I_{k}(\psi)
exists with

I_{k}(\psi)=\int_{bM}2\pi i\beta_{k}\wedge\iota^{*}\psi

where

2\pi i\beta_{k}=\frac{\langle d(F_{k}\circ\iota),F_{k}\circ\iota\rangle}{|F_{k}\circ\iota|^{2}}.

Since
d(F_{k}\circ\iota)=\iota^{*}dF_{k}=\iota^{*}\partial F_{k}
and
\langle\partial F_{k},F_{k}\rangle=\partial|F_{k}|^{2}=\partial B_{k}
we obtain

2\pi i\beta_{k}=\iota^{*}\left(\frac{\langle\partial F_{k},F_{k}\rangle}{|F_{k}|^{2}}\right)=\iota^{*}\left(\frac{\partial B_{k}}{1+B_{k}}\right)=\iota^{*}\left(\partial\log(1+B_{k})\right).

The claim follows.
∎

In order to handle the log-terms in (
7.1.1
) we need the following.

Lemma 7.5
.

With the notations and assumptions above we have that for each
k>0
and any compact set
K\subset M
there exists a constant
C_{k,K}>0
such that

(7.1.3)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}}\left|\log(|\langle a,\overline{F_{k}(z)}\rangle|)\right|d\mu^{G}(a)\leq C_{k,K}

holds for all
z\in K
.
Furthermore, we have that

\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}}\log|\langle a,\overline{F_{k}(z)}\rangle|d\mu^{G}(a)-\frac{1}{2}\log(1+B_{k}(z))=\int_{\mathbb{C}}\log|a_{0}|d\mu^{G}(a_{0})

for all
k>0
,
z\in M
.

Proof.

We have that
c:=\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}}|\log|a_{0}||d\mu^{G}(a)<\infty
is independent of
k>0
and
z\in\overline{D}
. Since
F_{k}(z)/|F_{k}(z)|
has unit length we obtain from the unitary invariance of
d\mu^{G}
that

\displaystyle c

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}}\left|\log\left(\left\langle a,\frac{\overline{F_{k}(z)}}{|F_{k}(z)|}\right\rangle\right)\right|d\mu^{G}(a)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}}\left|\log(\langle a,\overline{F_{k}(z)}\rangle)\right|d\mu^{G}(a)-|\log(|F_{k}(z)|)|.

Since
|F_{k}(z)|^{2}=1+B_{k}(z)\geq 1
we obtain (
7.1.3
). Similarly,
we find

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}}\log(\langle a,\overline{F_{k}(z)}\rangle)\ d\mu^{G}(a)-\log(|F_{k}(z)|)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}}\log\left(\left\langle a,\frac{\overline{F_{k}(z)}}{|F_{k}(z)|}\right\rangle\right)d\mu^{G}(a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{C}}\log|a_{0}|d\mu^{G}(a_{0}).

With
2\log(|F_{k}(z)|)=\log(1+B_{k}(z))
the claim follows.
∎

Now we can consider the last two terms in the right-hand side of (
7.1.1
). We have the following.

Lemma 7.6
.

With the notations and assumptions given
k>0
and
\psi\in\Omega_{c}^{n,n}(M)
we have that

II_{k}(\psi):=\int_{A_{k}}\left(-\int_{bM}\iota^{*}(\log(|f|)\wedge\overline{\partial}\psi)+\int_{M}\log(|f|)\wedge\partial\overline{\partial}\psi\right)d\mu_{k}(f)

exists with

II_{k}(\psi)=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}(\partial\log(1+B_{k}))\wedge\overline{\partial}\psi.

Proof.

Fix
k>0
and
\psi\in\Omega_{c}^{n,n}(M)
. From (
7.1.3
) it follows by the Fubini-Tonelli theorem that
II_{k}(\psi)
exists.
Put
c:=\int_{\mathbb{C}}\log|a_{0}|d\mu^{G}(a_{0})=\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}}\log|a_{0}|d\mu^{G}(a)
.
From Lemma
7.5
, the Fubini theorem and the definition of
\mu_{k}
we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{M}\left(\frac{1}{2}\log(1+B_{k})+c\right)\partial\overline{\partial}\psi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}}\int_{M}\log|\langle a,F_{k}\rangle|\partial\overline{\partial}\psi d\mu^{G}(a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{A_{k}}\int_{M}\log(|f|)\wedge\partial\overline{\partial}\psi d\mu_{k}(f).

With the same arguments we find

\displaystyle\int_{bM}\iota^{*}\left(\left(\frac{1}{2}\log(1+B_{k})+c\right)\overline{\partial}\psi\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}}\int_{bM}\iota^{*}\left(\log|\langle a,F_{k}\rangle|\overline{\partial}\psi\right)d\mu^{G}(a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{A_{k}}\int_{bM}\iota^{*}\left(\log(|f|)\overline{\partial}\psi\right)d\mu_{k}(f).

Using Stokes theorem we obtain
\int_{bM}\iota^{*}(\overline{\partial}\psi)=\int_{M}\partial\overline{\partial}\psi
and

\displaystyle\int_{bM}\iota^{*}\left(\log(1+B_{k})\overline{\partial}\psi\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{M}\left(d\log(1+B_{k})\right)\wedge\overline{\partial}\psi+\int_{M}\log(1+B_{k})\wedge d\overline{\partial}\psi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{M}\left(\partial\log(1+B_{k})\right)\wedge\overline{\partial}\psi+\int_{M}\log(1+B_{k})\wedge\partial\overline{\partial}\psi.

We conclude

\displaystyle 2II_{k}(\psi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{bM}\iota^{*}\left(\log(1+B_{k})\overline{\partial}\psi\right)+\int_{M}\log(1+B_{k})\wedge\partial\overline{\partial}\psi+2c\left(-\int_{bM}\iota^{*}(\overline{\partial}\psi)+\int_{M}\partial\overline{\partial}\psi\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{M}\left(\partial\log(1+B_{k})\right)\wedge\overline{\partial}\psi.

∎

Using (
7.1.1
) we obtain Theorem
7.3
as a consequence of Lemma
7.4
and Lemma
7.6
.

Proof of Theorem
7.3
.

Fix
k>0
and
\psi\in\Omega_{c}^{n,n}(M)
. From Lemma
7.4
and Lemma
7.6
it follows that

III_{k}(\psi):=\frac{i}{\pi}\int_{A_{k}}\left(-\int_{bM}\iota^{*}(\frac{1}{2f}\partial f\wedge\psi)-\int_{bM}\iota^{*}(\log(|f|)\wedge\overline{\partial}\psi)+\int_{M}\log(|f|)\wedge\partial\overline{\partial}\psi\right)d\mu_{k}(f)

exists with
III_{k}(\psi)<\infty
. Using Lemma
7.2
we find a set
\tilde{A}_{k}\subset A_{k}
with
\mu_{k}(\tilde{A_{k}})=1
such that Theorem
6.5
can be applied for all
f\in\tilde{A}_{k}
. We find that

\displaystyle III_{k}(\psi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{\pi}\int_{\tilde{A_{k}}}\left(-\int_{bM}\iota^{*}(\frac{1}{2f}\partial f\wedge\psi)-\int_{bM}\iota^{*}(\log(|f|)\wedge\overline{\partial}\psi)+\int_{M}\log(|f|)\wedge\partial\overline{\partial}\psi\right)d\mu_{k}(f)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\tilde{A_{k}}}\left(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right],\psi\right)d\mu_{k}(f)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{A_{k}}\left(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right],\psi\right)d\mu_{k}(f)=\mathbb{E}_{k}(\mathcal{Z}_{f})(\psi).

Hence
\mathbb{E}_{k}(\mathcal{Z}_{f})(\psi)<\infty
exists.
From Stokes theorem we find

\int_{bM}\iota^{*}\left(\partial\log(1+B_{k})\wedge\psi\right)=-\int_{M}\left(\partial\overline{\partial}\log(1+B_{k})\right)\wedge\psi-\int_{M}\left(\partial\log(1+B_{k})\right)\wedge\overline{\partial}\psi.

Since
III_{k}(\psi)=\pi^{-1}i(-I_{k}(\psi)/2+II_{k}(\psi))
we conclude from Lemma
7.4
and Lemma
7.6
that

\displaystyle III_{k}(\psi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{2\pi}\left(-\int_{bM}\iota^{*}((\partial\log(1+B_{k}))\wedge\psi)-\int_{M}(\partial\log(1+B_{k}))\wedge\overline{\partial}\psi\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{2\pi}\int_{M}\left(\partial\overline{\partial}\log(1+B_{k})\right)\wedge\psi.

∎

7.2.
A Variance Estimate for
\mathcal{Z}_{f}

The proof of Theorem
1.12
will mainly rely on a variance Estimate for the distribution valued random variable
\mathcal{Z}_{f}
which we are going to explain now. We start with an elementary lemma.

Lemma 7.7
.

For all
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{C}
with
|\alpha|^{2}+|\beta|^{2}=1
we have

\int_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}|\log(|a_{0}|)\log(|\alpha a_{0}+\beta a_{1}|)|d\mu^{G}(a)\leq\int_{\mathbb{C}}|\log(|a|)|^{2}d\mu^{G}(a).

Proof.

Put
c:=\int_{\mathbb{C}}|\log(|a|)|^{2}d\mu^{G}(a)
by the unitary invariance of
\mu^{G}
we obtain

\int_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}|\log(|\alpha a_{0}+\beta a_{1}|)|^{2}d\mu^{G}(a)=c.

By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality we conclude

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}|\log(|a_{0}|)\log(|\alpha a_{0}+\beta a_{1}|)|d\mu^{G}(a)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{\int_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}|\log(|a_{0}|)|^{2}d\mu^{G}(a)}\sqrt{\int_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}|\log(|\alpha a_{0}+\beta a_{1}|)|^{2}d\mu^{G}(a)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c.

∎

We have the following result for the variance of
\mathcal{Z}_{f}
.

Theorem 7.8
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
7.3
, with the notations above put

\mathbb{V}_{k}(\mathcal{Z}_{f}(\psi)):=\int_{A_{k}}\left|\mathcal{Z}_{f}(\psi)-\mathbb{E}_{k}(\mathcal{Z}_{f})(\psi)\right|^{2}d\mu_{k}(f),\,\,\,\psi\in\Omega_{c}^{n,n}(M).

Given
0<\varepsilon<1
there exist
k_{0}>0
such that for any compact set
K\subset M
there is a constant
C_{K}>0
with

\mathbb{V}_{k}(\mathcal{Z}_{f}(\psi))\leq C_{K}k^{\varepsilon}\|\psi\|^{2}_{\mathscr{C}^{2}(M,\Lambda^{n,n}\mathbb{C}T^{*}M)}

for all
k\geq k_{0}
and all
\psi\in\Omega_{c}^{n,n}(M)
,
\supp\psi\subset K
.

Proof.

Let
0<\varepsilon<1
be arbitrary. For
k>0
and
\psi\in\Omega_{c}^{n,n}(M)
put

R_{k}(\psi):=\int_{A_{k}}\left|\frac{i}{\pi}\int_{M}\partial\overline{\partial}\log|f|\wedge\psi-\frac{i}{2\pi}\int_{M}\left(\partial\overline{\partial}\log(1+B_{k})\right)\wedge\psi\right|^{2}d\mu_{k}(f)

and

\displaystyle R^{(1)}_{k}(\psi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{A_{k}}\left|\int_{bM}\iota^{*}\left(\frac{\partial f}{f}-\partial\log(1+B_{k})\right)\wedge\iota^{*}\psi\right|^{2}d\mu_{k}(f),

\displaystyle R^{(2)}_{k}(\psi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{A_{k}}\left|\int_{bM}\iota^{*}\left(\log\left(\frac{|f|}{\sqrt{1+B_{k}}}\right)\overline{\partial}\psi\right)\right|^{2}d\mu_{k}(f),

\displaystyle R^{(3)}_{k}(\psi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{A_{k}}\left|\int_{M}\log\left(\frac{|f|}{\sqrt{1+B_{k}}}\right)\partial\overline{\partial}\psi\right|^{2}d\mu_{k}(f).

We have
\frac{i}{\pi}\int_{M}\partial\overline{\partial}\log|f|\wedge\psi=\left(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right],\psi\right)=:\mathcal{Z}_{f}(\psi)
for all
f
in a subset
\tilde{A}_{k}
of
A_{k}
with
\mu_{k}(\tilde{A}_{k})=1
by Lemma
7.2
. Then, using Theorem
7.3
, we find

(7.2.1)

\displaystyle R_{k}(\psi)=\mathbb{V}_{k}(Z_{f}(\psi)).

Furthermore, from the definition of
\int_{M}\partial\overline{\partial}\log|f|\wedge\psi
and Stokes theorem we find

(7.2.2)

\displaystyle R_{k}(\psi)\leq\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}R^{(1)}_{k}(\psi)+\frac{4}{\pi^{2}}\left(R^{(2)}_{k}(\psi)+R^{(3)}_{k}(\psi)\right).

Hence, it is enough to show that there is
k_{0}>0
and, given a compact set
K\subset M
, a constant
C_{K}>0
such that
R^{(j)}_{k}(\psi)\leq C_{K}k^{\varepsilon}\|\psi\|^{2}_{\mathscr{C}^{2}(M,\Lambda^{n,n}\mathbb{C}T^{*}M)}
for all
k\geq k_{0}
,
\psi\in\Omega^{n,n}_{c}(M)
with
\supp\psi\subset K
and
j=1,2,3
. Then the claim in Theorem
7.8
follows immediately.

We will estimate the term
R^{(1)}_{k}(\psi)
using Theorem
5.13
. With respect to Theorem
5.5
we have that

\beta_{k}(x)=\frac{d_{x}\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,y)|_{x=y}}{2\pi i(1+\eta_{k}(T_{P})(x,x))},\,\,\,x\in bM

defines a smooth one form on
bM
for all
k>0
where
\eta=|\chi|^{2}
.
Recall that

\eta_{k}(T_{P})(z,w)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}|\chi_{k}(\lambda_{j})|^{2}f_{j}(z)\overline{f_{j}(w)}=\langle F_{k}(z),F_{k}(w)\rangle-1,\,\,\,z,w\in bM.

The components of
F_{k}
are functions in
\mathcal{O}^{\infty}(M)
. Hence we obtain
\partial F_{k}=dF_{k}
and in addition we find with
|F_{k}|^{2}=1+B_{k}
that

2\pi i\beta_{k}=\frac{\iota^{*}\partial B_{k}}{1+B_{k}\circ\iota}=\iota^{*}\partial\log\left(1+B_{k}\right).

Using Theorem
5.5
we obtain in conclusion

R^{(1)}_{k}(\psi)=\int_{A_{k}}\left|\int_{bM}\left(\iota^{*}\left(\frac{df}{f}\right)-2\pi i\beta_{k}\right)\wedge\iota^{*}\psi\right|^{2}d\mu_{k}(f)=4\pi^{2}\mathbb{V}_{k}(\mathcal{C}_{f}(\iota^{*}\psi))

for all
k>0
and all
\psi\in\Omega^{n,n}_{c}(M)
, where
\mathbb{V}_{k}(\mathcal{C}_{f}(\iota^{*}\psi))
is as in Theorem
5.13
with
X=bM
and
\kappa(k)\equiv 1
. Then, by Theorem
5.13
, there exist
k_{0},C_{1}>0
such that

(7.2.3)

\displaystyle R_{k}^{(1)}(\psi)\leq C_{1}k^{\varepsilon}\|\psi\|^{2}_{\mathscr{C}^{0}(M,\Lambda^{n,n}\mathbb{C}T^{*}M)}

holds for all
k\geq k_{0}
and
\psi\in\Omega^{n,n}_{c}(M)
. It remains to estimate
R_{k}^{(2)}(\psi)
and
R_{k}^{(3)}(\psi)
.

Fix a compact set
K\subset M
. Recall that
|F_{k}|\geq 1>0
for all
k>0
. For
z,w\in M
we can choose a unitary transformation
U^{z,w}_{k}
of
\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}
such
U^{z,w}_{k}\frac{\overline{F_{k}(z)}}{|F_{k}(z)|}=(1,0,\ldots,0)
and
U^{z,w}_{k}\frac{\overline{F_{k}(w)}}{|F_{k}(w)|}=(\alpha,\beta,0\ldots,0)
with
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{C}
,
|\alpha|^{2}+|\beta|^{2}=1
.
Denote by
p_{1},p_{2}\colon M\times M\to M
the projections on the first and second component.
We write

R^{(3)}_{k}=\int_{A_{k}}\int_{M\times M}\log\left(\frac{|f\circ p_{1}|}{\sqrt{1+B_{k}\circ p_{1}}}\right)\log\left(\frac{|f\circ p_{2}|}{\sqrt{1+B_{k}\circ p_{2}}}\right)p_{1}^{*}(\partial\overline{\partial}\psi)\wedge\overline{p_{2}^{*}(\partial\overline{\partial}\psi)}d\mu_{k}(f).

Using
U^{z,w}_{k}
we find by the invariance of
\mu^{G}
under unitary transformation and Lemma
7.7
that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}}\left|\log\left|\left\langle a,\frac{\overline{F_{k}(z)}}{|F_{k}(z)|}\right\rangle\right|\log\left|\left\langle a,\frac{\overline{F_{k}(w)}}{|F_{k}(w)|}\right\rangle\right|\right|d\mu^{G}(a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}}|\log(|a_{0}|)\log(|\alpha a_{0}+\beta a_{1}|)|d\mu^{G}(a)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{C}}|\log(|a|)|^{2}d\mu^{G}(a).

With
|F_{k}|^{2}=1+B_{k}
,
f=\langle a,\overline{F_{k}}\rangle
for
a\in\mathbb{C}^{N_{k}+1}
, and
p_{1}(z,w)=z
,
p_{2}(z,w)=w
it follows from the Fubini-Tonelli theorem that there is a constant
C_{3,K}>0
such that
R_{k}^{(3)}(\psi)\leq C_{3}\|\psi\|^{2}_{\mathscr{C}^{2}(M,\Lambda^{n,n}\mathbb{C}T^{*}M)}
holds for all
k>0
and
\psi\in\Omega^{n,n}_{c}(M)
with
\supp\psi\subset K
. With the same arguments we obtain
R_{k}^{(2)}(\psi)\leq C_{2,K}\|\psi\|^{2}_{\mathscr{C}^{2}(M,\Lambda^{n,n}\mathbb{C}T^{*}M)}
for some constant
C_{2,K}>0
independent of
k
and
\psi
,
\supp\psi\subset K
. Combining those estimates for
R_{k}^{(2)}(\psi)
and
R_{k}^{(3)}(\psi)
with the estimate for
R_{k}^{(1)}(\psi)
in (
7.2.3
) the claim of the theorem follows from (
7.2.1
) and (
7.2.2
).
∎

7.3.
Proof of Theorem
1.10

Theorem
1.10
follows from the next result by putting
c=1
.

Theorem 7.9
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
7.3
and with the notation above, assuming that
M
is compact, choose
\eta\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+},[0,\infty))
,
\eta\not\equiv 0
, and
c>0
. With
B_{k}^{\eta}
as in (
7.0.2
), for all
\psi\in\Omega^{n,n}(M)
, we have

(7.3.1)

\lim_{k\to\infty}\int_{M}k^{-1}i\partial\overline{\partial}\log(c+B^{\eta}_{k})\wedge\psi=\operatorname{mv}(\eta)\int_{bM}\frac{\xi}{\sigma_{P}(\xi)}\wedge\iota^{*}\psi,

where

(7.3.2)

\displaystyle\operatorname{mv}(\eta)=\frac{\int_{0}^{+\infty}t^{n+1}\eta(t)dt}{\int_{0}^{+\infty}t^{n}\eta(t)dt}.

Here
\iota\colon bM\to M
denotes the inclusion map. More precisely, there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\left|\int_{M}k^{-1}i\partial\overline{\partial}\log(c+B^{\eta}_{k})\wedge\psi-\operatorname{mv}(\eta)\int_{bM}\frac{\xi}{\sigma_{P}(\xi)}\wedge\iota^{*}\psi\right|\leq Ck^{-1}(\log(k)+1)\|\psi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{2}(M,\Lambda^{n,n}\mathbb{C}T^{*}M)}

for all
k\geq 1
and
\psi\in\Omega^{n,n}(M)
.

For the proof of Theorem
7.9
we need the following lemma.

Lemma 7.10
.

In the situation of Theorem
7.9
there exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
k>0
and
z\in M
we have
|\log(c+B_{k}^{\eta}(z))|\leq C(\log(k)+1)
.

Proof.

Given
z\in bM
and
k>0
we have
B_{k}^{\eta}(z)=\eta_{k}(T_{P})(z,z)
. Furthermore, using Theorem
2.10
we obtain a constant
R>0
such that
0\leq\eta_{k}(T_{P})(z,z)\leq Rk^{n+1}
holds for all
z\in bM
and
k>0
.
Since
B^{\eta}_{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(M)
is plurisubharmonic on
\text{int}(M)
it follows from the maximum principle for plurisubharmonic functions that
0\leq B^{\chi}_{k}(z)\leq Rk^{n+1}
for all
z\in M
and and
k>0
. The statement follows from
\log(c+B^{\chi}_{k})\geq\log(c)
with
c>0
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
7.9
.

Recall the definition of
L
in (
7.0.1
). Given a smooth CR function
f
on
X=bM
we have
L(f)\in\mathcal{O}^{\infty}(M)
and hence
\partial(L(f))=d(L(f))
on
M
. It follows that

(\iota^{*}\overline{\partial}B^{\eta}_{k})(z)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\eta_{k}(\lambda_{j})f_{j}(z)d\overline{f_{j}}(z)=d_{w}\eta_{k}(T_{P})(z,w)|_{z=w}

for all
z\in bM
.
Using Lemma
2.11
and Theorem
2.10
we conclude

(7.3.3)

k^{-1}\iota^{*}\overline{\partial}\log(c+B^{\eta}_{k})=k^{-1}\iota^{*}\left(\frac{\overline{\partial}B^{\eta}_{k}}{c+B^{\eta}_{k}}\right)=-i\operatorname{mv}(\eta)\frac{\xi}{\sigma_{P}(\xi)}+O(k^{-1})

on
bM
in
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}
-norm. It follows that there exists a constant
C_{1}>0
such that

(7.3.4)

\left|\int_{bM}k^{-1}\iota^{*}\left(\frac{\overline{\partial}B^{\eta}_{k}}{c+B^{\eta}_{k}}\wedge\psi\right)+\int_{bM}i\operatorname{mv}(\eta)\frac{\xi}{\sigma_{P}(\xi)}\wedge\iota^{*}\psi\right|\leq C_{1}k^{-1}\|\psi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{0}(\overline{D},\Lambda^{n,n}T^{*}\mathbb{C}^{n+1})}

for all
\psi\in\Omega^{n,n}(M)
and
k>0
.
Let
\psi\in\Omega^{n,n}(M)
be arbitrary. By Stokes theorem we find that

\displaystyle\int_{M}\overline{\partial}\log(c+B^{\eta}_{k})\wedge d\psi=

\displaystyle\int_{M}\overline{\partial}\log(c+B^{\eta}_{k})\wedge\partial\psi=\int_{M}d\log(c+B^{\eta}_{k})\wedge\partial\psi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{bM}\log(c+B^{\eta}_{k}\circ\iota)\wedge\iota^{*}(\partial\psi)+\int_{M}\log(c+B^{\eta}_{k})\wedge\partial\overline{\partial}\psi.

Then it follows from Lemma
7.10
that there exists a constant
C_{2}>0
such that

(7.3.5)

\left|\int_{M}\overline{\partial}\log(c+B^{\eta}_{k})\wedge d\psi\right|\leq C_{2}(\log(k)+1)\|\psi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{2}(M,\Lambda^{n,n}\mathbb{C}T^{*}M)}

holds for all
\psi\in\Omega^{n,n}(M)
and
k>0
.
Using Stokes theorem again we find

(7.3.6)

\int_{M}\partial\overline{\partial}\log(c+B^{\eta}_{k})\wedge\psi=\int_{bM}\iota^{*}(\overline{\partial}\log(c+B^{\eta}_{k})\wedge\iota^{*}\psi+\int_{M}\overline{\partial}\log(c+B^{\eta}_{k})\wedge d\psi.

Finally, the claim follows from (
7.3.3
), (
7.3.5
) and (
7.3.6
).
∎

7.4.
Proof of Theorem
1.12

Consider
k\in\mathbb{N}
and put

A_{\infty}=\Pi_{k=1}^{\infty}A_{k}
,
d\mu_{\infty}=\Pi_{k=1}^{\infty}d\mu_{k}
. Theorem
1.12
follows from the next result.

Theorem 7.11
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
7.3
and with the notation above, assuming that
M
is compact, we have for
\mu_{\infty}
-almost every
u=(u_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in A_{\infty}
that
(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(u_{k}\right)\right],\psi)
is well-defined for all
\psi\in\Omega^{n,n}(M)
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Furthermore, given
\psi\in\Omega^{n,n}(M)
, we have

\lim_{k\to\infty}\left(k^{-1}\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(u_{k}\right)\right],\psi\right)=\frac{\operatorname{mv}(|\chi|^{2})}{2\pi}\int_{bD}\frac{\xi}{\sigma_{P}(\xi)}\wedge\iota^{*}\psi

for
\mu_{\infty}
-almost every
u=(u_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in A_{\infty}
.
In addition, there exist
C,k_{0}>0
such that for any
k\geq k_{0}
one has

\displaystyle\mu_{k}\left(\left\{f\in A_{k}\colon\left|\left(k^{-1}\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right],\psi\right)-\frac{\operatorname{mv}(|\chi|^{2})}{2\pi}\int_{bM}\frac{\xi}{\sigma_{P}(\xi)}\wedge\iota^{*}\psi\right|\geq\frac{\|\psi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{2}(M,\Lambda^{n,n}\mathbb{C}T^{*}M)}}{\sqrt{k}}\right\}\right)\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{k}}

for all
\psi\in\Omega^{n,n}(M)
.

Proof.

From Theorem
6.5
and Lemma
7.2
it follows that
(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(u_{k}\right)\right],\psi)
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, is well-defined for all
\psi\in\Omega^{n,n}(M)
and
\mu_{\infty}
-almost every
u=(u_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in A_{\infty}
which proves the first part of the claim. Given
\psi\in\Omega^{n,n}(M)
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
put

R_{k}(\psi):=\int_{A_{k}}\left|\left(k^{-1}\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right],\psi\right)-\frac{\operatorname{mv}(|\chi|^{2})}{2\pi}\int_{bM}\frac{\xi}{\sigma_{P}(\xi)}\wedge\iota^{*}\psi\right|^{2}d\mu_{k}(f).

We will show that there exist
k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
and a constant
C>0
such that
R_{k}(\psi)
exists for all
k\geq k_{0}
with
R_{k}(\psi)\leq Ck^{-\frac{3}{2}}\|\psi\|^{2}_{\mathscr{C}^{2}(M,\Lambda^{n,n}\mathbb{C}T^{*}M)}
for all
k\geq k_{0}
and
\psi\in\Omega^{n,n}(M)
. From the Markov inequality, the last part of the claim follows. Furthermore, considering
k\in\mathbb{N}
, it follows that
\sum_{k=k_{0}}^{\infty}R_{k}(\psi)\leq C_{0}\|\psi\|^{2}_{\mathscr{C}^{2}(M,\Lambda^{n,n}\mathbb{C}T^{*}M)}
for some constant
C_{0}
(independent of
\psi
) which leads to the second part of the claim.

Given
\psi\in\Omega^{n,n}(M)
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
put

R^{\prime}_{k}(\psi):=\left|\frac{i}{2\pi k}\int_{M}\left(\partial\overline{\partial}\log(1+B_{k})\right)\wedge\psi-\frac{\operatorname{mv}(|\chi|^{2})}{2\pi}\int_{bM}\frac{\xi}{\sigma_{P}(\xi)}\wedge\iota^{*}\psi\right|^{2}.

From Theorem
7.9
it follows that there exists a constant
C_{1}>0
such that

R^{\prime}_{k}(\psi)\leq C_{1}k^{-\frac{3}{2}}\|\psi\|^{2}_{\mathscr{C}^{2}(M,\Lambda^{n,n}\mathbb{C}T^{*}M)}

holds for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\psi\in\Omega^{n,n}(M)
.
Since
\mathcal{Z}_{f}(\psi)=\left(\left[\operatorname{Div}\!\left(f\right)\right],\psi\right)
we find with Theorem
7.3
and
\mathbb{V}_{k}
as in Theorem
7.8
that

R_{k}(\psi)\leq 2k^{-2}\mathbb{V}_{k}(\mathcal{Z}_{f}(\psi))+2R^{\prime}_{k}(\psi)\int_{A_{k}}d\mu_{k}.

Then it follows from Theorem
7.8
with
\varepsilon=\frac{1}{2}
that there are
k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
and a constant
C>0
such that
R_{k}(\psi)\leq Ck^{-\frac{3}{2}}\|\psi\|^{2}_{\mathscr{C}^{2}(M,\Lambda^{n,n}\mathbb{C}T^{*}M)}
for all
k\geq k_{0}
and
\psi\in\Omega^{n,n}(M)
.
∎
